abstract_pt: 'Os avanços tecnológicos permitiram que organizações em todo o mundo
  investissem na transformação digital, uma tendência acelerada pela pandemia causada
  pelo COVID-19. Paralelamente, a crescente predominância das Gerações Y (Millennials)
  e Z na força de trabalho global exige uma compreensão mais profunda das suas perceções
  em ambientes de trabalho digitais. No entanto, uma falha nos estudos atuais sobre
  ambientes de trabalho digitais é não abordar as diversas necessidades e valores
  de uma força de trabalho multigeracional. Esta falta de conhecimento pode contribuir
  para o não envolvimento dos trabalhadores, sendo que apenas 23% da força de trabalho
  global se sentia envolvida em 2022, enquanto 59% não estavam ativamente envolvidos,
  também chamados de "quiet quitting," e 18% estavam ativamente não envolvidos.Esta
  tese de doutoramento compreende cinco estudos que investigam como as organizações
  podem implementar ambientes de trabalho digitais envolventes, adaptados a uma força
  de trabalho multigeracional. O nosso principal objetivo é desenvolver um framework
  e um método para apoiar as organizações durante este processo. Começámos com uma
  Revisão Sistemática da Literatura (RSL) sobre ambientes de trabalho digitais e o
  envolvimento dos trabalhadores, seguida de uma segunda RSL sobre características
  geracionais para investigar as necessidades, valores e comportamentos dos colaboradores,
  particularmente das Gerações X, Y e Z, e como percebem a experiência num ambiente
  de trabalho digital. As conclusões enfatizaram a necessidade de uma visão holística
  do ambiente de trabalho digital, considerando que reconhecemos 75 fatores que podem
  influenciar positiva ou negativamente o envolvimento dos trabalhadores. Além disso,
  elaboramos um processo detalhado e propusemos oito instrumentos para apoiar a implementação
  do nosso framework e método, que são o Modelo de Arranjo do Local de Trabalho (I1),
  os níveis de Virtualidade e Integração Empresarial (I2), Barreiras e Facilitadores
  (I3), Escala de Envolvimento dos Trabalhadores (I4), Teoria do Ator-Rede (I5), Interesses,
  Valores e Necessidades das Gerações X, Y e Z (I6), Melhores Práticas (I7) e Metodologia
  Ágil (I8), sendo Teoria do Ator-Rede (I5) e Metodologia Ágil (I8) instrumentos já
  existentes e não elaborados no contexto dessa tese.Baseando-nos nas perceções obtidas
  a partir das duas RSLs, realizámos um estudo fenomenológico longitudinal para compreender
  como um fenómeno extremo como a COVID-19 e os seus confinamentos se alinham com
  o que foi previamente investigado sobre barreiras ao trabalho remoto. Este terceiro
  estudo identificou três barreiras adicionais experimentadas apenas durante situações
  extremas, como a COVID-19: ansiedade, dificuldade em manter uma rotina de trabalho
  e dificuldade em conciliar as tarefas de trabalho com o cuidado das crianças e o
  ensino em casa. Se aplicadas durante crises, estas barreiras devem ser consideradas
  ao usar o instrumento de Barreiras e Facilitadores (I3) em estudos futuros.O quarto
  estudo é uma análise aprofundada dos fatores que influenciam o envolvimento dos
  trabalhadores em ambientes de trabalho digitais. Nós as categorizamos em cinco fatores
  e construímos o modelo conceptual da nossa pesquisa. Conduzimos um estudo confirmatório
  usando análise de dados quantitativos de um questionário online para validá-lo.
  Esta pesquisa revelou diferenças e semelhanças geracionais no envolvimento no ambiente
  de trabalho digital, além de facilitadores e desafios em ambientes de trabalho digitais.
  Uma descoberta significativa foi a diminuição do compromisso e o aumento do distanciamento
  dos trabalhadores da Geração Z. Este estudo reforça a necessidade de compreender
  os valores e comportamentos geracionais para projetar estratégias que promovam o
  envolvimento de trabalhadores de diferentes gerações.Por fim, o quinto estudo demonstra
  e avalia o framework e o método em duas organizações. Este passo permitiu-nos validar
  a eficácia de ambos e melhorar seus procedimentos e instrumentos práticos. Coletivamente,
  estes estudos contribuem para a base de conhecimento sobre ambientes de trabalho
  digitais, envolvimento dos trabalhadores e características geracionais, oferecendo
  diretrizes práticas e destacando a importância de adaptar as estratégias de ambiente
  de trabalho digital para atender às necessidades de uma força de trabalho diversa
  e multigeracional. Apesar da relevância das características geracionais para a experiência
  dos trabalhadores no ambiente de trabalho digital, devemos evitar a armadilha de
  considerar apenas esta variável. É importante que estudos futuros que do ambiente
  de trabalho digital considerem os diversos fatores que influenciam o engajamento
  de colaboradores neste modelo.'
abstract_en: 'Technological advancements enabled organizations worldwide to focus
  on digital transformation, a trend accelerated by the COVID-19 pandemic. Concurrently,
  the emerging dominance of Generation Y (Millennials) and Z in the global workforce
  necessitates a deeper understanding of their experiences in digital workplaces.
  However, there is a gap in current studies on digital work environments to address
  the multigenerational workforce''s diverse needs and values. This gap contributes
  to employee disengagement, with only 23% of the global workforce feeling engaged
  in 2022, while 59% were not actively engaged, also called "quietly quitting," and
  18% were actively disengaged. This doctoral thesis comprises five studies investigating
  how organizations can implement engaging digital workplaces tailored for a multigenerational
  workforce. Our main objective is to develop a framework and method to support organizations
  during this process. We started with a Systematic Literature Review (SLR) on digital
  workplaces and employee engagement, followed by a second SLR on generational traits
  to investigate employee’s needs, values, and behaviors, particularly from Generations
  X, Y, and Z, and how they perceive the work experience in a digital environment.
  The findings reveal the need for a holistic view of the digital workplace, considering
  we recognized 75 factors that may positively or negatively influence employee engagement
  in this context. Furthermore, we were able to elaborate a detailed process and propose
  eight instruments to support the implementation of our framework and method, which
  are the Workplace Arrangement Model (C1), Virtuality and Enterprise Integration
  (I2), Barriers and Enablers (I3), Employee Engagement Scale (I4), Actor-network
  Theory (I5), Gens X, Y, and Z Interests, Values, and Needs (I6), Best Practices
  (I7), and Agile Methodology (I8). Actor-Network Theory (I5) and Agile Methodology
  (I8) are existing tools that were not developed within the context of this thesis.Drawing
  upon the insights gleaned from the two SLRs, we conducted one longitudinal phenomenological
  study to understand how an extreme phenomenon like COVID-19 and its lockdowns aligned
  with what was previously researched about barriers to remote work. This third study
  identified three additional barriers experienced only during extreme situations,
  such as COVID-19: anxiety, difficulty keeping a work routine, and difficulty accommodating
  job tasks with the child’s care and home-schooling. If applied during crises, these
  barriers should be considered when using the Barriers and Enablers (I3) instrument
  in future studies. The fourth study dives into the factors influencing employee
  engagement in digital workplaces. We categorized them into five sets and built the
  conceptual model of our research. We conducted a confirmatory study using quantitative
  data analysis from an online survey to validate our conceptual model. This study
  revealed generational differences and similarities in engagement, attitudes, and
  challenges in digital workspaces. A significant finding was Generation Z employees''
  decreased commitment and increased detachment. This study highlights the need to
  understand generational values and behaviors to design strategies that foster the
  engagement of employees from different generations.Lastly, the fifth study demonstrates
  and evaluates the framework and method in two real organizations. This step allows
  us to validate the framework and method''s effectiveness and enhance the procedures
  and practical instruments. Collectively, these studies contribute to the body of
  knowledge on digital workplaces, employee engagement, and generational traits, offering
  practical guidelines and highlighting the importance of tailoring workplace strategies
  to meet the needs of a diverse, multi-generational workforce. Besides the relevance
  of generational characteristics to the employees'' experience in the Digital Workplace,
  we must avoid the trap of considering only the cohort variable. Future studies on
  the digital workplace need to consider the various factors influencing employee
  engagement within this model.'

# Designing Engaging Digital Workplaces for a Multigenerational Workforce

Caroline Relva de Moraes 
 
 
 
 
DESIGNING ENGAGING DIGITAL 
WORKPLACES FOR A MULTIGENERATIONAL 
WORKFORCE

Informação, orientada pelo Professor Doutor Paulo Rupino da Cunha e 
Professora Doutora Isabel Ramos e apresentada ao Departamento de 
Engenharia Informática da Faculdade de Ciências e Tecnologia da 
Universidade de Coimbra. 
 
 
 
Agosto de 2024


 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Caroline Relva de Moraes 
 
 
Designing Engaging Digital 
Workplaces for a Multigenerational 
Workforce 
 
 
 
 
 
Tese no âmbito do Doutoramento em Engenharia Informática, Sistemas de 
Informação, orientada pelo Professor Doutor Paulo Rupino da Cunha e Professora 
Doutora Isabel Ramos e apresentada ao Departamento de Engenharia Informática da 
Faculdade de Ciências e Tecnologia da Universidade de Coimbra. 
 
 
 
Agosto de 2024 
 


 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Caroline Relva de Moraes 
 
 
 
Designing Engaging Digital 
Workplaces for a Multigenerational 
Workforce 
 
 
 
 
 
This dissertation was written in the context of the Ph.D. Program in Informatics 
Engineering, advised by Professor Paulo Rupino da Cunha and Professor Isabel 
Ramos, presented to the Department of Informatics Engineering of the Faculty 
of Sciences and Technology of the University of Coimbra. 
 
 
August, 2024 
 
 


 
  
Resumo 
Os avanços tecnológicos permitiram que organizações em todo o mundo investissem na 
transformação digital, uma tendência acelerada pela pandemia causada pelo COVID -19. 
Paralelamente, a crescente predominância das Gerações Y (Millennials) e Z na força de 
trabalho global exige uma compreensão mais profunda das suas perceções em ambientes 
de trabalho digitais. No entanto, uma falha nos estudos atuais sobre ambientes de trabalho 
digitais é não abordar as diversas necessidades e valores de uma força de trabalho 
multigeracional. Esta falta de conhecimento pode contribuir para o não envolvimento dos 
trabalhadores, sendo que apenas 23% da força de trabalho global se sentia envolvida em 
2022, enquanto 59% não estavam ativamente envolvidos, também chamados de " quiet 
quitting," e 18% estavam ativamente não envolvidos. 
Esta tese de doutoramento compreende cinco estudos que investigam como as 
organizações podem implementar ambientes de trabalho digitais envolventes, adaptados 
a uma força de trabalho multigeracional. O nosso principal objetivo é desenvolver um 
framework e um método para apoiar as organizações durante este processo. Começámos 
com uma Revisão Sistemática da Literatura (RSL) sobre ambientes de trabalho digitais e 
o envolvimento dos trabalhadores, seguida de uma segunda RSL sobre características 
geracionais para investigar as necessidades, valores e comportamentos dos colaboradores, 
particularmente das Gerações X, Y e Z, e como percebem a experiência num ambiente de 
trabalho digital. As conclusões enfatizaram a necessidade de uma visão holística do 
ambiente de t rabalho digital, considerando que reconhecemos 75 fatores que podem 
influenciar positiva ou negativamente o envolvimento dos trabalhadores. Além disso, 
elaboramos um processo detalhado e propusemos oito instrumentos para apoiar a 
implementação do nosso framework e método, que são o Modelo de Arranjo do Local de 
Trabalho (I1), os níveis de Virtualidade e Integração Empresarial (I2), Barreiras e 
Facilitadores (I3), Escala de Envolvimento dos Trabalhadores (I4), Teoria do Ator-Rede 
(I5), Interesses, Valores e Necessidades das Gerações X, Y e Z (I6), Melhores Práticas 
(I7) e Metodologia Ágil (I8) , sendo Teoria do Ator -Rede (I5) e Metodologia Ágil (I8) 
instrumentos já existentes e não elaborados no contexto dessa tese. 
Baseando-nos nas perceções obtidas a partir das duas RSLs, realizámos um estudo 
fenomenológico longitudinal para compreender como um fenómeno extremo como a 
COVID-19 e os seus confinamentos se alinham com o que foi previamente investigado 
sobre barreiras a o trabalho remoto. Este terceiro estudo identificou três barreiras 
adicionais experimentadas apenas durante situações extremas, como a COVID -19: 
ansiedade, dificuldade em manter uma rotina de trabalho e dificuldade em conciliar as 
tarefas de trabalho com o cuidado das crianças e o ensino em casa. Se aplicadas durante 
crises, estas barreiras devem ser consideradas ao usar o instrumento de Barreiras e 
Facilitadores (I3) em estudos futuros. 
O quarto estudo é uma análise aprofundada dos fatores que influenciam o envolvimento 
dos trabalhadores em ambientes de trabalho digitais. Nós as categorizamos em cinco 
fatores e construímos o modelo conceptual da nossa pesquisa. Conduzimos um estudo 
confirmatório usando análise de dados quantitativos de um questionário online para 
validá-lo. Esta pesquisa revelou diferenças e semelhanças geracionais no envolvimento 
no ambiente de trabalho digital, além de facilitadores e desafios em ambientes de trabalho 
digitais. Uma descoberta significativa foi a diminuição do compromisso e o aumento do 

 
 
 
distanciamento dos trabalhadores da Geração Z. Este estudo reforça a necessidade de 
compreender os valores e comportamentos geracionais para projetar estratégias que 
promovam o envolvimento de trabalhadores de diferentes gerações. 
Por fim, o quinto estudo demonstra e avalia o framework e o método em duas 
organizações. Este passo permitiu -nos validar a eficácia de ambos e melhorar seus 
procedimentos e instrumentos práticos.  
Coletivamente, estes estudos contribuem para a base de conhecimento sobre ambientes 
de trabalho digitais, envolvimento dos trabalhadores e características geracionais, 
oferecendo diretrizes práticas e destacando a importância de adaptar as estratégias de 
ambiente de trabalho digital para atender às necessidades de uma força de trabalho diversa 
e multigeracional. Apesar da relevância das características geracionais para a experiência 
dos trabalhadores no ambiente de trabalho digital, devemos evitar a armadil ha de 
considerar apenas esta variável. É importante que estudos futuros que do ambiente de 
trabalho digital considerem os diversos fatores que influenciam o engajamento de 
colaboradores neste modelo. 
 
  
 
 
 
 
 
Palavras-Chave 
Ambiente de trabalho digital, envolvimento dos trabalhadores, características 
geracionais.

 
 
 
 
Abstract 
Technological advancements enabled organizations worldwide to focus on digital 
transformation, a trend accelerated by the COVID -19 pandemic. Concurrently, the 
emerging dominance of Generation Y (Millennials) and Z in the global workforce 
necessitates a deeper understanding of their experiences in digital workplaces. However, 
there is a gap in current studies on digital work environments to address the 
multigenerational workforce's diverse needs and values. This gap contributes to employee 
disengagement, with only 23% of the global workforce feeling engaged in 2022, while 
59% were not actively engaged, also called " quietly quitting ," and 18% were actively 
disengaged.  
This doctoral thesis comprises five studies investigating how organizations can 
implement engaging digital workplaces tailored for a multigenerational workforce. Our 
main objective is to develop a framework and method to support organizations during this 
process. We started with a Systematic Literature Review (SLR) on digital workplaces and 
employee engagement, followed by a second SLR on generational traits to investigate 
employee’s needs, values, and behaviors, particularly from Generations X, Y, and Z, and 
how they perceive the work experience in a digital environment. The findings reveal the 
need for a holistic view of the digital workplace, considering we recognized 75 factors 
that may positively or negatively influence employee engagement in this conte xt. 
Furthermore, we were able to elaborate a detailed process and propose eight instruments 
to support the implementation of our framework and method, which are the Workplace 
Arrangement Model (C1), Virtuality and Enterprise Integration (I2), Barriers and 
Enablers (I3), Employee Engagement Scale (I4), Actor-network Theory (I5), Gens X, Y, 
and Z Interests, Values, and Needs (I6), Best Practices (I7), and Agile Methodology (I8). 
Actor-Network Theory (I5) and Agile Methodology (I8) are existing tools that were not 
developed within the context of this thesis. 
Drawing upon the insights gleaned from the two SLRs, we conducted one longitudinal 
phenomenological study to understand how an extreme phenomenon like COVID-19 and 
its lockdowns aligned with what was previously researched about barriers to remote work. 
This third study identified three additional barriers experienced only during extreme 
situations, such as COVID-19: anxiety, difficulty keeping a work routine, and difficulty 
accommodating job tasks with the child’s care and home -schooling. If applied during 
crises, these barriers should be considered when using the Barriers and Enablers (I3) 
instrument in future studies.  
The fourth study dives into the factors influencing employee engagement in digital 
workplaces. We categorized them into five sets and built the conceptual model of our 
research. We conducted a confirmatory study using quantitative data analysis from an 
online survey to validate our conceptual model. This study revealed generational 
differences and similarities in engagement, attitudes, and challenges in digital 
workspaces. A significant finding was Generation Z employees' decreased commitment 
and increased detachment. This study highlights the need to understand generational 
values and behaviors to design strategies that foster the engagement of employees from 
different generations. 

 
  
Lastly, the fifth study demonstrates and evaluates the framework and method in two real 
organizations. This step allows us to validate the framework and method's effectiveness 
and enhance the procedures and practical instruments.  
Collectively, these studies contribute to the body of knowledge on digital workplaces, 
employee engagement, and generational traits, offering practical guidelines and 
highlighting the importance of tailoring workplace strategies to meet the needs of a 
diverse, multi -generational workforce. Besides the relevance of generational 
characteristics to the employees' experience in the Digital Workplace, we must avoid the 
trap of considering only the cohort variable. Future studies on the digital workplace need 
to consider the various factors influencing employee engagement within this model. 
 
 
 
Keywords 
Digital workplace, employee engagement, generational traits.

1 
 
Chapter 1  
Introduction 
Organizations globally have been actively pursuing digital transformation initiatives 
(Tavares et al., 2021). The COVID -19 pandemic has further accelerated the 
reconsideration of traditional workplace models, challenging the prevailing paradigm of 
full-time physical office presence (OECD, 2021). In the aftermath of the pandemic, many 
employees who can work remotely have expressed reluctance to return to full-time office 
work (OECD, 2021). Nevertheless, resistance from certain companies, such as Tesla, 
advocating for full -time office presence underscores the complexity of this transition 
(Amariei, 2022). Remote work, while popular, has also been associ ated with stress and 
burnout (Microsoft, 2022), highlighting the need for careful consideration in changing 
workplace arrangements. 
In Information Systems (IS), practitioners and academics emphasize aligning workplaces 
with digital business strategies (Zimmer et al., 2020). This necessitates addressing virtual 
or hybrid teams' challenges during the transition (Florea & Stoica, 2019). Successful 
digital workplace implementation involves not only overcoming cha llenges through 
appropriate tools, supportive leadership styles, and aligning existing practices with digital 
paradigms (Wakefield et al., 2008; Ruppel et al., 2013; Zimmer et al., 2020) but also 
accommodating the diverse needs and values of a multigenerational workforce (Buchanan 
et al., 2016). Particularly Millennials and Generation Z, given their emphasis on 
flexibility and work -life balance, areas where the digital work environment can offer 
support (Larkin, 2017). Recognizing the crucial role of employ ee acceptance for 
successful implementation, Marsh (2018) emphasizes the importance of creating 
engaging digital workplaces that align with employees' values. Given the substantial 
investments in digital transformation, understanding how to design effectiv e digital 
workplaces is vital (Shirer, 2022).  
This introductory chapter is structured into five sections. Section 1.1 sets the context for 
the study, while section 1.2 defines the research objectives. Section 1.3 then outlines the 
research approach, followed by a discussion on the significance of our study in Section 
1.4. The final section offers a summary of the thesis structure. 
 
 
 
 
 
 

Introduction 
 
2 
 
1.1  Background 
The term 'digital workplace' (DW) was initially coined in the late 1990s (Williams & 
Schubert, 2018). Since then, other authors have defined this concept by focusing on 
technology or the virtual dimension. The concept encompasses teleworking, 
telecommuting, e-working, remote, and agile (Zimmer et al., 2020). For instance, Köffer 
(2015) describes it as “the collection of all of the digital tools provided by an organization 
to allow its employees to do their jobs ,” while Alsharo et al. (2017) describe it as “[t] he 
resulting mix of teams in an organization, some completely distributed while others a mix 
of co-located and distributed, is deemed as the degree of team virtualness.” However, the 
DW concept has evolved to emphasize its sociotechnical nature, integrating people, 
processes, and technology (Larkin, 2017). Some studies often blend information 
processing theories with psychological, social, or communication theories, unde rlining 
the importance of both technical and human elements (Walvoord et al., 2008; O’Neill et 
al., 2014; Shaik & Makhecha, 2019). A more recent definition characterizes the DW as 
“the physical, cultural, and digital arrangements that simplify working life in complex, 
dynamic, and often unstructured working environments” (Zimmer et al., 2020). Adopting 
a holistic perspective on the DW is crucial because purely virtual teams may stagnate 
over time due to knowledge erosion (Kauppila et al., 2011), necessitating technological 
tools to facilitate social processes (Wakefield et al., 2008). Building on these perspectives 
and to serve as common ground in the scope of our research, we propose and use the 
following working definition:  
“A digital workplace is an ensemble of people, business processes, and technology 
designed to enable work to be done seamlessly from any location, such as home, 
collaborative space, office, or other, without compromising efficiency or effectiveness.” 
Previous research has yielded conflicting results on DW performance, mainly due to an 
inadequate exploration of the combined factors influencing employees' perceptions 
(Gilson et al., 2015). The same authors further propose that "examining the behaviors of 
various generations (e.g., communication, knowledge sharing, and collaboration) could 
offer insights into overcoming perceived barriers introduced by virtual environments. " 
Since sustainable organizational performance is closely tied to employee engagement 
(Gallup, 2022), organizations must understand how to design engaging DWs catering  to 
a diverse, multigenerational workforce.  
The literature explores various aspects of employee engagement, highlighting its 
multifaceted nature. It discusses different dimensions, including organizational, social 
and intellectual, work, and job (Shuck et al., 2017). It emphasizes the distinction between 
engagement and burnout and the need to assess them separately using distinct 
measurement tools (Shuck et al., 2017). Employee engagement is associated with positive 
outcomes, including improved work quality, reduced absenteeism, more outstanding 
organizational commitment, and lower turnover rates (Crowe, 2016). The literature 
mentions multiple contributors influencing engagement, such as individual 
characteristics, job resources or enablers, job demands or barriers, leadership, and job or 
organizational characteristics (Saks & Gruman, 2014). It also suggests that different 
generations may perceive employee engagement differently (Schullery, 2013). 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
3 
 
1.2  Research Objective 
This doctoral thesis aims to develop a comprehensive framework and a method that 
includes detailed procedures to design engaging digital workplaces in organizations. 
Combined, these artifacts aim to support the implementation of an engaging DW tailored 
to the needs, values, and behaviors of a multigenerational workforce. As digital 
workplaces become increasingly prevalent across various organizational sectors, they 
present an opportunity to address and resolve pressing challenges organizations face 
today (Florea & Stoica, 2019) . Specifically, the research aims to achieve the following 
objectives: 
1. Framework Development:  Support organizations in implementing engaging digital 
workplaces. This framework is specifically crafted to address the diverse needs of a 
multi-generational workforce. 
 
2. Method Development: Support the application of the proposed framework with 
detailed procedures that organizations can follow. This method includes specific 
instruments to support the execution of tasks within the method, making it practical 
and actionable for real-world implementation. 
By achieving these objectives, this doctoral research seeks to contribute to both academic 
literature and practical applications in the field of IS through the design of a framework 
and method to support the implementation of a digital workplace tailored f or a multi -
generational workforce needs, detailing the procedures for practical implementation, 
including instruments such as a Workplace Arrangement Model addressing virtuality and 
enterprise integration, identification of barriers and enablers impacting key digital 
workplace factors, an Employee Engagement Scale, Actor-Network Theory for mapping 
employee networks and conflicts, strategies based on generational values, and best 
practices for enhancing engagement. 
1.3  Research Approach  
The research approach adopted for this doctoral thesis is grounded in design -based 
research, characterized by the iterative development and testing of artifacts in real-world 
settings to address complex and actual problems. The artifacts of this research are a 
framework and a method designed to support organizations implementing engaging DW, 
tailored to the needs, values, and behaviors of a multigenerational workforce. 
Two systematic literature reviews were conducted to initiate the research process to 
identify, evaluate, and synthesize existing knowledge related to DWs, employee 
engagement, and generational traits. They provided the foundation and theoretical 
grounding for defining the problem statement, research objective, and proposed solution. 
They supported understanding the various factors influencing employee perceptions and 
experiences in a digital work environment. After this initial stage, we performed mixed 
quantitative and qualitative studies to gather rich and contextual insights into the factors 
affecting employee engagement in a DW, also in extreme situations such as the COVID-
19 outbreak. They enabled a holistic view of the employee experience, identifying the 
factors influencing their perceptions and attitudes toward the DW. 
We performed one longitudinal phenomenological study during the pandemic that 
included one online survey and two rounds of interviews to gather the distinct employees' 

Introduction 
 
4 
 
experiences of the digital workplace in extreme situations like the COVID -19 outbreak. 
This study provided the additional barriers experienced in this scenario, the human and 
digital actors, and their relationships in this context. After that, we were able to develop 
the conceptual model of our research. We performed a confirmatory study using 
quantitative data analysis from an online survey to validate it . The online survey was 
administered to employees from different organizations  worldwide, in different sectors, 
to assess the validity and reliability of applying the instruments combined to capture their 
influence on the engagement of different generations in a DW. 
The framework and method were validated and refined through one complete 
implementation cycle to ensure practical relevance, usability, and effectiveness in 
fostering engagement in a digital work environment. Combining qualitative and 
quantitative studies,  this research aimed to comprehensively understand the complex 
dynamics and factors influencing employee engagement in a DW. Design -based 
research's iterative and participatory nature facilitated the development and validation of 
the framework and method in a real organizational environment. 
Chapter 2 provides a detailed account of the research process. 
1.4  Relevance 
The relevance of this study encompasses both practical organizational implications and 
contributions to the IS community. In the contemporary digital era, transforming 
traditional workplaces into engaging digital work environments is no longer a mere trend 
but a necessity for organizations striving to adapt, innovate, and sustain competitive 
advantage (Shaik et al., 2019). This research addresses this imperative by focusing on 
developing a framework and a method specifically tailored to support the implementation 
of an engaging DW that considers the unique needs, values, and behaviors of a 
multigenerational workforce. 
Implementing an engaging DW addresses a real organizational problem with the 
opportunity to offer flexible work arrangements, a feature highly valued by newer 
generations (Larkin, 2017) . A well -designed DW can significantly contribute to 
sustainable organizational performance by enhancing employee communication, 
collaboration, and knowledge sharing (Alsharo et al., 2017). This, in turn, can lead to 
improved innovation, operational efficiency, and overall organizational resilience in the 
face of evolving business challenges and opportunities (Alsharo et al., 2017).  
This research aims to contribute to the IS community by exploring and demonstrating 
how technology, people, and processes can influence employee engagement in a DW. 
Developing a comprehensive framework and method through design -based research 
provides theoretical and practical contributions to the IS field. The research offers a 
deeper understanding of the complex dynamics and factors influencing the employee 
experience in a DW, and it provides organizations with actionable guidance and 
instruments to design, implement, and optimize their DW strategies effectively. 
The research findings, insights, and practical implications were disseminated to the IS 
community through publications in peer -reviewed journals and conferences. They 
address real organizational challenges and contribute to advancing knowledge and 
practices. Through its integrative approach, the research aims to support organizations in 
harnessing the potential of digital technologies to create engaging and strategically 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
5 
 
aligned workplaces that cater to the diverse needs of a multigenerational workforce in 
today's digital business landscape. 
1.5  Thesis Outline 
This thesis consists of six chapters organized as follows: 
Chapter 1 (the current chapter) introduces the reader to the research discussed in this 
thesis. This overview consists of the study's background, the research's objective, the 
adopted research approach, and the study's relevance. This first chapter concludes with a 
synopsis of the thesis structure. 
Chapter 2 deals with research methodology. The first part describes the discussion to 
define the research methodology. Next, Section 2.2 frames the Actor -network Theory in 
our investigation, followed by Section 2.3, which provides the evolution of DSR, its 
principles, guidelines, and procedures, concluding with the evalua tion framework 
adopted in our investigation. Section 2.4 instantiates our design science research project 
and presents its initial phases. The final part of Chapter 2 outlines the research question 
this thesis aims to answer. 
Chapter 3 provides the background of the study. The first part conceptualizes the 
evolution of DW arrangements and the theories that underpin the use of technologies in 
the work environment. This is followed by a discussion of the influence of generational  
traits in the work environment and its implications at individual, team, and organizational 
levels of analysis. The subsequent section explores the engagement phenomenon and the 
different contributors and outcomes of employee engagement, followed by synth esizing 
prior artifacts identified in the literature to implement engaging digital workplaces. The 
final part of Chapter 3 summarizes the research gaps and opportunities in the literature. 
Chapter 4, titled "DSR Design and Development," focuses on conceptualizing, designing, 
and developing the framework and method for designing engaging DWs. The chapter 
introduces the DSR Process Model for engaging DWs, followed by Artifact Design and 
Development. Section 4.1 details the data collection and analysis of the longitudinal 
phenomenological study and the confirmatory study that provided the necessary 
information to validate the conceptual model of our research and the instruments used to 
design the artifacts. The artifact development section outlines the framework, method, 
instruments, and procedures to support their implementation process. The chapter 
concludes by describing the iterative design process and the rationale behind the design 
decisions. 
Chapter 5, "DSR Demonstration and Evaluation," focuses on the practical application and 
assessment of the developed framework and method for designing engaging DWs. The 
chapter begins by explaining the participants' selection process and ethical considerations 
applied to demonstrate and evaluate the framework and method in the organizations 
involved. Section 5.2 presents all data and findings from the two demonstration cycles 
performed in a real organization. Next, Section 5.3  details the process applied and the 
findings of the evaluation cycle performed with two distinct teams in another real 
environment. This is followed by assessing the framework and method's usability, 
feasibility, and scalability to determine its broader applicability. Furthermore, Section 5.5 
consolidates the challenges encountered during the implementation process and lessons 
learned. The chapter concludes by summarizing the insights gained from implementing 
and evaluating the artifacts. 

Introduction 
 
6 
 
Chapter 6, "DSR Communication and Conclusions," starts by listing the six papers 
published during this doctoral work, followed by the contribution summary in Section 
6.2. Next, Section 6.3 highlights the recommendations for practitioners and organizations 
on implementing engaging DWs. This chapter concludes with the research limitations 
and directions for future research.

 
7 
 
Chapter 2  
Research Methodology 
The choice of research methodology becomes paramount in understanding and addressing 
complex organizational challenges within the realm of IS. Chapter 2 sets the stage for defining 
and contextualizing our research approach, evaluating through Action Resear ch (AR) and 
Design Science Research (DSR) methodologies. Deliberations on our research methodology 
were guided by the imperative of selecting a framework that facilitates problem -solving and 
accommodates iterative researcher engagement within organizationa l contexts. AR and DSR 
offer promising avenues for such endeavors, each endowed with distinctive contributions and 
methodological considerations. 
The first section of this chapter contextualizes both AR and DSR. After thorough discourse 
among the researcher and her supervisors, DSR emerged as the chosen path, aligning 
seamlessly with our objective of developing method artifacts to solve a real organizational 
problem. It is followed by Section 2.2, which delves into applying Actor -Network Theory 
(ANT) as an indispensable tool in our research. ANT provides a lens through which we unravel 
the intricate interplay between digital and  non-digital actors, shaping relationships and 
networks within the DW. 
Section 2.3 details the DSR methodology, starting with its evolution and followed by principles, 
guidelines, and procedures. This section concludes with the evaluation methods applied in our 
research. Transitioning into Section 2.4, the focus shifts toward s the instantiation of our DSR 
project. Herein lies the main objective and methods used during each Research phase. 
Additionally, the conclusions of the initial two phases of our DSR project (Identify the Problem 
and Motivation and Define the Objectives of a Solution) are included. 
Finally, Section 2.5 summarizes the conclusions of our methodological journey, laying the 
groundwork for a rigorous and systematic exploration of DW dynamics driven by the 
commitment to rigor and relevance. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

Research Methodology 
 
8 
 
2.1  Research Methodology Selection 
We aim to create and evaluate a framework and method for supporting the implementation of 
engaging DW. We have considered whether to use AR or DSR since both are problem-solving 
oriented and allow for iterative researcher involvement within organizational contexts (Hevner 
et al., 2004; Sein et al., 2011). We analyzed similarities and differences between AR and DSR 
to discuss their paradigmatic assumptions by analyzing their ontological, epistemological, 
methodological, and ethical perspectives. Both AR and DSR transform individual experiences 
into actionable insights and provide valuable content for practical application (Cunha & 
Figueiredo, 2002; Hevner et al., 2004). AR is characterized by an anti-realist ontology, viewing 
social reality as socially constr ucted and changeable (Iivari & Venable, 2009). In contrast, 
DSR's ontology may vary from realistic to anti-realistic, with examples from engineering often 
leaning towards realism (Iivari & Venable, 2009). While DSR adopts a flexible operational 
structure i nstead of rigid guidelines (Hevner et al., 2004), it is rooted in an engineering 
perspective from an epistemological standpoint, whereas AR is grounded in social science. 
Epistemologically, DSR can incorporate both positivistic and anti -positivistic approa ches, 
especially in evaluating artifacts, while AR epistemology leans towards anti -positivism, 
emphasizing understanding from the perspective of those directly involved (Iivari & Venable, 
2009). AR generates knowledge through action (Sein et al., 2011), wh ereas DSR focuses on 
developing and testing artifacts to address specific challenges (Hevner et al., 2004). 
Methodologically, AR is idiographic, focusing on individual cases rather than general laws. In 
contrast, DSR may use nomothetic and idiographic meth ods, with constructive research being 
a distinctive category for building method artifacts (Iivari & Venable, 2009). Table 2.1 
summarizes the paradigmatic assumptions of both methodologies. 
Table 2.1 - Summary of AR and DSR assumptions, adapted from Iivari & Venable (2009). 
Paradigmatic 
dimension 
Action Research Design Science Research 
Ontology Anti-realism Realism or anti-realism 
Epistemology Mainly anti-positivism Mainly positivism, but also anti -positivism, 
especially in the evaluation 
Methodology Idiographic Constructive (building)  
Nomothetic (evaluation)  
Idiographic (evaluation) 
While AR emphasizes understanding and change within specific contexts, DSR focuses on 
developing generalizable solutions, often within technology and engineering (Iivari & Venable, 
2009). After extensive reflection regarding the merits and drawbacks of AR and DSR for our 
project, we have opted to employ the DSR methodology due to its primary objective of 
supporting the development of method artifacts with organizational focus, such as our proposed 
framework and method (Cleven et al., 2009). Moreover, considering several factors and actors 
involved in the digital workplace, we decided to use ANT since it offers a multidimensional 
perspective to understand and analyze the compl ex networks of human and non -human actors 
(e.g., technologies, policies, practices) involved in the design and implementation of the artifact 
(Cresswell et al., 2010). The following section provides information regarding ANT. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
9 
 
2.2  Actor-Network Theory (ANT) 
ANT's epistemological and ontological stance posits that the world comprises networks 
(Cresswell et al., 2010). ANT provides a framework for comprehending how human and non -
human actors interact within these networks (Latour, 2005), focusing on creating and describing 
relationships and offering intervention tools (Carroll et al., 2012). In this context, an "actor" can 
be any human or non-human entity that influences actions within the network (Cresswell et al., 
2010). ANT allows for a nuanced understanding of organizational complexities, particularly 
how technology shapes social interaction s (Cresswell et al., 2010). Depending on the 
conceptualization of each reality, actors may only act in conjunction with others within existing 
possibilities (Cresswell et al., 2010). The socio -ecological model of ANT (Figure 2.1) aids in 
navigating the int ricate interplay of individual, organizational, technological, societal, and 
cultural factors (Shin, 2016). 
 
Figure 2.1 - Socio-ecological model of ANT, from Shin (2016). 
ANT facilitates the identification of actor relationships, the examination of network dynamics, 
and the attainment of temporary stability within networks. It is instrumental in recognizing 
conflicts of interest among actors and their resultant challenges, helping researchers balance 
these interests (Doolin & Lowe, 2002). The network's equilibrium is affected by the addition or 
removal of any actor (Doolin & Lowe, 2002), with relationships becoming more evident when 
disruptions occur within the network (Cres swell et al., 2010). ANT employs materiality, 
inscription, and translation concepts to elucidate how technological elements impact networks 
(Carroll et al., 2012). Figure 2.2 depicts the formation of an actor-network as a phenomenon, 
highlighting the processes of translation and inscription (Carroll, 2014). 
 
Figure 2.2 - Process of translation and inscription, from Carroll (2014). 


Research Methodology 
 
10 
 
The translation process aims to align the interests of various actors, unfolding in four phases: 
(1) Problematization: The focal actor identifies the problem, and procedures are agreed upon 
to achieve system equilibrium. An obligatory passage point (OPP) is established to mobilize 
actors toward a common goal (Callon, 1986). 
(2) Interessement: The focal actor aligns actors' interests to bolster relationships and maintain 
network equilibrium (Callon, 1986). 
(3) Enrollment: Other actors accept the defined interests, assuming new roles within the 
emerging actor-network (Callon, 1986). 
(4) Mobilization: Actors represent the interests of others, ensuring their inclusion within the 
network (Callon, 1986). 
Advancements in digital communication technologies facilitate new forms of interaction, 
enabling the alignment of actor interests and supporting network formation (Carroll et al., 
2012). The inscription phase creates technical objects to protect actor interests and determines 
network operations (Carroll, 2014). Once irre versibility is reached, actors accept changes and  
are no longer willing to reverse to previous states. 
Using ANT in our DSR project offers a multidimensional perspective to understand the 
complex networks of human and non -human actors involved in designing engaging DWs 
tailored for a multigenerational workforce. ANT's actor -centered approach facilitates 
identifying, mapping, and analyzing interactions and relations among various actors, enabling 
a holistic understanding of the DW ecosystem. By designing the artifact considering the 
integration of different actors, we could use the ANT process to effectively promote negotiation 
and alignment of the DW solution to meet diverse needs. Additionally, ANT supports an 
adaptive and dynamic approach to design and development, enabling continuous adaptation, 
evolution, and improvement of the DW in response to the chang ing needs and interactions 
among various actors within the organizational context. 
In applying ANT to the design and development phase, the artifact's design considered 
identifying and mapping key actors, analyzing interactions and relations, and engaging 
stakeholders in collaborative co -design to ensure the DW meets their needs and expe ctations 
(detailed in Chapter 4). The artifact adopted an iterative design process, facilitating continuous 
DW solution adaptation and improvement based on various actors' feedback and contributions. 
Furthermore, the demonstration and valuation phases (det ailed in Chapter 5) considered 
empirical evaluation and validation of the designed artifact to assess its effectiveness, usability, 
and impact on engagement among different organizational actors. By integrating ANT into our 
DSR project, we created an artifact to implement an engaging DW that addresses this context's 
complex and dynamic needs. 
2.3  Design Science Research Methodology 
The primary objective of this section is to provide a comprehensive overview of the DSR 
methodology, elucidating its evolution, principles, and processes that guide the execution of 
our research to ensure it is systematic, rigorous, and reproducible. We explore the iterative 
nature of DSR, its foundational activities, and the criteria for rigor and relevance that guide the 
design and evaluation of artifacts. Addition ally, we discuss the role of theory in DSR, 
highlighting how we integrated the theoretical insights of ANT into the design process to ensure 
the development of robust and practical solutions. This section serves as a roadmap for the 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
11 
 
subsequent chapters, outlining the methodological framework that underpins the research 
endeavors and guiding the reader through the systematic and rigorous process of design, 
creation, and evaluation inherent in the DSR approach. 
In IS research, it is imperative to analyze technology and behavior in conjunction with a task 
facilitated by the DSRM. Table 2.2 summarizes the rationale for the research methodology. 
Table 2.2 - Summary of the DSRM focus and rationale, adapted from Wahyuni (2012).

Methodological

(Research Process) 
Design science methodology involves systematically creating and 
evaluating innovative solutions to address specific problems. It 
emphasizes the iterative development of artifacts, guided by 
established design principles and rigorous evaluation criteria. This 
methodology integrates theoretical knowledge and practical 
application to produce novel solutions that contribute to advancing the 
IS field and implementing digital workplaces. 
Research Design It involves the systematic development and evaluation of innovative 
artifacts for real-organizational problems. It follows a structured 
approach, including problem identification, solution design, 
development, demonstration, evaluation, and communication. 
Methods Systematic Literature Reviews, Case Studies. 
Data Collection and 
Analysis 
Online surveys and interviews, using analytical statistics for 
quantitative data and a top-down approach for coding qualitative data. 
Role of researcher Create and evaluate an innovative framework and method to address a 
real organizational problem. This includes identifying relevant 
research questions, designing and developing a method artifact, and 
rigorously assessing its effectiveness. The researcher contributes new 
knowledge to the Information Systems (IS) field by documenting and 
sharing their findings. Additionally, the researcher must ensure the 
solution's relevance and applicability while upholding ethical standards 
throughout the research process. 
The DSR methodology (DSRM) enables us to tackle real organizational challenges by creating 
and evaluating artifacts grounded in technology, organization, and people (Hevner et al., 2004; 

Research Methodology 
 
12 
 
Vom Brocke et al., 2020). As Simon (1969) states, " [d]esign science endeavors to create 
solutions that fulfill human purposes." 
2.3.1  Evolution of Design Science Research 
DSR has been utilized by IS researchers for nearly three decades, evolving significantly during 
this time (Peffers et al., 2007). While numerous scholars have made significant contributions to 
DSR, the seminal paper by Hevner et al. (2004) propelled DSR in  IS to the forefront. Some 
researchers consider it the standard approach for conducting and evaluating DSR. In 2004, 
Hevner et al. stated that design science aims to expand human and organizational capabilities 
by innovating new artifacts. In contrast, behavioral science strives to develop and validate 
theories that elucidate or forecast human or organizational behavior. Figure 2.3 illustra tes the 
primary contributions to the evolution of DSR (Baskerville et al., 2018). 
 
Figure 2.3 - DSR Evolution, adapted from Baskerville et al. (2018). 
The emergence of DSR in IS can be attributed to the increasing demand for practical and 
actionable solutions to complex problems organizations and individuals face in effectively 
leveraging IT. It goes beyond traditional scientific methods to create and ev aluate innovative 
artifacts (Hevner et al., 2004). March and Smith (1995) identified four types of artifacts in DSR: 
construct, model, method, and instantiation. A construct is a conceptualization used to describe 
and specify solutions to problems within a  domain, while a model consists of propositions 
expressing relationships among constructs. Methods are steps used to perform tasks, and 
instantiations are realizations of artifacts in their operational environment. In recent years, 
theorizing has been reco gnized as a significant output of DSR (Gregor & Jones, 2007), and 
theory has been incorporated as a potential DSR artifact (Winter, 2008). Peffers et al. (2007) 
expanded the taxonomy of artifact types, including frameworks (metamodels) and algorithms. 
Meanwhile, Gregor and Jones (2007) view frameworks as a form of theory. DSR has grown in 
prominence and acceptance, guided by evolving objectives and principles.  
2.3.2   Principles and Guidelines of Design Science Research 
The core components of a methodology include principles, guidelines, rules, and procedures 
(Peffers et al., 2007). In DSR, the fundamental principle is utility (Hevner et al., 2004). An 
artifact developed through DSR must exhibit methodological rigor and r elevance, 
demonstrating high quality and effectiveness. The fundamental principles, as outlined by 
Baskerville et al. (2018) and Vom Brocke et al. (2020), are: 
(1) Positioning: Define the problem’s context and relevance, ensuring the research addresses 
a significant issue. 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
13 
 
(2) Grounding: Base the research on solid theoretical and empirical foundations, using existing 
knowledge to support the design and development of the artifact. 
(3) Aligning: Ensure coherence between research objectives, methods, and outcomes, aligning 
the artifact with the problem and intended contributions. 
(4) Advancing: Contribute new knowledge and innovation, demonstrating how the research 
extends existing theories, practices, or methodologies. 
These principles collectively guide researchers in conducting rigorous and impactful DSR, 
ensuring that their work is well -positioned, grounded in existing knowledge, coherently 
aligned, and contributes to advancing the field. Moreover, Hevner et al. (2004 ) provided 
practical guidelines for conducting DSR: 
● Guideline 1 - Design as an Artifact: Design, develop, and evaluate novel and innovative 
artifacts that address specific and practical problems or opportunities in the domain of 
IS. 
● Guideline 2 - Problem Relevance: Address significant real -world problems, ensuring 
the research produces practical, helpful, and applicable solutions. 
● Guideline 3 - Design Evaluation: The design of the artifact itself is the primary outcome 
of the research. 
● Guideline 4 - Research Contributions: Integrate and validate theoretical insights by 
designing and evaluating artifacts to advance theoretical knowledge in IS. 
● Guideline 5 - Research Rigor: To ensure the rigor and relevance of the research through 
systematic and rigorous design, development, and evaluation processes guided by 
established principles and methodologies. 
● Guideline 6 - Design as a Search Process: The design process involves generating 
alternatives and testing them iteratively against requirements and constraints. 
● Guideline 7 - Communication of Research: To communicate and disseminate the 
research findings, methodologies, and contributions effectively to the academic 
community, practitioners, and other stakeholders. 
In summary, DSR is fundamentally a problem -solving process, with its core principle being 
that knowledge to design a solution for a problem is acquired through creating and applying an 
artifact. This principle underpins the seven DSR guidelines that requir e developing an 
innovative, purposeful artifact tailored to a specific problem domain. As the artifact is purpose-
driven, it must provide utility for the identified problem, necessitating thorough evaluation. 
Novelty is also essential, as the artifact must  offer a new solution to an existing problem or 
address it more effectively or efficiently. This differentiates design-science research from mere 
design practice. The artifact should be rigorously defined, formally represented, coherent, and 
internally con sistent. Creating the artifact often involves a search process, constructing a 
problem space, and proposing or implementing a mechanism to find an effective solution. 
Lastly, the findings of the design -science research must be effectively communicated to b oth 
technical and managerial audiences, including researchers and practitioners, to facilitate further 
research and potential implementation in organizations (Hevner et al., 2004). 
2.3.3  Design Science Research Procedures 
In addition to the principles and guidelines, DSRM adheres to procedures, as illustrated in 
Figure 2.4 (Peffers et al., 2007). Each step, from identifying problems to communicating 
research findings, is crucial for generating knowledge and addressing significant organizational 
challenges.  

Research Methodology 
 
14 
 
 
Figure 2.4 - DSRM Process Model from Peffers et al. (2007). 
Each step of the DSRM Process Model can be described as follows (Peffers et al., 2007): 
• Step 1 - Problem identification and motivation: Gain a thorough understanding of the problem 
to ensure an effective solution. 
• Step 2 - Define the objectives for a solution: Determine the goals of the proposed solution 
based on an understanding of the problem and existing solutions. 
• Step 3 - Design and development: Develop the artifact's anticipated functionalities and 
architecture. 
• Step 4 - Demonstration: Illustrate how the artifact can solve the problem through 
experimentation, simulation, case study, or proof. 
• Step 5 - Evaluation: Assess the results to determine if the artifact has achieved its objectives. 
• Step 6 - Communication: Share the artifact and its findings with relevant audiences. 
Additionally, the DSRM Process Model from Peffers et al. (2007) includes four possible 
research entry points that offer flexibility in starting the research process based on the specific 
context or stage of the investigation, which are: 
● Problem-Centered Initiation: Start by identifying and defining a relevant problem. 
● Objective-Centered Solution: Establish the objectives for a potential solution. 
● Design and Development Centered Initiation: Create the artifact to solve the identified 
problem. 
● Client/Context Initiated: Demonstrate the artifact's effectiveness in solving the problem. 
The principles, guidelines, and procedures provide a comprehensive framework for conducting 
DSR. They synthesize the foundational concepts and methodologies proposed by prominent 
scholars in the field. 
2.3.4  Evaluation in Design Science Research 
Considering the relevance of the evaluation process for assessing the design artifact's 
effectiveness, efficiency, and utility, we adopted the Framework for Evaluation in Design 
Science (FEDS) (Venable et al., 2016) in our project.  FEDS emphasizes four key steps: 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
15 
 
1. Explanation of the Evaluation Objectives 
In this initial step, the primary goal is to clarify the purpose of the evaluation. This involves 
defining what the evaluation aims to achieve, including assessing the artifact’s utility, efficacy, 
efficiency, and overall impact. Key activities in this step include: 
● Identifying Stakeholders: Determine who will benefit from the evaluation and who 
will use the results. Stakeholders typically include researchers, practitioners, and end -
users. 
● Setting Evaluation Goals : Define specific objectives for the evaluation, such as 
validating the artifact’s functionality, assessing its performance in real -world 
conditions, or verifying its theoretical contributions. 
● Establishing Success Criteria : Develop criteria for what constitutes a successful 
evaluation. This could involve benchmarks for performance, user satisfaction, or 
alignment with theoretical expectations. 
2. Selection of Evaluation Strategies 
Once the objectives are clear, the next step is to select appropriate evaluation strategies that 
align with these objectives. This involves choosing between different types of evaluations, such 
as: 
● Formative vs. Summative: Decide whether the evaluation will be formative (iterative 
and developmental) or summative (final assessment after implementation). 
● Naturalistic vs. Artificial : Determine whether the evaluation will be conducted in a 
real-world (naturalistic) or controlled environment (artificial). 
● Ex-Ante vs. Ex-Post: Choose between evaluating the artifact’s potential before its full 
development (ex-ante) or assessing its actual performance after deployment (ex-post). 
The selection of strategies depends on factors such as the artifact’s maturity, the context in 
which it will be used, and the resources available for the evaluation. 
3. Identification of Properties to be Evaluated 
This step involves identifying the specific properties or attributes of the artifact that need to be 
evaluated to meet the established objectives. Properties may include: 
● Functional Properties : Assessing whether the artifact performs the tasks it was 
designed to do. 
● Usability: Evaluating the ease of use and user experience associated with the artifact. 
● Performance: Measuring the artifact's efficiency, speed, and resource consumption. 
● Reliability and Robustness : Ensuring the artifact consistently performs well under 
various conditions and is resilient to failures. 
● Utility and Impact: Determining the artifact’s practical value and impact on users and 
organizations. 
Identifying these properties helps to evaluate the most critical aspects of the artifact. 
 

Research Methodology 
 
16 
 
4. Design of Individual Evaluation Episodes 
The final step is to design specific evaluation episodes or activities that will be conducted to 
assess the identified properties. This involves planning the detailed execution of the evaluation, 
including: 
● Method Selection: Choosing appropriate methods for each evaluation episode, such 
as case studies, surveys, simulations, or expert reviews. 
● Data Collection: Defining the data that needs to be collected, how it will be collected, 
and the tools and instruments required for data collection. 
● Analysis Plan: Developing a plan for analyzing the collected data to derive 
meaningful insights and conclusions about the artifact’s properties. 
● Timeline and Resources: Establishing a timeline for the evaluation episodes and 
allocating the necessary resources, including personnel, tools, and budget. 
Hevner et al. (2004) stress the importance of an iterative approach to evaluation, where 
feedback loops between design and evaluation help refine the artifact until it meets the desired 
standards of utility and rigor. Additionally, Venable et al. (2016) highlight the importance of 
choosing appropriate evaluation methods based on the artifact's maturity and the research 
context. Both Venable et al. (2016) and Hevner et al. (2004) underline the necessity of 
integrating multiple evaluation methods to ensure a holistic assessment of DSR artifacts. The 
evaluation phase involves: 
As detailed by Venable et al. (2016), the FEDS approach provides a structured and systematic 
framework for evaluating artifacts in DSR. By following these four steps , researchers can 
ensure that their evaluations are thorough and relevant and contribute significantly to theoretical 
knowledge and practical applications. 
2.4  Instantiation of the Design Science Research Project 
We instantiated our DSR project by planning its six iterative phases to achieve our main 
objective: developing a framework and method to support the implementation of an engaging 
DW tailored for a multigenerational workforce. The proposed method includes d etailed 
procedures and instruments for applying the framework in a real organization. Figure 2.5 
depicts the main objective, methods applied, and outcomes of each phase, if applicable. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
17 
 
 
Figure 2.5 - DSRM Process Model. 
We performed one cycle of our DSR project with a problem-centered initiation (Peffers et al., 
2007). The "Identify Problem and Motivation" and "Define Objectives of a Solution" phases of 
our DSR project serve as the foundation for the entire research endeavor.  
2.4.1  DSR Identify Problem and Motivation 
We instantiated the first phase of our DSR project by performing SLR on DW, employee 
engagement, and generational differences to confirm the existing gaps in the research area. 
Despite numerous studies on digital work environments, a glaring oversight exis ts: a 
multigenerational workforce's diverse needs and values need to be adequately addressed 
(Gilson et al., 2015). The current situation leads to a growing issue of employee disengagement 
in a digital work environment (Gallup, 2022). This gap between rese arch and practical 
implementation pinpoints the existing body of knowledge's limitations and delineates our 
investigation's scope.  
The motivation for our study stems from the relevance of digital environments for 
organizations, the observed employees’ disengagement in these work arrangement models, and 
the existing gap in addressing the diverse needs and values of a multigenerational workforce. 
The project aims to enhance employee engagement across different generational cohorts by 
addressing that, ultimately contributing to organizational success and competitiveness in the 
digital era. 
Systematic Literature Review on Digital Workplace and Employee Engagement 
To consolidate the findings, gaps, and limitations in the existing literature related to the digital 
workplace and employee engagement, we conducted an SLR. Following the guidelines outlined 
by Webster and Watson (2002) and aligning with our research quest ion, we surveyed six 
reputable scholarly databases: EBSCO, AISeL, ScienceDirect, IEEE, ACM, and Web of 
Science, since we aimed to ensure comprehensive coverage of publications relevant to our 
study. The initial search spanned from the last week of June 2020 to the first week of July 2020, 


Research Methodology 
 
18 
 
with subsequent updates made in March 2023. Our inclusion criteria encompassed conference 
and journal papers written in English and available in PDF format, published since the year 
2000 when Jeffrey Beir first introduced the term "digital workplace" in his seminal article (Beir, 
2000). The search query we initially formulated was: 
(("digital workplace" OR "remote work" OR "future of work" OR "virtual teams") AND ("engagement" 
OR "motivation" OR "satisfaction" OR "organization" OR "organisation")) 
Initial searches on Google Scholar indicated the relevance of additional keywords such as 
"effectiveness," "millennial," "individual," and "employee" to refine our results further. These 
searches also highlighted the scarcity of papers that consider genera tional characteristics. 
Consequently, we adopted a strategy of utilizing "OR" combinations of keywords to ensure 
broader coverage. This approach allowed us to encompass various workplace configurations, 
including virtual teams, remote work, and DWs, as wel l as different constructs related to 
engagement, such as motivation, job satisfaction, and effectiveness. The final search query 
employed was: 
((“digital workplace” OR “remote work” OR “future of work” OR “virtual team”) AND (engage OR 
“motivation” OR “satisfaction” OR “effectiveness”) AND (millennial OR organization OR organisation 
OR organizational OR organisational OR individual or employee)) 
Initially focusing on the titles, abstracts, and keywords of publications, we retrieved 560 papers. 
After eliminating twenty -nine duplicate papers, two researchers independently assessed the 
remaining 531 articles based on their relevance to our research, using a classification system of 
Yes/No/Maybe. Our criteria included the presence of constructs as sociated with digital work 
environment, such as engagement or motivation, and exploration of individual characteristics 
within DW settings, such as personality traits or personal values. Through triangulation of the 
results, involving discussions on classi fication discrepancies and ambiguous findings 
("Maybes"), we excluded 470 articles that did not pertain to an organizational context, such as 
those focusing on student or nursing studies. Subsequently, the full text of the remaining 61 
articles underwent d etailed full-text analysis. Appendix A, Table A.1, details the description 
and classification of the selected papers. Figure 2.6, inspired by Mitchell & El -Gayar (2022), 
illustrates the paper selection process. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
19 
 
 
Figure 2.6 - Phases of the First Systematic Literature Review. 
Building on the outcomes of this SLR, we were able to create a work definition for DW 
(presented in Section 1.1) and identify some instruments to be applied in our artifacts, such as 
the workplace arrangement model, levels of virtuality, and enterprise int egration, barriers and 
enablers, and employee engagement scales. Additionally, we have identified several individual, 
group, and organizational characteristics that influence employees’ engagement in the digital 
work environment.  
2.4.2  DSR Define Objectives of a Solution 
Given the identified problem and the existing gap in research and implementation, the objective 
was to design and develop a comprehensive framework and method for supporting 
organizations in implementing an engaging DW tailored to the needs and preferences of a 
multigenerational workforce. The proposed solution aims to enable continuous improvement 
of the digital work environment to enhance engagement and  establish an ongoing evaluation 
and enhancement based on feedback, metrics, and evolving workforce dynamics. It integrates 
mechanisms for adapting and updating the DW to align with the changing needs and 
preferences. 
Furthermore, we used behavioral theories during this phase to design, develop, implement, and 
evaluate the artifact (Hevner et al., 2004). We opted to adopt the ANT as our theoretical lens, 
aiming to shed light on how technology influences social interacti ons within the intricate 
realities of organizations (Cresswell, 2010). ANT allows us to analyze the interactions between 
human and non -human actors within their contexts (Latour, 2005), focusing on establishing 
networks and interventions within these interactions (Carroll et al., 2012). 
Systematic Literature Review on Generational Traits 
In our first SLR, we identified a mere six papers that delved into the distinct characteristics of 
different generations. Moreover, there is a paucity of understanding regarding the generational 
impacts within a digital work environment (Großer & Baumöl, 2 017). The younger cohorts, 
often referred to as "Digital Natives" (Larkin, 2017), may perceive digital work environments 
as natural, considering traditional office settings (face -to-face) as exceptions (Gilson et al., 


Research Methodology 
 
20 
 
2015). According to Westover et al. (2020), Generation X is uncomfortable learning new 
technologies. In contrast, Generation Y is the first cohort to have grown up with Internet access 
(Bencsik et al., 2016), and Generation Z is the first to have been raised in a globally connected 
world (Bencsik et al., 2016). 
To elucidate the poorly systematized differences among distinct generations regarding their 
attitudes toward work, we conducted a second SLR. Our search expression was: 
(("generation x" OR "generation y" OR "generation z") AND (work)) 
By incorporating the keyword "work" into the search expression, we aimed to retrieve papers 
discussing topics such as "work values," "work satisfaction," and "workforce." We selected the 
EBSCO database to ensure comprehensive and multidisciplinary coverage of publications. The 
inclusion criteria were academic papers written in English, available in PDF format, and 
published since 2000, consistent with the cut -off date used in the previous SLR. Our search, 
confined to titles, abstracts, and keywords, yielded  437 hits. We eliminated 346 articles 
unrelated to the workplace, such as those focusing on tourism aspects for each generation, 
differences in mobile service usage among generations, and immigrants' engagement in 
relocation. Additionally, six duplicate pa pers were discarded. Subsequently, we analyzed the 
titles and abstracts of the remaining 85 articles, excluding 66 papers that used the search terms 
within specific contexts, such as retirement, teenagers, and consumer behavior. Finally, the full 
texts of the remaining 19 articles underwent detailed analysis. Figure 2.7, inspired by Mitchell 
& El-Gayar (2022), illustrates the paper selection process. 
 
Figure 2.7 - Phases of the Second Systematic Literature Review. 
Despite the few selected papers, they offer broad coverage, encompassing studies from seven 
disciplines. Additionally, these papers cover various organizational sectors, including industry 
and services, and represent multiple countries. Approximately 65% of the studies examined two 
or three generations (X, Y, or Z), while only four (around 20%) specifically addressed 
Generation Z. This lower number was anticipated, as Generation Z has only recently entered 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
21 
 
the workforce. Appendix B, Table B.1, provides a detailed description and classification of the 
selected papers. 
Building on the outcomes of this SLR, we were able to establish the differences among 
generations regarding their attitudes toward work and their interests, values, and needs. 
Additionally, we were able to consolidate a backlog with best practices already validated in the 
literature that aim to mitigate the existing barriers and promote the enablers in a DW. This 
comprehensive analysis contributes to a deeper understanding of how generational differences 
shape perceptions and interactions within the workplace. 
2.5  Conclusion 
This chapter presented the methodological approach applied in our research. By integrating 
theoretical insights, empirical findings, and practical applications, we aim to support 
organizations in fostering employee engagement and navigating the complexitie s of digital 
transformation. In selecting our research methodology, we discussed between AR and DSR. 
Ultimately, we chose DSR because it focuses on developing and testing artifacts to address 
specific challenges, aligning with our goal of creating a methodological framework and method 
for implementing DW. Furthermore, our adoption of A NT provided a theoretical lens to 
comprehend the intricate interactions between human and non -human actors within the DW. 
ANT facilitated the identification of actor relationships, network dynamics, and conflicts of 
interest, shedding light on the complexities of technology-mediated social interactions. 
The first two phases of our DSR project provided essential groundwork for our research, 
identifying existing literature gaps and defining the objectives of our proposed solution . This 
solution aims to develop a framework and method that assists organizations in creating an 
engaging digital workplace tailored to the needs of different generations. 
As illustrated in Figure 2.8, building on the gaps and opportunities identified in the first two 
phases of our DSR project and considering the relevance of a digital environment for 
organizations, our research question is: 
RQ: How can business services organizations implement engaging digital workplaces tailored 
for a multigenerational workforce?
