Chapter 3  
State of the Art 
This chapter lays the groundwork for our study, encompassing the dimensions of the DW, 
employee engagement, and generational traits. We aim to unfold the different workplace 
arrangement models, particularly emphasizing the DW. We then provide an in -depth 
understanding of the engagement phenomenon and its pivotal role in successfully implementing 
workplaces tailored to accommodate a multigenerational workforce. We delve into the 
theoretical underpinnings of the DW, the influence of generational traits, and the critical aspect 
of employee engagement. We dissect the success predictors of the DW, placing significant 
emphasis on trust, leadership, collaboration, knowledge sharing, autonomy, and 
communication. Furthermore, we delve into the subtle distinctions bet ween face-to-face and 
virtual approaches and explore the inherent barriers and enablers within the digital workspace 
paradigm. 
The impact of generational traits on work dynamics is considered, wherein we identify the 
distinct characteristics of Generations X, Y, and Z. Drawing from a theoretical framework, we 
unravel the similarities and differences across generations, shedding light on their implications 
for workplace design and employee engagement. Section 3.3 examines the contributors to 
employee engagement and their consequential outcomes. Additionally, we evaluate two 
existing measurement tools to gauge employee engagement levels. Section 3.4 consolidates the 
prior artifacts identified in the literature developed to guide the design and implementation of 
engaging DWs. 
Finally, we consolidate the existing gaps in the literature, underscoring the imperative for 
interdisciplinary approaches to address the several factors that influence engagement in DWs 
of a multigenerational workforce. This chapter is foundational for und erstanding the 
complexities of designing engaging DWs for a multigenerational workforce. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

State of the Art 
 
24 
 
3.1  Digital Workplace 
In 1973, a NASA engineer, Jack Nilles, coined the term 'telecommuting' as a solution to urban 
traffic congestion and resource conservation (Nilles, 1988). Six years later, IBM experimented 
with five employees working from home (Casey, 2019). With the advent of the Internet in 1983, 
IBM's remote workforce grew to 2000 employees, and academics also began exploring online 
conferencing (Byrd, 2021). In 2003, Skype revolutionized remote communications with its 
video calling service (Megalapete et al., 2018), and the first coworking space emerged in 2005 
(Grinberg, 2023). While 60% of jobs offered in Silicon Valley included the option to work from 
home by 2017 (Viskovic, 2021), European statistics in 2019 showed that, on average, only 5.4% 
of employed individuals in the EU regularly worked from home (Eurostat, 2020). 
However, the landscape changed dramatically in 2020 when the COVID -19 pandemic led to 
the widespread adoption of remote work. These developments laid the groundwork for 
transitioning from the traditional office setting to the flexibility of working from anywhere.  
The DW can bring numerous benefits to organizations, encompassing cost reductions, cycle -
time reductions, integration of distant members, and improved decision -making and problem-
solving skills (Graham et al., 2016). Introducing communication tools and tec hnologies 
mitigates time and space barriers and fosters efficiency, flexibility, connectivity, and task -
focused project management (Park et al., 2015). Virtual teams facilitate access to the most 
qualified individuals irrespective of location, enabling swi ft responses to competition and 
providing flexibility for remote work. Moreover, commuting time savings contribute to reduced 
carbon emissions (Vaidyanathan & Debrot, 2010). Global virtual teams offer diverse 
perspectives, knowledge heterogeneity, and cros s-learning potential, enhancing creativity and 
innovation (Florea & Stoica, 2019). These teams also allow organizations to tap into a broader 
pool of skills, reduce development time, adapt better to changing market demands, and achieve 
economies of scale (Mockaitis et al., 2012). Digital work increases overall organizational agility 
and responsiveness (Derven, 2016). From the employees' perspective, the benefits include 
personal convenience (Bader & Kaiser, 2017), work-life balance, empowerment (Attaran et al., 
2019), time saved on commuting (Eurofound, 2018), and autonomy (Bader & Kaiser, 2017; 
Larkin, 2017). 
Specific authors have highlighted the paradoxical impacts of digitalization: while it is 
anticipated to lower operational costs and drive revenue growth, particularly in small and 
medium-sized enterprises, it may necessitate significant upfront investments  in hardware or 
software that financial gains might not readily offset (Fajardo et al., 2016; Attaran et al., 2019). 
Additionally, employees sometimes view remote work as more stressful, as they feel compelled 
to be available throughout the day, blurring t he boundaries between work and personal time 
(Köffer, 2015; Attaran et al., 2019).  Digital work can significantly impact aspects of culture 
and family background, potentially leading to conflicts and increased stress and depression. 
Specifically, drawback s such as employee isolation, the challenge of maintaining regular 
interpersonal interactions with colleagues, and difficulties in organizing union activities have 
been highlighted (Beňo, 2021). Moreover, the increased workload, frequent interruptions, 
feelings of isolation, and heightened stress levels associated with digital work can exacerbate 
problems in family and personal life (Ferreira et al., 2021). Despite these challenges, previous 
research suggests that the benefits of digital work often outweigh the drawbacks (Beňo, 2021). 
The existing literature discusses various models of work arrangements, including the physical 
workplace, virtual workplace, remote work, and DW. The physical workplace denotes the 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
25 
 
conventional office setup where employees share a physical space (Mukherjee et al., 2012). 
Remote work is when employees perform their duties away from their main office at a different 
location (Perry et al., 2018). The virtual workplace involves dispersed individuals collaborating 
through electronic technologies to achieve specific objectives (Shin, 2004). The DW 
encompasses all the information, technologies, tools, and processes employees utilize in their 
work environment (Attaran et al., 2019). It facilitates a personalized, real-time, mobile-enabled, 
and collaboratively driven work experience (Attaran et al., 2019). These four models share a 
common aspect of virtuality, each having different levels. Scholars have identified virtuality as 
a characteristic that distinguishes virtual teams from face-to-face teams (Ferretti, 2016).  
3.1.1  Interaction Approach (face-to-face vs. virtual) 
Two decades ago, face -to-face work was considered preferable to virtual work due to the 
perceived difficulties in developing shared understanding, maintaining trust, transferring 
organizational culture, and fostering information sharing among group members  in virtual 
environments (Chudoba et al., 2005). Co -located workers experience a higher level of 
information exchange than remote workers, as remote workers need a more informal support 
context and have limited access to informal communication (Deshpande et al., 2016). Some 
authors identified process losses in a virtual environment compared to face -to-face teams due 
to increased complexity as virtuality increases (Schmidtke & Cummings, 2017). Other authors 
indicate that virtual teams can achieve significant process gains despite the difficulties in 
communication, coordination, cohesion, and trust (Florea & Stoica, 2019). D espite these 
challenges, virtual teams have the potential to facilitate knowledge sharing in multinational and 
geographically dispersed organizations (Kauppila, 2011). Furthermore, global virtual teams can 
generate more unique ideas due to their diverse pe rspectives and experiences (Schmidtke & 
Cummings, 2017). 
Building trust and effective collaboration among team members are cited as aspects that are 
more challenging in a virtual approach as compared to face -to-face interactions (Chudoba et 
al., 2005; Furumo & Pearson, 2007; Neufeld et al., 2010; Pinjani & Palvi a, 2013; Hassell & 
Cotton, 2017; Florea & Stoica, 2019).  Face-to-face interactions are often considered preferable 
for making prompt decisions (Schmidtke & Cummings, 2017), managing conflicts (Hassell & 
Cotton, 2017), and cultivating a more cohesive team (Neufeld et al., 2010). This approach is 
also more effective for disseminating corporate culture and facilitating the swift sharing of 
information (Chudoba et al., 2005). Face -to-face interactions provide greater access to 
information, particularly informal communication (Deshpande et al., 2016). Challenges such as 
physical dispersion and time zone differences can make the flow of information difficult in 
virtual teams (Hassell & Cotton, 2017; Schmidtke & Cummings, 2017). With physical 
proximity, team members can avoid synchronous information and communication technologies 
(ICTs), such as telephones or shared applications, as substitutes for face -to-face interactions 
(Montoya et al., 2009). It is essential to foster face -to-face interactions on certain occasions to 
build trust, such as team formation and conflict management (Kauppila et al., 2011; Adamovic, 
2018; Stratone, 2019). 
As organizations embrace virtuality and adopt digital technologies, the number of systems, 
applications, and platforms they use increases. These disparate systems may include 
communication tools, project management software, customer relationship managemen t 
systems, and more (Attaran et al., 2019). Existing research highlights a standard toolkit used in 
the DW that encompasses a laptop, Internet connectivity, and communication tools like email, 

State of the Art 
 
26 
 
videoconferencing, mobile devices, chat platforms, and blogging (Fajardo et al., 2016; 
Eurofound, 2018; Attaran et al., 2019; Bick et al., 2020). While many of these tools are also 
employed in traditional physical workplaces, their relevance and usage are often adapted for 
virtual work scenarios. Additionally, some scholars highlight emerging opportunities stemming 
from technological advancements, such as augmented reality (Bertrand et al., 2018), Artificial 
Intelligence (AI) (Attaran et al., 2019), and immersive virtual environments utilizing webcams, 
broadband, shared repositories, and flat-screen 3D graphics (Hofma et al., 2017). 
3.1.2  Theoretical Background on Digital Workplace 
The practical implementation of a DW hinges on adopting appropriate strategies and the 
capacity to acclimate to new digital work techniques (Buchanan et al., 2016; Hamburg, 2019) 
and prioritizing employees' experience. To gain acceptance, the DW must fulfi ll employees' 
needs, such as autonomy and flexibility (Shin, 2004). This underscores the need for 
organizations to adopt a human -centered approach while designing the new digital work 
environment (Buchanan et al., 2016; Tavanapour et al., 2019). Culture an d engagement are 
cited by 87% of organizations as two of their significant challenges (Buchanan et al., 2016). A 
recent Gallup study indicates that a mere 23% of employees across the globe are actively 
engaged in their work (Gallup, 2023). A DW also involv es redesigning work activities and 
processes and using adequate tools for the active participation of employees (Attaran et al., 
2019). Due to the relevance of both technical and human aspects of DW implementations, 
existing studies often combine informati on-processing theories with psychological, social, or 
communication theories (Walvoord et al., 2008; O’Neill et al., 2014; Shaik & Makhecha, 2019). 
The existing studies grounded on information processing theories often narrow down Media 
Richness (MRT), Media synchronicity (MST), channel expansion, and cognitive load theories 
as extensions of information processing theory, which studies how the mind an alyzes 
information from the environment (Walvoord et al., 2008). In 1986, Daft and Lengel presented 
MRT as a framework to evaluate the richness of communication media, such as phone calls, 
video conferencing, and email. Ngwenyama and Lee (1997) demonstrate  the influence of 
culture and social background on media choice. Some authors combine MRT with Social 
Presence Theory to explore how digital technologies can influence the sense of “presence” of 
the other person (Walvoord et al., 2008). Media Synchronicity Theory (MST) was proposed to 
explain MRT better. The MST provides a general understanding of the communication 
processes and the fit between media capabilities to support a specific task (Wang & Carte, 
2018). Furthermore, the Multi-Motive IS Continuance Model (MISC) focuses on understanding 
individuals' motivation to continue using information systems, particularly in collaborative 
contexts. This model acknowledges that individuals are driven by multiple motives or needs 
that influence their decision to use a system (Tavanapour et al., 2019). For instance, the attitude 
toward technology can be understood using the Technology Acceptance Model (TAM). It offers 
a framework for identifying the factors that drive the acceptance of technology, which is a 
crucial determinant of one's intention to use it (Elias et al., 2012). TAM builds upon the Theory 
of Reasoned Action, which suggests that behavioral intentions, influenced by attitudes, directly 
predict behavior (Elias et al., 2012).  
The application of the MST is particularly relevant in designing collaboration systems. It aligns 
with the fundamental elements of collaboration, encompassing communication, cooperation, 
coordination, and pursuing a common goal. MST underscores the signifi cance of considering 
task and technological aspects when individuals subjectively evaluate fit and express 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
27 
 
preferences for specific technologies. It emphasizes that technology preference should stem 
from a comprehensive understanding of the task that the technology supports (Nguyen et al., 
2021). 
During our literature review, we identified some studies incorporating insights from social 
theories, such as Social Exchange Theory, which provides a valuable lens through which we 
can understand how digital tools and social interactions intertwine to inf luence employee 
engagement within an organization (Robert & You, 2018). Furthermore, Reasoned Action 
(Park et al., 2015) and Personality -Job fit (O’Neill et al., 2014) theories underscore the 
significance of an individual's personality, attitudes, and beha viors in comprehending their 
decision to engage in a particular endeavor. Selimović et al. (2021) emphasize that “employees’ 
attitudes and perceptions regarding new technologies do not imply the classic question of 
adoption, but their expectancies towards a future working environment.” 
3.1.3  Success Predictors of the Digital Workplace 
Researchers frequently focus on specific predictors to explore individual performance, turnover 
intentions, job satisfaction, and other outcomes in a DW. These predictors offer insights into 
employee behaviors, interactions, and outcomes within digital env ironments, including trust, 
leadership, collaboration, knowledge sharing, autonomy, and communication (Shachaf, 2008). 
Some authors have investigated the interplay of these success predictors, shedding light on their 
mutual influence. For instance, Alsharo  et al. (2017) delved into the positive impact of 
knowledge sharing on trust and collaboration among virtual team members. Furthermore, 
scholars have noted the reciprocal relationship between trust and collaboration, which affects 
knowledge sharing, demons trating a solid interdependence among these factors (Lin et al., 
2010; Pinjani & Palvia, 2013). Autonomy has also been identified as a precursor to trust (Shin, 
2004). Additionally, leadership and workplace climate have been recognized as crucial 
elements in mitigating challenges within the digital workspace (Cordery & Soo, 2008), where 
effective communication within teams is influenced by leadership (Neufeld et al., 2010). Other 
studies highlight the influence of cultural diversity (Schachaf, 2008) and com puter literacy or 
domain knowledge (Shin, 2004) as positive precursors in a digital environment. For our 
investigation, we have considered the six precursors: trust, leadership, collaboration, 
knowledge sharing, autonomy, and communication. The following subsections provide detailed 
information on each one of the success predictors in the DW context. 
Trust 
Trust is crucial in digital or virtual settings (Kowalski & Swanson, 2005). It is a foundation for 
successful teamwork, nurturing cooperation, and mutual respect among team members (Robert 
& You, 2018). Its development is particularly challenging in global  virtual teams due to the 
absence of a shared social context, often resulting in a fragile and depersonalized form of trust 
that necessitates continuous facilitation and reinforcement (Mukherjee et al., 2012; Florea & 
Stoica, 2019). However, the absence of face-to-face interaction and the transient nature of many 
digital team arrangements present significant obstacles to trust development (Lippert & 
Dulewicz, 2018). Despite its challenges, trust is crucial for knowledge sharing and conflict 
resolution among team members (Furumo & Pearson, 2007; Mukherjee et al., 2012). Trust in 
virtual teams relies heavily on cognition -based factors, such as perceived evidence of 
trustworthiness, as opposed to affect -based elements (Schmidtke & Cummings, 2017). 
Furthermore, it is often established through actions rather than goodwill, necessitating clear and 
explicit communication (Mukherjee et al., 2012). Trust -building efforts should prioritize 

State of the Art 
 
28 
 
actions that promote mutual understanding and confidence among team members (Breuer et al., 
2016). Trust is the foundation for organizational success in digital environments (Mockaitis et 
al., 2012; Florea & Stoica, 2019). It is the main drive of effective leadership, influencing team 
dynamics and performance (Panteli et al., 2019). It also grows through reliability, consistency, 
and responsiveness rather than interpersonal interactions (Kirkman et al., 2002). 
Collaboration  
The active assistance team members provide to one another in their tasks (Vaidyanathan & 
Debrot, 2010). New information-sharing capabilities have fostered collaboration within digital 
and virtual teams, although challenges persist compared to face -to-face interaction (Florea & 
Stoica, 2019). Over the past decade, various communication and collaboration tools  have 
proliferated, including cloud software for document sharing, meeting software, project 
management software, and corporate social networking platforms , offering teams diverse 
means to collaborate (Adamovic, 2018). Despite the growing array of computer -mediated 
communication (CMC) tools, research predominantly focuses on conventional CMC tools, 
highlighting a gap between practice and research (Gilson et al., 2015). Additionally, high 
personnel turnover challenges effective collaboration (Vaidyanathan & Debrot, 2010). 
Knowledge Sharing 
Organizations grapple with the persistent tension between leveraging Information and 
Communication Technologies (ICTs) to codify and disseminate explicit knowledge, 
exemplified by Vaccaro et al. (2009), and embracing traditional methods like face -to-face 
interactions and communities of practice to nurture the development and transmission of tacit 
knowledge. This dynamic contradicts earlier theoretical studies such as Vaccaro et al. (2009), 
which underscore ICTs' potential to engender a virtual realm for sha ring and generating new 
knowledge at both implicit and explicit levels. Implicit or tacit knowledge is pivotal in fostering 
successful innovations, aiding strategic decision -making, and facilitating sharing 
geographically dispersed groups through virtual technologies. Meanwhile, the cooperative and 
competitive nature of knowledge sharing among team members, as highlighted by Lin et al. 
(2010), underscores the intricate interplay between individual incentives and collective goals. 
These dynamics are further influenced by factors such as the perceived value of shared 
knowledge (Lin et al., 2010) and the challenges inherent in international working environments, 
including time differences and cultural barriers. A midst these complexities, effectively 
managed team knowledge, as emphasized by various scholars, emerges as a linchpin for project 
success, suggesting the criticality of cohesive social ties and trust within organizations (Pinjani 
& Palvia, 2013). In this multifaceted landscape, the continuous recreation and reconstitution of 
knowledge within digital environments (Malhotra et al., 2007) become paramount, alongside 
the recognition of knowledge as a communal process occurring within social interactions 
(Kauppila et al., 2011). Thus, navigating the intricacies of knowledge sharing necessitates a 
nuanced understanding of contextual factors and adopting hybrid approaches that synergize 
ICTs with traditional practices to foster innovation and organizational learning (Vaccaro et al., 
2009). 
Communication 
Effective communication is essential for success in a DW (Großer & Baumöl, 2017). Achieving 
effective and efficient communication involves selecting media that match task demands 
(Ruppel et al., 2013) and establishing standards and routines for communication methods, tools, 
and frequencies (Großer & Baumöl, 2017). Non -co-located teams must utilize diverse 
communication modes, including asynchronous and synchronous methods (Deshpande et al., 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
29 
 
2016). Teams tend to establish their unique communication patterns, emphasizing the 
importance of setting rules and self -regulation strategies to mitigate the negative impact of 
instant messaging on productivity (Ruppel et al., 2013). Understanding differe nt cultural 
communication styles is relevant for facilitating task -level coordination (Lippert & Dulewicz, 
2018). Adaptation to communication behavior and language proficiency is necessary for 
multicultural teams, with frequent and in-depth interactive communication and shared identity 
development enhancing perceptions of proximity and relationship quality (Schulze & Krumm, 
2017). Enforced communication practices, such as those in Agile development, can mitigate 
challenges for distributed teams, reducing temporal, geographical, and socio-cultural distances 
(Deshpande et al., 2016). Training in communication skills can also enhance digital team 
performance (Schmidtke & Cummings, 2017). Frequent and spontaneous communication is 
vital for handling conflict, inc reasing visibility, maintaining trust, enhancing shared 
understanding, and facilitating information processes in temporally dispersed teams (Schulze 
& Krumm, 2017). Whenever possible, face -to-face interaction aids in bridging cultural 
boundaries and promot ing communication (Ruppel et al., 2013). With sufficient time for 
relationship building and adaptation to communication mediums, digital teams can 
communicate as effectively as face-to-face groups (Vaidyanathan & Debrot, 2010). 
Leadership 
Leaders assume a critical role in digital teams, where discontinuities abound. They are often 
described as boundary spanners, bridge makers, and blenders, pivotal in fostering motivation 
and commitment among dispersed team members (Panteli et al., 2019). Leadership practices in 
this domain have garnered signif icant attention, with scholars highlighting the multifaceted 
nature of effective leadership in a DW context. For instance, successful leaders in this work 
arrangement exhibit diverse behavioral repertoires, from mentoring and fostering care among 
members to ensuring clarity in communication and asserting authority (Panteli et al., 2019). 
Besides possessing technological proficiency, a leader in a digital environment needs cross -
cultural management skills and the ability to foster trust and collaboration (St ratone, 2019). 
Additionally, shared leadership is a powerful mechanism for promoting satisfaction, trust, and 
collaboration within digital teams (Robert & You, 2018). The evolution of traditional leadership 
roles in digital settings necessitates an adaptive function termed "e-leadership," wherein leaders 
execute their roles through virtual communication, emphasizing knowledge management, trust-
building, and maintaining influence (Walvoord et al., 2008). Effective e -leadership hinges on 
cultivating a solid presence and facilitating meaningful interactions (Walvoord et al., 2008). As 
digital teams increasingly become the norm, the role of leaders in promoting cohesion, 
managing cultural diversity, and leveraging technology for effective communication becomes 
paramount. Through inclusive leadership practices, leaders can harness diversity, foster mutual 
respect, and create an environment conducive to innovation and collaboration (Derven, 2016). 
Autonomy 
Autonomy, the extent to which individuals possess freedom, independence, and discretion in 
executing their tasks, holds significant importance in work dynamics (Robert & You, 2018). 
This attribute is widely acknowledged as a valuable resource for individua ls, with scholars 
emphasizing its critical role in the success of digital work setups, as it directly contributes to 
employee well-being (Perry et al., 2018). While digital workers may perceive themselves as 
having increased autonomy due to the flexibility  in choosing their work location, it is 
noteworthy that their autonomy regarding task execution methods might still be limited (Perry 
et al., 2018). In digital organizations where traditional managerial oversight is minimal, 

State of the Art 
 
30 
 
autonomy emerges as a core value, with employees expected to be self -motivated and self -
directed in defining their goals, work schedules, methodologies, and outputs (Shin, 2004). 
3.1.4  Barriers and Enablers in a Digital Workplace 
Previous research has shown that achieving success in the DW requires overcoming various 
barriers from both organizational and employee perspectives. Organizational challenges 
include investment costs, user resistance (Bertrand et al., 2018), concerns from  trade unions 
(Byhovskaya, 2018), security issues (Buchanan et al., 2016; Attaran et al., 2019; Deloitte, 
2019), regulatory compliance, internal governance (Buchanan et al., 2016), and centralized 
processes and structures (Attaran et al., 2019). The increa singly virtual nature of work 
introduces new challenges for employees, such as strained team relationships (Westover et al., 
2020), miscommunication, and role overload (Bejtkovsky, 2016). Additionally, factors like 
digital literacy (Marsh, 2018), Internet access (Wiesche et al., 2019), burnout (Buchanan et al., 
2016; Bader & Kaiser, 2017), distractions (Bader & Kaiser, 2017), inadequate physical 
workspace (Bick et al., 2020), and adherence to digital etiquette further complicate matters 
(Buchanan et al., 2016). 
Moreover, studies conducted during the COVID -19 pandemic have highlighted additional 
barriers employees face in extreme circumstances, such as anxiety and social isolation in remote 
work setups (Prasad et al., 2020). Issues like burnout, difficulty prioritizing work, interruptions, 
overworking, loneliness, and poor health habits have also been reported (Prasad et al., 2020). 
A survey involving over 2,000 individuals working from home during the outbreak found that 
employees worked an additional 28 hours per month on average, increasing the risk of burnout 
(Linkedin, 2020). 
On the other hand, the literature also identifies relevant enablers for fostering a thriving DW. 
These include effective governance and processes (Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019), clear roles 
and responsibilities (Bejtkovsky, 2016), explicit and measurable goa ls (Bencsik et al., 2016), 
and mechanisms for rewards, recognition (Pouchová, 2011; Bano et al., 2015), and feedback 
(Kaliannan et al., 2015; Bencsik, 2016). A robust and inclusive organizational culture 
(Kaliannan et al., 2015; Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019) , marked by effective communication 
(Kaliannan et al., 2015), alignment with organizational values and mission (Mukherjee et al., 
2012), and frequent communication (Westover et al., 2020), is also essential. Furthermore, 
access to information, appropriate tools for job functions, and the ability to anticipate work -
related tasks are identified as critical facilitators (Barford & Hester, 2011). 
Some authors suggest that limited IT experience can be a barrier when employees are not 
proficient with the required tools (Schmidt et al., 2018; Attaran et al., 2019). Additionally, 
Gilson et al. (2015) propose that investigating the behaviors of differen t generations, 
particularly in communication, knowledge sharing, and collaboration, could offer insights into 
addressing perceived barriers that arise from digital work environments. 
 
 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
31 
 
3.2  Generational Traits 
A generation is defined as the collective of individuals born within a similar timeframe or life 
cycle who share a familiar historical context, thereby forming a shared collective identity (Jonck 
et al., 2017). Usually, each generation covers 20 -25 years ( Cseh-Papp et al., 2017).  Various 
authors declare that they used the age cohort proposed by the Pew Research Institute due to its 
commitment to measuring public attitudes for decades (Khan et al., 2021). Table 3.1 displays 
an adapted comparison from Bejtko vsky (2016) of distinct labels assigned to different 
generations and the diverse chronological schemes utilized to categorize individuals born in 
specific years into a generation, as outlined by the sources listed in the first column.

State of the Art 
 
32 
 
Viewing a generation as a cohort enables us to identify shared values and behaviors in social 
and organizational contexts (Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019). Birthday cohorts emerge from 
generational cohort theory, which suggests that generations differ because of birth years, life 
experiences, values, and social and historical life events, such as wars, economic fluctuations, 
disasters, and technological advances (Martin & Gentry, 2011). Besides chronological age, 
previous studies consider the current personal stage and social context (e.g., cultural context 
and education) (Gladwell, 2010; Whitehouse, 2017) also influence generational characteristics. 
Some authors warn that "[c]aution is given to stereotyping individuals based on generational 
values and characteristics" (Barford & Hester, 2011). Authors commonly agree that not only 
the age cohort defines a generation (Chatrakul & Smithson, 2016; Jonck et al., 2017). Table 3.2 
shows each other's distinct values and behaviors adapted from Bejtkovsky (2016) with 
additional references in some cells. Individuals born at one end of the date range or another 
may see overlapping characteristics with the preceding or succeeding generation. We 
maintained the Veterans and Baby Boomers' information in Table 3.2 to frame the generations 
in the scope of our study (Gens X, Y, and Z).

Design Engaging Digital Workplace

Generally, Gen X is the resilient generation (Westover et al., 2020), Gen Y is self-confident 
(Jonck et al., 2017), and Gen Z is more individualistic (Bencsik et al., 2016). Furthermore, the 
preferences for benefits may be influenced by factors such as age and gender (Gladwell et al., 
2010). Research suggests that job values evolve s ignificantly during young adulthood but 
stabilize as individuals age. While extrinsic work values (e.g., salary and benefits) typically 
decline, intrinsic values (e.g., meaningful work, learning, and growth) remain relatively stable. 
This evolution in work  values reflects changes during early adulthood and may also be 
influenced by transitions into adult roles such as marriage and parenthood (Krahn & Galambos, 
2014). Table 3.3 consolidates some identified challenges in light of generational differences 
(Bencsik et al., 2016).

Generational differences significantly influence workplace dynamics, and organizations often 
attract employees whose values align with their own (Mukherjee et al., 2012). The social 
environment should cater to individuals' fundamental psychological needs, facilitating personal 
growth, achievement, and overall well -being. Meeting career -related needs is vital for 
subjective career success (Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019). To effectively align employees' 
motivation with the organization's objectives, Talent Management (TM) emerges as a strategic 

State of the Art 
 
36 
 
framework. It aims to synchronize human resource processes with organizational goals, 
explicitly focusing on recognizing and cultivating talent (Kaliannan et al., 2015). Additionally, 
the value reinforcement theory posits that individuals tend to harmonize  their values with 
perceived or anticipated rewards, such as those associated with work and parenting roles (Krahn 
& Galambos, 2014). Understanding generational impacts in a DW environment still needs to 
be explored (Gilson et al., 2015). The following sub sections provide detailed information 
regarding the values and needs of the three generations representing most of the global 
workforce. 
3.2.1  Gens X, Y, and Z in Essence 
Gen X values financial stability and community leadership (Jonck et al., 2017). Organizations 
should offer tangible and immediate rewards, flexible work arrangements, skill development, 
and a positive work environment to attract and retain this generation (Gladwell et al., 2010). 
Gen X employees may have good performance and collaboration skills. However, they need 
help to achieve the organization's mission (Bejtkovsky, 2016). Some authors argue that 
"[g]eneration X could have led to lower work commitment and more value placed on extrinsic 
rather than intrinsic work rewards " (Krahn & Galambos, 2014). Also, they show less 
motivation than younger generations to develop new technological skills (Westover et al., 
2020). 
The self-confidence of Gen Y facilitates them becoming leaders at a young age (Jonck et al., 
2017). Millennials do not expect “just a job” (PWC, 2011). They tend to seek organizations 
where they can achieve their goals (Bano et al., 2015). The organization's reputation and image 
are relevant factors (Civelek et al., 2017). Organizations should show a forward -looking 
business strategy to attract and retain this generation (Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019) and be 
highly technology-oriented (Pouchová, 2011). Also, to meet their expectations, organizations 
should provide a meaningful (Jonck et al., 2017) and fun work environment where they can feel 
their contribution is rewarded (Gladwell et al., 2010). Instead of being loyal to a single 
company, Gen Ys are usually open to opportunities in the labor market (Koekemoer & Crafford, 
2019). They prefer flexible working arrangements, nonhierarchical organizational structures, 
and flexible office rules (Civelek et al., 2017). They tend to question the authority and the 
"status quo" (Westover et al., 2020). 
Gen Z is known as the first global generation. This influences their learning and relationship 
with digital technologies (Cseh -Papp et al., 2017). They are agile, impatient, and welcome 
continuous change (Bencsik et al., 2016). Gen Z uses the Internet as a  knowledge source to 
learn, find solutions, and solve problems (Bencsik et al., 2016). They are more individualistic 
and try to achieve their goals alone (Bencsik et al., 2016). To attract and retain this generation, 
organizations should have a social resp onsibility, a sustainable strategy (Cseh -Papp et al., 
2017), and efficient communication (Bencsik et al., 2016). Some authors argue that Gen Z lacks 
interpersonal skills, such as communication and relationships with other individuals 
(Bejtkovsky, 2016). Since Generation Z just entered the labor market, there are fewer studies 
about them in the work environment, and some are hypothetical (Cseh-Papp et al., 2017). 
3.2.2  Generational Similarities and Differences 
Despite the differences in some values, previous studies show that the three generations (X, Y, 
and Z) are motivated by flexible work arrangements and work -life balance (Gladwell et al., 
2010; Bencsik et al., 2016). Some authors argue that younger generati ons prefer extrinsic 
rewards, such as money or additional vacations, to intrinsic rewards (Bano et al., 2015). Others 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
37 
 
say that Gen X values tangible and immediate rewards (Gladwell et al., 2010), Gen Y values 
teamwork, and Gen Z prefers independent work, especially in a virtual environment (Larkin, 
2017). Some studies cite Gens Y and Z as distrustful and needing help work ing in teams 
(Civelek et al., 2017). Gen X is not comfortable learning new technologies, while Gen Y is the 
first to grow up having access to the Internet (Bencsik et al., 2016), and Gen Z is the first to 
grow up in a connected global world (Bencsik et al. , 2016). Hence, Gen Y and Z differ from 
previous generations in how technology is an embedded knowledge in daily activities (Barford 
& Hester, 2011). Gen Y and Gen Z are more likely than Gen X to leave their jobs when 
unsatisfied and disagree with the orga nizational practices or values (Larkin, 2017). However, 
Gen Z is more concerned with unemployment and career development than previous 
generations (Bencsik et al., 2016). Gen X leaders struggle to manage Gen Y employees due to 
the different values and behaviors in the workplace (Kaliannan et al., 2015).  
Managing the diverse values and behaviors of different generations within the workplace 
presents a significant challenge for effective leadership (Kaliannan et al., 2015). Understanding 
and navigating these differences is crucial for cultivating a dynamic digital work environment 
(Larkin, 2017). Customizing benefits packages to align with each employee's unique needs and 
preferences, regardless of age, is essential. What may suit a 55-year-old employee might not be 
suitable for a 25 -year-old (Gladwell et al ., 2010). Incorporating financial and non -financial 
benefits is advantageous (Kaliannan et al., 2015). 
Authors have provided examples of benefits tailored to specific generations; for instance, 
Generation X valued parental leave a decade ago, whereas travel reimbursement and employee 
assistance programs were relevant for Generation Y (Gladwell et al., 2010) . Additionally, 
Generation Y appreciates a fun work environment (Civelek et al., 2017) and community 
engagement programs that allow employees to assist others (Westover et al., 2020).  
Generation Z values diverse tasks and support for career development (Cseh-Papp et al., 2017). 
Moreover, organizations can create programs that capitalize on the strengths of each generation, 
thereby enhancing engagement across various age groups (Bano et al., 2015). In today's 
globalized and competitive societies, retaining a skilled workforce is paramount for maintaining 
a company's competitive edge (Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019). 
3.3  Engagement 
Engagement, studied as a multidisciplinary phenomenon for over 35 years, was first 
conceptualized by Kahn (1990) with three dimensions: affective, cognitive, and physical, 
drawing from Goffman's role theory, motivation, and group theories (Goffman, 1961). Before 
2000, researchers considered engagement as the positive antipode of burnout, the latter 
associated with mental exhaustion. Both burnout and engagement used to be measured together 
as two dependent and opposite poles. However, around 2000, researcher s concluded that 
engagement and burnout have independent dimensions. Therefore, they should be measured 
independently and with different instruments (Schaufeli & Bakker, 2004). In the literature, we 
identified various engagement-related constructs listed in Table 3.4.

Organizational engagement emphasizes a worker's commitment to their role within the 
enterprise (Shuck et al., 2017). Social and intellectual engagement gauges an employee's 
intellectual involvement in their job and social connections with team members (Shuck et al., 
2017). Work engagement is characterized by vigor, dedication, and task absorption (Crowe, 
2016), while job engagement refers to the effort and ener gy devoted to tasks (Shuck et al., 
2017). Employee engagement, a positive psychological state, encompasses cognitive, 
emotional, and behavioral energy related to work (Shuck et al., 2017; Cordery & Soo, 2008; 
Florea & Stoica, 2019). While job engagement fo cuses on individual tasks, employee 
engagement encompasses broader work experience (Shuck et al., 2017). 
Some authors have also proposed a " discipline-independent definition of engagement and 
measurement scale" (Vreede et al., 2019). They suggest that engagement occurs across three 
temporal levels: task/activity, initiative, and continuous engagement, each corresponding to the 
expected duration (Vreede et al., 2019). Task/activity engagement measures an individ ual's 
engagement only during the period when the activity takes place. Initiative engagement assesses 
an individual's engagement throughout the lifespan of a project or initiative. Finally, continuous 
engagement represents an ongoing state that persists in  the relationship between entities 
(Vreede et al., 2019). 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
39 
 
3.3.1  Contributors to Employee Engagement and Their Outcomes  
Positive outcomes of engaged employees include improved work quality, individual 
performance, reduced absenteeism, increased organizational commitment, and lower turnover 
(Schaufeli et al., 2006; Crowe, 2016; Shaik & Makhecha, 2019). Essential leadership styles for 
promoting engagement include transformational and empowering leadership, focusing on 
inspiring, developing, and intellectually stimulating followers (Schullery, 2013). An engaging 
management process can reduce stress and promote well -being and self-management support 
(Crowe, 2016). Some authors categorize the contributors of engagement into job resources, job 
demands, leadership, job characteristics, individual differences, and personal resources (Saks 
& Gruman, 2014). Job resources encompass auto nomy, supportive colleagues, coaching, 
feedback, opportunities for development, social support, a positive workplace culture, recovery 
time, recognition, rewards, job variety, and work role fit. Challenging demands a positive 
impact, while physical and hin drance demands decreased engagement. Positive job 
characteristics include task variety, significance, feedback, problem -solving processes, 
complexity, and social support. Individual characteristics influencing engagement include core 
self-evaluations, cons cientiousness, positive affect, and a proactive personality. Personal 
resources like self-efficacy, organization-based self-esteem, and optimism positively influence 
engagement (Saks & Gruman, 2014). Figure 3.1 consolidates the main contributors to employee 
engagement and their outcomes (Saks & Gruman, 2014). 

State of the Art 
 
40 
 
 
Figure 3.1 - Contributors to employee engagement, adapted from Saks & Gruman (2014). 
Leadership is recognized as pivotal in the engagement process, with leaders playing a 
significant role in providing employees with job resources and buffering them from hindrance 
demands. Transformational, empowering, and leader -member exchange leadership styles can 
influence engagement by managing job resources, job demands, and psychologi cal conditions 
(Saks & Gruman, 2014). Effective management of a multigenerational workforce requires 
strong leadership tailored to meet the unique needs of each generation (PWC, 2011). 
Job resources encompass physical, psychological, social, or organizational aspects that 
contribute to achieving work goals, mitigating job demands, and fostering personal growth and 
development. These resources can originate from various sources, including  the organization 
(e.g., compensation, career prospects, job security), interpersonal relationships (e.g., supervisor 
and coworker support, team dynamics), work organization (e.g., clear roles, decision -making 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
41 
 
involvement), and the nature of tasks themselves (e.g., skill variety, autonomy, feedback) (Saks 
& Gruman, 2014). Specific job characteristics, such as autonomy, performance feedback, and 
a supportive work environment, are considered job resources as they correlate positively with 
engagement. Conversely, job demands require sustained physical, mental, or psychological 
effort from employees, potentially resulting in physiological or psychological strain. Examples 
include work overload, job insecurity, role a mbiguity, time pressure, and conflict (Saks & 
Gruman, 2014). Additionally, individual differences, referred to as personal resources (e.g., 
self-efficacy, optimism, organizational -based self-esteem), can be activated by job resources 
and contribute to enga gement. These personal resources are malleable and can predict 
engagement while mediating the relationship between job resources and engagement levels 
(Saks & Gruman, 2014). 
3.3.2  Employee Engagement Measurement Tools  
Employee engagement can be assessed using different scales, including the Employee 
Engagement Scale (EES) (Shuck et al., 2017) and Gallup's Q12 Employee Engagement Survey. 
The EES is rooted in Schaufeli's original conceptualization of personal engagement f rom the 
1990s. The authors assert its uniqueness as a measurement model for employee engagement, 
highlighting its status as an independent phenomenon (Shuck et al., 2017). Furthermore, they 
argue that the EES encompasses " the full spectrum of the immediate work experience ," 
including aspects such as work, job, team dynamics, and active engagement in the act of 
working itself (p. 4). The EES comprises 12 questions, with 4 of them dedicated to each 
dimension (cognitive, emotional, and behavioral) (Shuck et al., 2017), as ill ustrated in Table 
3.5.

In 2013, Gallup proposed a different scale. It considered the basic needs, individual needs,

Gallup proprietary Q12 survey comprises twelve elements influencing employee engagement 
and can predict high team performance in critical business outcomes such as reten tion, 
productivity, safety, sales, and revenue. These elements and, consequently, the level of

Gallup classifies employees as follows: 
●       Engaged: Employees are involved in and enthusiastic about their work and workplace. 
They drive performance and innovation and move the organization forward. 
●       Not engaged: Employees are psychologically unattached to their work and company. 
They put time, but not energy or passion, into their work. 
●       Actively disengaged: Employees are unhappy and resentful at work. These workers 
potentially undermine what their engaged coworkers accomplish. 
In 2022, twenty -three percent of the world's workforce reported being engaged at work, 
marking the highest level since Gallup initiated its global engagement measurements in 2009. 
Despite declining engagement in 2020, it has since resumed its historically positive trajectory 
(Gallup, 2023). However, the majority of global employees (59%) are categorized as "quietly 
quitting," indicating a lack of active engagement, followed by 18% classified as actively 
disengaged employees (Gallup, 2023).  
3.4  Prior Artifacts to Design Digital Workplaces 
The design of DWs has garnered significant attention in both academic and business circles. As 
organizations increasingly recognize the importance of the employee’s perception of the work 
environment for productivity and innovation (Santana & Cobo, 2020), we identified some 
artifacts in the literature developed to guide the design and implementation of DWs. This 
section details some of these artifacts based on their focus and purpose. 
In 2001, Lurey and Raisinghani proposed a framework to assess team effectiveness within 
digital environments, emphasizing the integration of design, group dynamics, and external 
support factors crucial for distributed teams. Through this framework, they id entified several 
factors that positively correlate with team effectiveness. Notably, team processes and 
interpersonal relations among team members exhibited robust relationships with team 
performance and member satisfaction. Additionally, the study found m oderate associations 
between selection procedures, executive leadership styles, and measures of effectiveness. 
However, predictor variables such as the design process, internal group dynamics, and external 
support mechanisms showed weaker relations.  
Later, Lin et al. (2010) developed a model drawing from coopetition principles to elucidate 
perceived job effectiveness in team collaboration. Their model highlighted the direct influence 

State of the Art 
 
44 
 
of knowledge sharing, cooperative attitudes, and competitive conflicts on perceived job 
effectiveness. Moreover, they demonstrated that cooperative attitudes and competitive conflicts 
influence knowledge sharing. Empirical testing of this model within info rmation technology 
(IT) organizations confirmed the applicability of coopetition principles in digital teams. 
Other authors have also explored cultural diversity's impact on team dynamics (Shin, 2004; 
Shachaf, 2008). Shachaf (2008) introduced a model focusing on the effects of cultural diversity 
and ICT on team effectiveness. Results indicated that while cultural diversity positively 
influenced decision-making, it had a negative impact on communication. ICT mitigated this 
negative impact on intercultural communication and supported decision -making. Effective 
technologies for intercultural communication included email, teleconferencing combined with 
e-meetings, and team rooms. Moreover, cultural diversity influences the selection of media for 
communication. 
The person-environment (P-E), person-organization (P-O), person-group (P-G), and person-job 
(P-J) fit frameworks have been employed to identify individual, organizational, group, and job 
characteristics essential for digital organizations (Shin, 2004). Att ributes such as valuing 
autonomy, flexibility, diversity, and possessing virtual communication skills and domain 
knowledge are highlighted as pivotal for achieving fit in digital organizations. 
Cordery and Soo (2008) presented a process -oriented model of digital team effectiveness, 
addressing challenges associated with transactive memory systems, work engagement, and 
collective efficacy in digital teams. They also discussed virtual team leadershi p and team 
climate as factors to mitigate potential process losses. 
Leadership's influence in digital work contexts has been thoroughly examined (Neufeld et al., 
2010). Transformational leadership, communication effectiveness, and physical distance 
between leaders and followers influence perceived leader performance. Surprisingly, distance 
did not affect communication effectiveness or perceived leader performance. 
In 2013, Pinjani and Palvia designed a framework exploring the relationship between diversity, 
mutual trust, and knowledge sharing among digital teams. They emphasized the moderating 
impact of collaborative technology and task characteristics. Their findin gs underscored the 
significance of diversity in influencing team processes such as mutual trust and knowledge 
sharing, with collaborative technology and task interdependence playing crucial roles as 
moderators. 
Schulze and Krumm (2017) synthesized existing literature to propose a comprehensive model 
of virtual teamwork KSAOs (knowledge, skills, abilities, and other characteristics). Their 
model encompasses distal characteristics (personality, experience) and prox imal qualities 
(knowledge, skills, and motivation), with technology use, cultural aspects, and geographic 
dispersion impacting various aspects of teamwork. 
Shaik and Makhecha (2019) built on the Job Demands -Resources theory to conceptualize five 
drivers of employee engagement in digital teams: cultural intelligence, communication (formal 
and informal), technology, trust, and individual maturity. They emphasiz ed the importance of 
appropriate team membership, supported by technology and formal/informal communication, 
and fostering a climate of trust among team members to enhance engagement levels. 
One artifact built by business organizations in digital workplace design is the Digital Workplace 
Framework developed by Gartner (2022). This framework emphasizes integrating technology, 
processes, and people to create a cohesive and productive digital wor kplace environment. It 
identifies critical components such as collaboration tools, content management systems, and 
employee experience platforms, providing organizations with a structured approach to digital 
workplace design. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
45 
 
In conclusion, the exploration of digital environments encompasses a multifaceted landscape, 
as evidenced by the diverse frameworks and models discussed. From Lurey and Raisinghani's 
work in 2001 to more recent studies by Pinjani and Palvia (2013), the literature demonstrates a 
different perspective on the complex interplay between team processes, leadership styles, 
cultural diversity, and technology use. These studies underscore the importance of considering 
individual, team, and organizational characteris tics in assessing team effectiveness and other 
outcomes, such as performance and employee engagement. Moreover, synthesizing various 
theoretical perspectives, from coopetition principles to fit frameworks, provides a 
comprehensive lens through which to examine the dynamics of digital teamwork. 
3.5  Research Opportunities 
The literature on virtual teams and DWs is expanding rapidly; however, understanding the 
benefits and challenges these teams face still needs to be improved (Tsuji et al., 2019). Despite 
numerous studies, the results of remote work research need further in vestigation to elucidate 
some inconsistencies (Santana & Cobo, 2020). One of the possible reasons for conflicting 
results regarding the performance of teams according to the workplace arrangement model is 
diversity. Some authors also cite the need for broader studies considering various characteristics 
of a diverse workforce and how these can influence employees' perceptions in a DW (Gilson et 
al., 2015). Cultural and social aspects of the digital work environment and the behaviors of 
different generations within it require deeper investigation (Ruppel et al., 2013; Gilson et al., 
2015; Pedreira et al., 2015). Also, research on virtual teams tends to be fragmented, with much 
attention focused on comparing them to traditional teams (Pinjani & Palvia, 2013). T here is a 
need for empirical research conducted in authentic organizational environments, as much of the 
existing DW research relies heavily on student projects and laboratory studies (Schweitzer & 
Duxbury, 2010). Acharya (2019) argues that future research  should consider the type of 
individuals, tasks, and team size. Exploring DWs across diverse contexts and sectors presents 
further opportunities for multidisciplinary research (Gilson et al., 2015). Adjustments to 
traditional engagement approaches are anticipated in the DW, necessitating research into cross-
generational mentorship and inclusive culture-building (Panteli et al., 2019; Gilson et al., 2015). 
Various scholars have underscored the significance of future research on employee engagement 
and DWs (Saks & Gruman, 2014; Shuck et al., 2017). Methodologically, studies are being 
called to incorporate measures of virtuality to provide causal evidence for proposed links 
(Schulze & Krumm, 2017). Additionally, longitudinal studies are advocated for due to the 
complex nature of team dynamics (Gilson et al., 2015; Acharya, 2019). 
From a technological standpoint, there is a need to explore new collaboration and 
communication tools to support virtual teams (Gilson et al., 2015). Despite the existence of 
various CMC tools, research attention has needed to be much higher for newer tools compared 
to traditional options like email and chat (Gilson et al., 2015). Emerging technologies such as 
gamification platforms and immersive virtual environments offer promising avenues for 
research (Pedreira et al., 2015; Alsharo et al., 2017; Hofma et al., 2017). Moreover, topics like 
cyberslacking require further exploration ( O’Neill et al., 2014) and the scalability of Agile 
Methods within DWs (Deshpande et al., 2016; Kiely et al., 2017). Besides the technological 
aspects, the interaction of different generations within DWs and the impact of Information IT 
on these dynamics require more attention (Gilson et al., 2015).  
The DW encompasses the physical environment, technology, and people (Attaran et al., 2019). 
While the DW concept is essential to the IS community, more is needed to know how it should 
be implemented to support employee engagement toward different generations. The estimate is 

State of the Art 
 
46 
 
that companies invested $1.7 trillion in digital transformation in 2019, with nearly $1,000 per 
employee in end-user technologies spent in DW programs (Deloitte, 2019).  
In the following chapters, we aim to develop a framework and method to help organizations 
establish a successful digital work environment strategy that fosters employee engagement. Our 
work is significant for individuals and organizations since sustainable organizational 
performance relies on employee engagement (Papagiannidis & Marikyan, 2019). This thesis 
addresses some research opportunities highlighted by addressing several fac tors influencing 
employee engagement in the DW, including the needs, values, and behaviors of a 
multigenerational workforce. Furthermore, empirical research conducted in a real 
organizational environment enhances the applicability and relevance of our findings. 
3.6  Conclusion 
As institutional pressures and technological imperatives mount, DWs become increasingly 
crucial in supporting digital business strategies (Zimmer et al., 2020). However, in adopting 
DW arrangements, organizations must acknowledge that virtual or hybrid tea ms face distinct 
challenges compared to face -to-face teams (Florea & Stoica, 2019). Previous research has 
identified various factors influencing effective DW implementation, including the need for 
adequate tools (Ruppel et al., 2013), supportive and partic ipative leadership styles (Wakefield 
et al., 2008), and the transformation of existing workplace practices (Zimmer et al., 2020). Table 
3.7 consolidates the references for the barriers and enablers employees perceive in a DW cited 
in this chapter.

Furthermore, team members in DWs may exhibit diverse abilities, motivations, behaviors, 
attitudes, and commitment levels. Organizations face new challenges in managing this diversity 
(Jonck et al., 2017). On the other hand, studies also show that a diverse  workforce brings 
creativity and better results when solving complex tasks (Bejtkovsky, 2016). Thus, companies 
should be prepared to recruit, manage, and engage employees from different generations 
(Bejtkovsky, 2016). The global workforce comprises four ge nerations: Baby Boomers, 
Generation X, Generation Y (Millennials), and Generation Z. Generations Y and Z collectively 
constitute around half of the global workforce (Buchanan et al., 2016). Notably, they prioritize 
flexibility and work-life balance as pivotal motivators, emphasizing the need for DWs to align 

State of the Art 
 
48 
 
with these values (Larkin, 2017). Bejtkovsky (2016) states, "[t]he first thing to consider is the 
individual and his or her underlying values, or personal, lifestyle characteristics and workplace 
characteristics." 
A multigenerational workforce amplifies the challenges of designing a successful DW. 
However, many factors affecting employees' perceptions of their work environment further 
compound this complexity. Besides the relevance of generational characteristics to  the 
employees' experience in the DW, we must avoid the trap of considering only the cohort 
variable. 

 
49 
 
Chapter 4  
DSR Design and Development 
After presenting the research methodology, rationale, and the first two steps of our DSR project, 
this chapter delves into the design and development phase, as highlighted in Figure 4.1. During 
this phase, we built the proposed artifacts for implementing a n engaging DW tailored to the 
needs and preferences of a multigenerational workforce. Given the diverse characteristics and 
technological competencies of different generations in the workforce, crafting a solution that 
resonates with all is a complex challenge. 
 
Figure 4.1 - DSRM Process Model, emphasizing the Design and Development phase. 
The design and development of the framework and method were performed to provide a 
comprehensive coverage of the challenges experienced by employees in DW. The objective is 
to provide organizations with a robust and adaptable framework and method that fosters 
continuous improvements in DW implementation to improve employee engagement. This 
phase comprised two steps: Artifact Design and Artifact Development, detailed in the following 
sections. To enhance the depth and credibility of research findings, ultimately leading to more 
robust and reliable results, we employed a multi -method approach, which included one 


DSR Design and Development 
50 
 
longitudinal phenomenological study that encompassed one online survey, two rounds of 
interviews, and a confirmatory study built on the statistical data analysis gathered from one 
online survey.  
The design and development phase was iterative, and, as such, the feedback and insights 
gathered during the demonstration and evaluation phases (Chapter 5) were used to refine and 
enhance the proposed artifacts. This cyclical approach ensures that the deve loped framework 
and method are theoretically sound, practically viable, and effective in addressing the identified 
challenges and requirements. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
51 
 
4.1  Artifact Design 
The outcomes of previous phases of our DSR project provided several instruments to be 
employed in our framework and method. These artifacts are the primary outcome of our 
research and aim to support organizations implementing engaging digital workplaces. 
However, since none of the studies in the reviews addressed extreme situations such as the 
COVID-19 pandemic and the resulting lockdowns, we designed a one -year longitudinal 
phenomenological study that explores how employees perceived and overcame barriers to 
remote work during COVID-19 lockdowns. It enabled us to ensure our artifacts are resilient to 
future crises by compiling a comprehensive list of barriers that handle extreme situations.  
After complementing our findings with the employee's perspective of the digital workplace in 
extreme situations, we were able to move forward with the validation of the conceptual model 
of our research by investigating the influence of several instruments and factors on employee 
engagement. We performed a confirmatory study to explore the impact of the instruments and 
the individual, group, and organizational characteristics identified in the previous phases on 
employees’ engagement from Generations X, Y, and Z. By doing that; we identified similarities 
and differences among the generations in their engagement, attitudes, and challenges in digital 
workspaces.  
4.1.1  Longitudinal Phenomenological Study 
We designed this one -year-long longitudinal phenomenological study to understand further 
barriers and engagement in DW in extreme situations such as the COVID -19 pandemic. We 
departed from the systematic literature review on barriers in digital workplaces,  detailed in 
Section 2.4.1, to investigate the barriers remote workers experienced during COVID -19 
lockdowns, which are unique to extreme situations compared to normal operations. We also 
mapped the human and non -human actor networks formed around DW during the pandemic . 
Individual and organizational circumstances influence employee engagement. 
From a phenomenological point of view, the DW must be understood in terms of living the 
experience that is embodied, social, and real. We need to understand how technology shapes 
the social interactions of employees, co -workers, family, and non -humans, like hardware and 
processes. These have complex links and influence one another. ANT enables us to analyze that 
reality (Callon, 1986). It helped us map the relationships between the actors, investigate the 
network movement, and support the achievement of temporary stability (Cresswell et al., 2010). 
Additionally, it helped identify the conflicts of interest between the actors and the problems 
that resulted from them.  
This study had three steps for data collection and analysis. We started by performing an online 
survey, followed by two rounds of interviews spaced one year apart. The first round of 
interviews aimed to enhance the data collected during the online survey with the experience of 
a small group of remote workers during the initial stage of the outbreak. One year later, the 
second round of interviews aimed to analyze how the same workers had overcome the existing 
barriers at the beginning of the pandemic. The fo llowing subsections provide detailed 
information for each step. 
4.1.1.1  Data Collection and Analysis from the Online Survey 
The online survey (Appendix C, Table C.1) took place from the end of April 2020 to the middle 
of May 2020 and had 67 respondents. Most (63%) were between 25 and 40 years old, 36% were 
more than 40 years old, and only 1% were less than 25 years old. We aime d to identify the 

DSR Design and Development 
52 
 
human and digital actors in the digital workplaces and the main barriers perceived by the 
respondents. The answers provided the initial data that enabled us to identify the human and 
non-human actors and apply ANT to study the networks formed among them du ring remote 
work under lockdown. First, we identified the human and non -human actors involved in this 
context: employees, children, spouse, physical space, mobility tools (mobile, Internet, and 
notebook), leaders, co -workers, support team, communication to ols (e.g., Zoom), security, 
management tools, and daily tasks tools. Then, we sketched the typical network experienced by 
employees during the outbreak, represented in Figure 4.2. We considered the employee to be 
the focal actor in the network. The common interest of the respondents was to keep everyone 
safe while maintaining regular activities and work (with minimal possible impact) during the 
outbreak caused by the COVID-19 pandemic. 
 
Figure 4.2 - Typical employee’s network in a digital workplace during the outbreak. 
We also confirmed the use of a technological toolkit similar to that reported in the literature, 
consisting of a laptop, Internet access, and a communication tool (email, videoconferencing, 
mobile, chat, and blogging) (Fajardo et al., 2016; Attaran et al., 2019; Bick et al., 2020). More 
than 80% of the survey respondents used these tools and devices to work in a digital 
environment. 
Through the data analysis, we characterized and differentiated the respondents into three 
groups: disengaged (G1), engaged (G2), and very engaged (G3). We then analyzed how each 
of the three organizational (period working in the organization, team tenure, and contract type) 
and four personal (gender, generation, education level, and family status) circumstances 
influenced the level of engagement among employees. Table 4.1 presents a comprehensive 
analysis of factors influencing employee engagement levels, c onsidering the cited variables. 
This analysis provides valuable insights into the various aspects that contribute to the 
engagement of employees in different groups.

The female employees exhibit a nuanced engagement spectrum: 36% are not engaged, 27% are 
engaged, and 38% are very engaged. Meanwhile, male employees demonstrate slightly higher 
overall engagement, with 31% not engaged, 35% engaged, and 34% very engaged. G en Z has 
the highest number of disengaged employees (49%), whereas Gen X and Y display more 
balanced engagement distributions. Individuals holding a doctorate exhibit the highest level of 
engagement (44%), while individuals with secondary or vocational edu cation exhibit lower 
engagement levels. Moreover, employees with children generally demonstrate higher 
engagement levels. Regarding organizational factors, employees with 1 to 3 years of tenure 
exhibit the highest disengagement rate (39%). In contrast, alm ost half of the employees with 
more than 8 years of tenure are very engaged (46%). Furthermore, team tenure shows a 
balanced distribution of engagement levels. Freelancers have the highest engagement levels, 
showing 67% engaged and 33% very engaged. 
As the pandemic prevented co-located work, the actors needed to establish new agreements and 
procedures to achieve a common purpose. Competing interests and barriers caused some

conflicts. Table 4.2 consolidates the barriers experienced by employees working remotely 
during the COVID-19, as identified in the online survey.

Some barriers are similar to those cited in studies about digital workplaces made before the 
pandemic, such as isolation, poor physical workspace conditions, poor internet connectivity, 
distractions and interruptions, and poor communication with co -workers. However, new 
barriers were experienced by employees during the lockdown, such as difficulty keeping a work 
routine, difficulty reconciling job tasks with children care and home -schooling, and general 
anxiety regarding the impact of COVID-19. These are the barriers experienced only in extreme 
situations. 
Based on the results of this online survey, we developed the questions (Appendix C, Table C.2) 
necessary to enhance the data and interview a small group of remote workers during the initial 
stage of the outbreak. Additionally, we defined the characteristics required  to select the 
interviewees: (1) individuals with and without children and (2) individuals already familiar with 
and unfamiliar with remote work, as detailed in the following sub-section. 
4.1.1.2  Data Collection and Analysis from the Interviews 
The first round of interviews occurred from May to June 2020. We reached out to a list of eight 
individuals experiencing the phenomenon to constitute a convenience sample (Saumure & 
Given, 2012). All study participants underwent a complete lockdown and were mandated  to 
work from home during this period. They were Brazilian and Portuguese employees employed 
across various organizations. Of the participants, only 25% were self -employed, 12.5% had 
fixed-term contracts, and 62.5% had open-ended agreements. Moreover, the majority (62.5%) 
worked in the technology industry. Table 4.3 provides additional contextual information 
regarding the sample participants. We opted for email interviews to enhance participant access 
and encourage greater involvement among working adults, for whom synchronous interviews 
could have been more feasible (Fritz & Vandermause, 2017). Our objective was to identify the 
barriers experienced in a digital workplace at the onset of the COVID -19 pandemic, the 
involved actors, their relationships, and the conflicts experienced in this context. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
55 
 
Table 4.3 - Contextual factors of the participants comprising the sample. 
Gender With children Without children 
Female 1 1 
Male 4 2 
The second round of interviews took place between April and August 2021, involving the same 
group of eight interviewees. It aimed to understand the employees’ perceived experience after 
one year of working remotely during the outbreak. Among all participants, only 25% had 
partially returned to the office, while the remaining 75% continued to work from home full -
time. We developed a new questionnaire (Appendix C, Table C.3) to understand if the focal 
actor had overcome the barriers and solved the conflicts one year after the outbreak began. We 
also conducted the second round of interviews by email for the same reason cited previously. 
The participants received an explanation of the research process and purpose of the study before 
the beginning of each round of interviews. Then, they had two weeks to return the filled -in 
questionnaire. 
We transcribed and analyzed the data collected in both rounds of interviews by content to 
identify recurrent statements. We coded the data manually to enable an interpretive analysis of 
an experienced phenomenon and used the ‘top -down’ approach proposed by  Miller and Fox 
(1997). This approach allowed a systematic and focused analysis based on established 
categories: the participants' feelings, barriers experienced, perceived benefits, and the involved 
actors. We stored all data in a secure location. In each round of the interviews, we performed 
three steps for coding procedures. First, we analyzed the data collected by only two participants 
to confirm the completeness of the information to answer our research q uestions. Before 
sending it to the rest of the sample, any necessary adjustments were made to the questionnaire 
to ensure a better understanding of the questions and avoid repetition. Second, with the 
completed database, we carefully coded all answers, see king the feelings of the participants, 
barriers experienced, benefits perceived, and the involved actors (human and non -human). 
Finally, during the third step, we reviewed the answers to ensure accuracy and identified the 
similarities and differences among coded data. 
During the bracketing phase, we tried to set aside our own experiences. To that end, we logged 
all biases and preconceived notions that arose during this study in a journal. 
The first round of interviews with the survey respondents focused on understanding the first 
stage of ANT’s translation process (problematization). Each focal actor had a network, so we 
captured their similarities. Tables 4.4 and 4.5 describe the existing interests and problems of 
the human and non-human actors identified in the network.
