In the first round of interviews, we could divide the results from the sample into two distinct 
moods: gratitude and anxiety. The grateful interviewees felt secure at home, with their families, 
while maintaining a job during such an uncertain time. On the other hand, the lack of 
predictability about the future provoked anxiety in the other half of the sample. The employees 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
57 
 
on a temporary contract felt insecure. Nevertheless, unsecure job contracts increase engagement 
with the work since freelancers and self -employed workers expressed high levels of 
engagement. Eventually, the need for a stable income motivated these workers to focus on their 
tasks. Since freelancers and self-employed typically invest in areas they find enjoyable for their 
career development, it may induce higher engagement rates. 
Further, we identified a sense of guilt when both parents worked and the children were under 
six years old. One of the participants stated,"(...) having to combine my work activities with my 
daughter’s school activities affected me negatively. I often felt guilty about leaving my daughter 
watching TV to be able to work ". For those parents, accommodating job activities, 
housekeeping, home -schooling, and children care - many of these during traditional office 
hours - were relevant barriers. It is essential to underscore that individual  with children 
demonstrated heightened engagement with their work. This suggests a notable endeavor to 
balance parental and professional responsibilities, driven by the imperative to fulfill their 
children's educational and other essential needs. 
The inability to allocate physical spaces at home for the various activities and actors generated 
conflicts. Inadequate workspaces due to the lack of a dedicated desk, ergonomic chair, or noisy 
environments were cited as having a significant negative impac t, as were distractions, 
interruptions, and the lack of social interaction. Moreover, employees with adequate tools (e.g., 
management and knowledge -sharing tools) to perform their tasks perceive remote work as a 
better work arrangement model. Many had less  or no disposition to practice regular physical 
activity, even though previous studies identified it and gratitude as relevant factors for 
individual well-being (HHP, 2021). One of the participants stated, "I'm not doing any physical 
activity. I lack the mood." 
The interviewees shared different views regarding the return to a physical workplace (in-office). 
Three of them declared preferring this alternative, two others would choose to continue at home, 
and three participants showed interest in combining both (home office and company’s office). 
The participants who preferred a digital or hybrid model perceived increased interaction with 
their families and less commuting time as benefits. 
Callon (1986) proposes identifying the OPP to solve the conflicts and pursue network balance. 
The OPP is the set of mandatory procedures commonly agreed upon by all actors to achieve the 
balance of the system (Callon, 1986). Our focal actor is the employee , so their interests drive 
the OPP. To enhance the resilience capabilities of organizations, it is essential to align the goals 
of all human and non-human actors involved in the organization's functioning. This alignment 
helps create a shared goal that ultimately benefits the focal actor. 
Although this study did not focus on ANT’s phases of interessement, enrollment, and 
mobilization, we revisited these topics during a second round of interviews with the same 
participants one year later. We wanted to determine if the focal actors had made p rogress in 
achieving network balance, including how they convinced others to accept their 
problematization and agree on solutions. Overall, the actors could argue, establish some 
procedures with other actors, and enroll the network to achieve balance.  The feeling of isolation 
decreased by about 37.5%. Also, we did not identify general anxiety and difficulty keeping a 
work routine in the second round of interviews. However, accommodating home -schooling 
during working hours remained a problem that impacted 50% of the focal actors. Furthermore, 
new conflicts arose, caused by a heavier burden of increasing work -related meetings and the 
sense of being almost constantly online. Stress and burnout became more evident.  
Nevertheless, almost half of the participants described the experience as a suitable surprise after 
the first year of adaptation and resilience. Organizational support, such as reimbursement of 

DSR Design and Development 
58 
 
Internet expenses, support for practicing physical activity, or psychological support, positively 
influenced the employees' experience. However, most companies provided only technical and 
material support, raising employees' difficulty overcoming the experienced barriers. Employees 
also expected emotional support, such as mindfulness training. Only one respondent showed 
interest in returning to work face-to-face due to the need for equipment available at the physical 
workplace. The majority preferred to kee p working remotely or in a hybrid model. The 
interviewees cited the following situations as relevant for in -person contexts: easy access to 
people to get information, keeping sporadic contact with the team, meeting new clients, and 
informal time with co -workers. Little was mentioned regarding changes to the organizational 
processes to make them better suited to digital work. Usually, the managers follow up with 
employees via email, mobile, or videoconference status meetings. 
4.1.1.3  Conclusions from the Longitudinal Phenomenological Study 
After this longitudinal phenomenological study, we understood how an extreme phenomenon 
like COVID-19 and its lockdowns aligned with what was previously researched about barriers 
to remote work. The online survey addressed identifying the barriers experien ced during 
COVID-19 lockdowns in remote work and the human and digital actors in the DW, using ANT, 
besides providing the input to design the questionnaire for the interviews. Then, we confirmed 
these barriers in two rounds of interviews, which also provid ed the necessary information to 
map employee networks in a DW and the human actors’ interests and problems. Figure 4.3 
shows the barriers identified during the online survey (blue) and the first and second rounds of 
interviews (red and orange, respectively). 
 
 
Figure 4.3 - Barriers identified during the three steps of the Phenomenological Study. 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
59 
 
We also identified a set of common barriers at the beginning of the pandemic, both in the online 
survey and the first round of interviews, as the most relevant to employees, such as anxiety, a 
feeling of isolation, difficulty reconciling job tasks with the children care, difficulty in keeping 
a work routine, and distractions and interruptions. Table 4.6 consolidates the barriers 
experienced in extreme situations vs. normal operations.

Regarding which barriers are expected only in extreme situations (like lockdowns) vs. regular 
operations, we observed that some are common to those identified in pre -pandemic studies 
about digital workplaces. For example, isolation, poor physical workspace conditions, poor 
internet connectivity, distractions and interruptions, and poor communication with co-workers. 
However, new barriers were experienced by employees during the lockdown, such as difficulty 
keeping a work routine, difficulty reconciling job tasks with children care and home-schooling, 
and general anxiety regarding the impact of COVID-19. Furthermore, new barriers arose as the 
situation prolonged, such as burnout due to increasing meetings and worked hours, lack of 
organizational support, and conflicts with family. 
Employees with support from other actors (e.g., spouse, leader, or adequate tools) more easily 
overcame initial barriers. Most interviewed parents declared no availability to practice regular 
physical activities. However, employees who managed to allocate time for themselves were 
less susceptible to burnout than those who did not. This finding aligns with previous studies 
that show the relevance of regular physical activities in improving life satisfaction during 
confinement (Zhang et al., 2020).  
Furthermore, we observed that individuals experiencing a feeling of gratitude (for being safe) 
were aware of the difficulties but were more willing to carry out their tasks than the employees 
feeling anxious. Recent research by the Harvard Medical School shows that grateful people can 
improve their health, deal with adversity, and build strong relationships (HHP, 2021). Providing 
mindfulness training in the workplace and reinforcing managers to recognize the people who 
work for them are some examples of pro moting gratitude regularly with employees (HHP, 
2021). We have also identified various practices in the literature that can help overcome the 
perceived barriers by employees in a digital workplace. These practices can support the focal 
actor in establishin g the necessary procedures to proceed with the interessement, enrollment, 
and mobilization phases, promoting work engagement. For instance, the feeling of isolation can 
be mitigated by organizational incentives for physical activities (Zhang et al., 2020; HHP, 2021) 
or by promoting social events between co -workers (Stratone, 2019). Furthermore, poor 

DSR Design and Development 
60 
 
communication with co-workers and a lack of adequate tools or information needed to perform 
the job can be avoided by utilizing flexible communication methods (Ruppel et al., 2013), such 
as synchronous vs. asynchronous and virtual vs. face-to-face communication, enabling the flow 
of information between teams and departments (Lee -Kelley, 2006; Schmidtke & Cummings, 
2017), and adequate tools for sharing knowledge considering oral, written, and electronic 
communication (Kauppila et al., 2011). Moreover, the la ck of knowledge required to perform 
the job can be overcome using flexible training options, such as interactive training and 
individual and informal learning with co-workers (Alsharo et al., 2016). Lastly, it is important 
to caution that before adopting any of the proposed best practices, it is necessary to understand 
the current organizational, team, job context, and characteristics. 
The effectiveness of digital tools plays a significant role in how employees experience the 
digital workplace. Some organizations needed to prepare for such a dramatic overnight change 
in their work routines, so employees were confronted with inadequate me chanisms to manage 
and participate in organizational processes, communicate, access information, and share 
knowledge. In these cases, they felt that the digital workplace was unsuitable for their job 
activities and preferred an in -office or hybrid work arr angement after the outbreak. Some 
organizations provided technical support to deal with the challenging situation, which had a 
positive impact. However, the employees still needed to catch up on the emotional and material 
support required to overcome the c onflicts raised by remote work during the pandemic. Still, 
regarding the use of technology, we have not identified new trends such as AI, tools to support 
data management, or IVEs to increase the likelihood of feeling "physically" present. 
Regarding the personal and organizational circumstances that influence employee engagement, 
our study shows that the younger generation (Gen Z) feels less engaged with work. This may 
be related to the fact that only some of these respondents have long-term commitments, such as 
children, and may be pressured to dedicate more time and effort to completing their work tasks. 
Parents express more engagement with work.  
It is also interesting to see that respondents with higher academic degrees eventually feel more 
engaged with the work because it is more meaningful and aligned with their prior educational 
efforts. Similarly, people with higher control over their tasks, s uch as freelancers and self -
employed workers, are more engaged with the work.  
Finally, our study shows that the longer people work in the same organization, the more 
engaged they feel about their work. This can result from a better understanding of what the 
organization expects from them and solid relationships with other workers, making it easier to 
perform tasks online or in a hybrid format. 
According to Schaffer et al. (2021), organizations must handle volatility, uncertainty, 
complexity, and ambiguity to mitigate the barriers. The same authors state that " resilient 
organizations are successful in coping with crises,"  and companies should have a business 
model to bear organizational resilience. 
4.1.2  Confirmatory Study of the Research Conceptual Model 
After concluding the longitudinal phenomenological study, we had eight instruments (detailed 
in section 4.2.2) to support organizations applying the framework and method when 
implementing engaging DW. We also identified 75 factors (variables) affecting emp loyee 
engagement in a digital workplace. Some of these factors are part of the instruments, such as 
Barriers and Enablers (I3) and Virtuality and Enterprise Integration (I2). The others are related 
to different individual, team, and organizational characte ristics that may influence employee 
engagement in a DW. We grouped the relevant organizational factors influencing employee 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
61 
 
engagement into Organizational Attractiveness and Predecessors. The team’s characteristics 
pertinent to our research were classified as Interaction Approach (Face -to-face vs. Virtual). 
Considering our research question, the generational cohort is the relev ant individual 
characteristic (independent variable) to be studied. Employee Engagement is the primary 
outcome to be analyzed, as depicted in our conceptual model. 
We categorized the several factors that influence employee engagement into six groups: 
Barriers and Enablers (detailed in section 4.2.2.3), Predecessors for a Successful Digital 
Workplace (detailed in section 3.1.3), Organizational Attractiveness Factors, Interaction 
Approach (detailed in section 2.1.1), and Enterprise Integration and Technological Tools 
(detailed in section 4.2.2.2). Our conceptual model (shown in Figure 4.4) has been developed 
with the recognition that these 75 factors may exert either po sitive or negative influences on 
employee engagement. Furthermore, employee engagement can be assessed using a scale 
comprising 16 questions. 
 
Figure 4.4 - Research Conceptual Model. 
We utilized generational cohorts as the independent variable to scrutinize the various factors 
outlined in the conceptual model represented in Figure 4.4. Barriers and Enablers delineate 
employees' perceptions of common factors within a digital workplace t hat may negatively or 
positively affect their experience. The Predecessors are the pivotal factors, elucidating the 
interrelationships between different elements affecting employee engagement in a digital 
workspace. Factors such as Organizational Attractiv eness, Interaction Approach, and 
Enterprise Integration and Technological Tools can positively or negatively influence the 
predecessors, subsequently impacting employee engagement, which serves as the primary 
outcome. The conceptual model provides a compre hensive understanding and explanation of 
the intricate interplay of factors influencing employee engagement within a digital workplace. 
Given these premises, we designed a qualitative study to validate our conceptual model by 
investigating six hypotheses: 
H1. Generations X, Y, and Z differ in their perceptions of the barriers and enablers in a 
digital workplace. 


DSR Design and Development 
62 
 
H2. Generations X, Y, and Z converge in their perceptions of the organization's 
attractiveness factors. 
H3. Generations X, Y, and Z converge in their perceptions that certain tasks are more 
effectively accomplished through face-to-face interactions rather than virtual ones. 
H4. Generations X, Y, and Z differ in their adoption of the digital workplace, which is  
based on enterprise integration and technological tools. 
H5. Generations X, Y, and Z differ in their perceptions of the predecessors for a 
successful digital workplace. 
H6. Generations X, Y, and Z differ in their level of engagement in the organization. 
Subsequent sub-sections describe the questionnaire design process, data collection, analysis, 
and findings from our confirmatory study. 
4.1.2.1  Questionnaire Design 
Once we identified the relevant factors to be studied in our research, we could design a proper 
questionnaire to collect the necessary data to be analyzed. Built on the P-E fit model for digital 
organizations, we grouped the individual, team, and organizational characteristics that influence 
employee engagement in a DW (Shin, 2004). This led to the structure of the questionnaire, 
which is composed of four groups of questions: (1) Individual, (2) Team, (3) Organizational, 
and (4) Employee Engagement. The fourth group was added to capture the necessary 
information to measure the level of employee engagement, which is the primary outcome 
observed in our research. 
The Individual group of questions aims to identify the employees’ characteristics that influence 
their engagement in a DW. It is composed of seven questions (detailed in Table 4.7); the main 
characteristic to be observed in the scope of our study is the generation cohort (question ID I.1). 
However, we decided to include other relevant individual characteristics cited previously in the 
literature to support in deep data analysis in case only the generational cohort did not bring 
statistical evidence for the study.

The Employee Engagement group of questions presents the questions of our EES, detailed in 
Table 4.22. Additionally, we provided a free text question to enable the participants to provide 
any additional comments about how you evaluated the affirmatives of the scale previously. 
The questionnaire has 20 questions. Before answering it, each participant had access to the 
study's objective, a brief explanation of DW, and an estimated time necessary to conclude it. 

DSR Design and Development 
68 
 
We also ensured confidentiality and confirmed the respondents' acceptance of participating in 
this study.  
4.1.2.2  Data Collection 
We employed LimeSurvey to conduct the online survey from June 7th to July 29th, 2022, to 
collect the necessary data to validate our conceptual model. Our target audience consisted of 
employed individuals across diverse business sectors engaged in digital o r remote work to 
varying degrees. This approach ensured a comprehensive understanding of the digital 
workplace environment and enhanced the overall quality of the collected data. We reached out 
to potential respondents through LinkedIn, Facebook, and Instagram, as well as through direct 
contacts spanning various industries. Participants were provided with a clear explanation of the 
study's objectives and ethical considerations, including assurances of anonymity and 
confidentiality. Participation was entirely voluntary and without any form of incentives. 
We adopted and adapted scales already validated in the literature to ensure precise measurement 
of theoretical constructs. The questionnaire encompassed Generation Cohort, Barriers and 
Enablers, Predecessors of a successful digital workplace, Organizational Attractiveness, 
Interaction Approach, Enterprise Integration and  Technological Tools, and EES. Participants 
were asked to rate the predecessors and employee engagement using a 5-point Likert-type scale 
ranging from 'excellent/strongly agree' to 'very poor/strongly disagree.' Barriers and enablers, 
organizational attractiveness, enterprise integration level, and technological tools usage were 
assessed using a 3-point Likert-type scale ('agree,' 'neutral,' 'disagree,' or 'high,' 'neutral,' 'low'). 
One professor specializing in social studies conducted a thorough review to ensure 
questionnaire quality, making necessary wording and scale usage adjustments. A test phase was 
also undertaken to refine the questionnaire for clarity and comprehensibility, incorporating 
feedback by implementing minor wording alterations. The questionnaire was available in 
English and Portuguese to accommodate a broader audience. 
Following the data collection phase, we utilized IBM SPSS for data analysis. Generational 
cohorts were used to compare the perceptions of three generations regarding the 73 
organizational, team, individual, and engagement factors influencing employee engagement in 
a digital workplace. 
4.1.2.3  Data Analysis 
A total of 688 participants accessed the online questionnaire through the provided link. Only 
fully completed questionnaires were considered, amounting to a total of 378 responses . Table 
4.10 provides a breakdown of their demographics. Notably, the distribution of generations in 
our sample mirrors previous research findings, which project that Millennials and Generation 
Z will collectively make up approximately 75% of the workforce  by 2030 (Deloitte, 2019). 
Most respondents (91%) held either a bachelor's or master's degree. A substantial portion (63%) 
reported stable employment, with 80% expressing satisfaction with their contractual situation. 
Concerning tenure within their current  organizations, 31% of respondents had eight or more 
years, 20% had between 3 and 8 years, 23% had 1 to 3 years, and 25% had less than one year. 
Notably, half of the respondents expressed dissatisfaction with the frequency of face -to-face 
interactions with their co-workers, with 60% advocating for an increase.

We conducted an exploratory factor analysis encompassing two sets of factors. Firstly, we 
examined respondents' statements in the context of the EES. Secondly, we aimed to identify 
distinct factors contributing to facilitating digital workplaces. 
Regarding the statements, Table 4.11 summarizes our findings by identifying three distinct 
components. These components collectively account for 59.79% of the variance (with a Kaiser-
Meyer-Olkin adequacy index of 0.898), providing insight into how responde nts perceive their 
current employment situations.

in 5 iterations. Only weights more significant than 0.5 are included. Note: KMO 0.898; Bartlett's Test of Sphericity with p-
value 0.000; 3 factors retained based on screeplot suggestion (explained variance 59,79%) 
The primary factor, wielding the most substantial explanatory power over workers' perceptions, 
consolidates motivations associated with organizational commitment. It encompasses actions 
such as exceeding expected effort levels to bolster the organization's success, going above and 
beyond to help the team attain its objectives, willingly expending additional effort without 
prompting, concerns for the organization's future, personal attachment to the role performed 
within the organization, belief in the organization's mission and purpose, and a strong sense of 
organizational belonging. This factor, which accounts for 41% of the variance, merges into a 
category we may label "engaged employees." A secondary factor, less significant but still 
noteworthy (explaining 10.4% of the variance), congregates recognition -related statements. It 
clusters workers who gauge their work and workplace regarding the recognition they receive. 
It includes considerations like recent praise or acknowledgment received for a job well do ne 
within the last seven days, discussions about workplace progress within the previous six 
months, perceptions of genuine concern for the worker's well -being by supervisors or 
colleagues, and access to opportunities for professional growth and development within the past 
year. We may refer to this factor as "perception of the organization and work influenced by 
received recognition." Lastly, a third factor, possessing a relatively lower explanatory capacity 
(accounting for 8.4% of the variance), relates to  self-centered tendencies. It is associated with 
the perception of being highly focused when carrying out assigned tasks, seeing oneself as 
particularly task-oriented at work, and possessing the ability to concentrate effectively during 
workplace activitie s. We observe individuals predominantly "focused on individual tasks 
within this factor."  

DSR Design and Development 
72 
 
Concerning digital workplaces, we identified three factors that collectively expound upon 
53.06% of the variance indicated in Table 4.12, with a Kaiser-Meyer-Olkin adequacy index of 
0.862 (Kaiser, 1970). The foremost, accounting for 41% of the variance, underscores facets 
related to career development as pivotal facilitators in the context of a digital workplace, 
encompassing elements such as effec tive governance and processes, delineated roles and 
responsibilities, establishing explicit, attainable, and measurable goals, and mechanisms for 
rewards, recognition, and feedback. Respondents perceive these as instrumental in fostering a 
digital workplace. A secondary factor, elucidating 10.9% of the variance, pertains to aspects 
associated with a robust and open organizational culture. It encompasses attributes like the 
ability to function effectively within a culturally diverse organizational environment, alignment 
with the organization's values and mission, active engagement and attachment to fellow team 
members, and promoting frequent and spontaneous communication. Individuals regard these 
attributes as integral in nurturing the development of digital workplaces. Lastly, a third factor, 
explaining 7.8% of the variance, is characterized negatively, spotlighting aspects perceived as 
irrelevant in facilitating digital workplaces. These encompass adequate access to information, 
the availability of appropriate tools to perform job functions, and the capacity to anticipate or 
predict work -related tasks. This factor again highlights the existence of a subgroup of 
individuals within the organization who appear to be predominantly self-centered in their work 
activities and task orientation, exhibiting a disconnected stance from broader organizational 
goals and digital workplace initiatives.

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
73 
 
Having identified general factors that allow us, through exploratory factor analysis, to 
characterize and differentiate the group of respondents to test our hypotheses, we must 
investigate how Generations X, Y, and Z are situated concerning these groups of  respondents 
and what factors bring them closer or further from each other To address this objective, we 
employed three distinct approaches, applied to a battery of seven sets comprising the 73 (of the 
75) variables mentioned in Figure 4.4. The first two a pproaches rely on descriptive statistics, 
utilizing Pearson's Chi-squared test and bivariate correlation analysis. These methods involve 
contingency tables to distribute and compare the factors among the three generations (X, Y, and 
Z). The third approach, aimed at highlighting trends (even when they may not attain statistical 
significance based on the employed statistical metrics), entails generating analyses based on 
three criteria: i) Assessing the degree to which the three generations converge or diverge in their 
rankings or attributions of importance to each of the analyzed factors; ii) Examining the extent 
to which there exists a discernible trend, whether ascending or descending, that separates the 
three generations in their perspectives; iii) Ascertaining to what extent and in what manner one 
of the generations clearly distinguishes itself from the other two. These three approaches were 
applied systematically across seven sequential groups, each with specific objectives: i) 
Identifying the varied perc eptions of barriers and enablers within digital workplaces as 
perceived by employees across different generations (H1); ii) Gauging the extent to which 
particular predecessors of the digital workplace are perceived as either poor or very poor, or 
good or very good by the three generations (H2); iii) Identifying the consistency of values with 
those previously identified as significant for workplace generations, seeking to evaluate how 
these factors contribute to making an organization an appealing employer (H3); iv) Identifying 
activities that may be perceived as more effective when conducted via face-to-face interactions 
from the employee's perspective (H4); v) Assessing the level of enterprise integration and 
evaluating the maturity level of technology usag e (H5); vi) Appraising the level of employee 
engagement, utilizing the EES (H6). This comprehensive approach allows for a nuanced 
exploration of differences and commonalities across a broad spectrum of factors, providing 
insights into the dynamics and perc eptions of three generations within the organizational 
context. 
Regarding our first hypothesis, we aim to highlight the significant barriers employees face when 
working in a digital environment, as perceived from their perspective. Among the barriers 
presented in Table 4.13, "Ambiguity of function" (Chi -squared = 0.005 ), "Rewards, 
recognition, or feedback" (Chi -squared = 0.016), "Explicit, achievable, and measurable 
objectives" (Chi-squared = 0.005), "Adequate access to information" (Chi -squared = 0.002), 
and "Identification with the organization's values and purpose" ( with a p-value of 0.017 in the 
correlation analysis) exhibit statistical significance when correlated with the three generational 
groups. Table 4.13 consolidates the 23 variables that assess potential barriers and enablers in a 
digital work environment and indicates that more than 50% of respondents consider the impact 
of these to be high for 10 of them. Conversely, for only 3 of these items, less than 30% of 
respondents perceive the effects of barriers and enablers to be high. Regarding items that 
maintain a statistically significant relationship with the respondents' generational affiliation, the 
level of impact of "Ambiguity of function" is regarded as low, particularly by Generation Z 
(9.8%), in contrast to Generation X (24.7%) and Generation Y (23.6%). The item "Incentives 
from the organization for training or education," "Adequate access to information," and 
"Rewards, recognition, or feedback" follow a similar trend, with a high level of impact 
perceived mainly by Generation Z. However, the item "Identi fication with the organization's 
values and purpose" exhibits a downward trend, with Generation Y (38.2%) and Generation Z 
(39.3%) perceiving a lower impact compared to Generation X (46.4%). Additionally, the item 
"Explicit, achievable, and measurable objectives" brings Generations X and Z closer together, 
distinguishing them from Generation Y, as a significantly higher percentage of Generation Y 

DSR Design and Development 
74 
 
(30.6%) considers the impact to be high compared to Generation X (16.7%) and Generation Z 
(13.8%). While statistical significance is absent, notable trends emerge, indicating that 
Generation Z is moving further away from the collective logic of relationships when performing 
tasks and gravitating closer to virtual work environments and their associated challenges. These 
trends manifest in two ways when examining the percentage of respondents considering the 
high impact of barriers and enablers in a digital w ork environment. On the one hand, they 
illustrate a convergence between Generations X and Y and a divergence from Generation Z 
regarding specific barriers and enablers, such as miscommunication due to different time zones 
or language difficulties, poor team relationships, governance and appropriate processes, and the 
availability of suitable tools for work. On the other hand, while maintaining a closer alignment 
between Generations X and Y, these trends reveal an increasing valuation as one moves from 
the older generation to the younger one. Examples include the ability to anticipate or predict 
tasks at work, clarity about the roles and responsibilities of team members, the flow of 
information, and the skills and preferences of team members. It partially sup ports our first 
hypothesis, as it reveals differences in the perceptions of barriers and enablers in a digital 
workplace. Generation Z exhibits more distinct views than Generation X and Y, who share a 
relatively closer perspective.

The statistical measures, including Pearson's Chi -squared test and correlation tests, do not 
reveal significant relationships between the generations and the 11 variables that individuals 
may perceive as attractive. However, specific patterns emerge when we examine the results 
through trend analysis. Table 4.14 shows that the variables considered essential for making an 
organization attractive tend to be ranked similarly by all three generations. For instance, 
Generation Y and Z deem competitive salaries or other financial incentives the most important 
and the second most important Generation X. Work -life balance is among the top two critical 
variables for all three generations. With one notable exception, when one generation attributes 
less than 10% of importance to a particular variable, the other generations t end to follow this 
trend. Specifically, variables such as international opportunities, a good reputation for ethical 
practices, a reputation as an employer of the best people, and the value of the organization's 
brand are considered unimportant by less tha n 10% of members in each generation. The 
exception to this pattern is related to corporate values aligned with individual values. Only 6.6% 
of Generation Z emphasize this variable. In comparison, it garners greater attention from 18.6% 
of Generation X and 19.3% of Generation Y. Interestingly, there is a decreasing trend in the 
importance of flexible working arrangements as the age of the generation decreases. 
Generations X (47.4%) and Y (46.7%) show more significant  emphasis on this variable 
compared to Generation Z (29.5%), which places less importance on it as a variable contributing 
to organizational attractiveness. Our findings are consistent with previous research emphasizing 
the importance of flexible work arra ngements and work -life balance for three generations 
(Bencsik et al., 2016; Gladwell et al., 2010). However, Gen Z emphasizes career development 
more and expresses concerns about unemployment compared to earlier generations (Bencsik et 
al., 2016). Also, the results are independent of gender or parental status among employees, thus 
deviating from a prior study's suggestion that gender may influence benefit preferences 
(Gladwell et al., 2010). In summary, while statistical tests do not reveal significant 
relationships, trend analysis uncovers noteworthy patterns in how Generations X, Y, and Z 
perceive these organizational variables. Despite some variations, there is a considerable degree 
of alignment in their rankings of these variables, shedding light on shared values and priorities 
across the generational spectrum. It partially supports our second hypothesis, as it reveals that 
Generations X, Y, and Z converge in their perceptions of the organization's attractiveness 

DSR Design and Development 
76 
 
factors in the case of the three most relevant variables: Competitive salaries or other financial 
incentives, Work-life balance, and Career development opportunities.

We endeavored to assess the beliefs of the three generations regarding the advantages of 
adopting a face -to-face approach versus a virtual one for various aspects of professional 
functions. While there is a prevailing tendency to reject the notion that "Al l activities carried 
out virtually are more efficient," it is evident and statistically significant (Chi-squared = 0.004) 
that this belief correlates with age. Older generations, such as Generation X, are more inclined 
to believe that all virtual activitie s are inherently more efficient. Conversely, the younger 
Generation Z respondents are more likely to think that virtual activities entail a loss of 
effectiveness. For example, 16.5% of Generation X respondents believe that all virtual activities 
are more e fficient, whereas only 4.9% of Generation Z respondents share this belief. It may 
appear paradoxical that the youngest generation, which is assumed to be the most tech -savvy 
and accustomed to virtual work environments, has the lowest faith in the efficiency of the virtual 
approach for all work activities. However, it is worth postulating and exploring in future studies 
whether the greater exposure of Generation Z to virtual work environments has led them to 
conclude that certain activities are unsuitable for a virtual approach.  
Considering the ten variables in Table 4.15, the prevailing belief is that virtual approaches are 
more effective than face -to-face ones. Celebration activities, conflict management, and 
discussions regarding benefits packages are where all generations believe most in the efficacy 
of face-to-face ap proaches. However, among these, most respondents from each generation 
concur on the effectiveness of face-to-face interactions only in the case of celebration activities. 
For "conflict management," only Generations Y and Z consider face -to-face approaches more 
effective than virtual ones. Across various dimensions, there is an upward trend from 
Generation X (the oldest) to Generation Z (the youngest). This suggests that, even though the 
differences may not be statistically significant in our sample, Generat ion Z generally strongly 
believes in the effectiveness of face-to-face approaches. This trend is observed, for example, in 
conflict management (highlighted by 46.4% of Generation X, 52.8% of Generation Y, and 
60.7% of Generation Z as a dimension where face -to-face is more effective than virtual), 
meeting with customers (28.6%, 27.8%, and 42.6%, respectively), and training (18.6%, 19.8%, 
and 27.9%, respectively). It is intriguing to note that all three generations agree that the 
dimension of "Carrying out da ily activities" is one in which the face -to-face approach is less 
effective than the virtual one. Interestingly, Generation Z, which is typically associated with 
virtual work environments, exhibits the slightest belief that face -to-face interaction is more  

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
77 
 
effective for "Carrying out daily activities." This underscores the significance of Generation Z's 
extensive exposure to virtual work environments, allowing them to make more nuanced 
distinctions between virtual and face -to-face virtual meetings' pros and cons. It partially 
confirms our third hypothesis: Generations X, Y, and Z converge in their perceptions that 
specific tasks are more effectively accomplished through face -to-face interactions rather than 
virtual ones, especially in the case of the three most relevant variables: Conflict manage ment, 
Team building, and Celebration.

Table 4.16 presents six statements concerning the level of enterprise integration. As a general 
trend, there is a consensus in agreement with all six statements. However, statistically 
significant differences among the three generations emerged, with Generation Z sh owing a 
higher inclination to agree than X and Y in all but one statement. Two of the statements maintain 
statistical significance across generations. In both cases, the older generation tends to agree to 
a lesser extent, while the younger one is more inclined to agree. Specifically, for the statement 
"Processes are based on departmental software," 27.8% of Generation X agree, compared to 
37.3% of Generation Y and 45.9% of Generation Z. Regarding the statement "A 
multidisciplinary group of people who use IT  to carry out their tasks," we observe agreement 
of 74.2% for Generation X, 77.8% for Generation Y, and 83.6% for Generation Z. In analyzing 
statistically non-significant trends, it is evident that for the statement "A multidisciplinary group 
of people who share responsibilities and objectives," Generations X and Y align closely, with 
the agreement of 66% and 67.9%, respectively, which is significantly different from Generation 
Z’s 82%. This pattern applies to the statement, "Daily processes and activities are integrated 
through automated workflows to enable data/information exchange and improve relationships 
with customers and partners." In this case, Generations X and Y exhibit agreement of 46.4% 
and 45.8%, respectively, in contrast with Generation Z’s 11.5%.

We also sought to assess the three generations' different usage of technological tools concerning 
17 variables (Table 4.17). The prevailing trend is a consensus regarding integrating tools into 
daily workplace practices, underscoring the close daily proximity of all three generations to 
technological tools. We observe a significant relationship with the generation variable in almost 
half of the variables (7 out of 17). Notably, the percentage of Generation Z respondents who 
agree with the idea that "access to the Internet and e -mail" is being implemented as a tool in 
their daily workplace practices (55.7%) is lower than that of the other two generations (69.4% 
for Generation Y and 76.3% for Generation X). Conversely, there are five variables with a 
statistically significant relationship to the generation variable in which the agreement 
percentage is consistently lower in Generation X and higher in Generation Z: "There is little or 
no standardization in technology" (53.6%, 56.6%, and 70.5%); "There is little or no technology 
policy" (12.4%, 18.4%, and 26.2%); "The organization has policies and processes for managing 
change" (51.5%, 58.5%, and 75.4%); "The organization has standardized hardware, software, 
and access control to platforms" (50.5%, 60.8%, and 68.9%); "The dedicated technical team 
has in-depth knowledge of the platforms to support the business strategy" (54.6%, 62.3%, and 
68.9%). Other variables follow a similar pattern (lower agreement percentages among 
Generation X and higher percentages among Generation Z), although they do not exhibit 
statistical significance. These include "I use a personal computer connected to a network via 
the Internet." Furthermore, there are variables without a statistically significant relationship 
with the generation variable, indicating a greater alignment between Generations X and Y and 
a divergence from Generation Z. For these variables, G eneration Z consistently shows higher 
agreement percentages. Examples include "Access to systems, data, and infrastructure is 
controlled," "System availability and predictable support levels," and "Development and 
implementation of technology solutions to obtain a competitive advantage that differentiates 
the organization from its peers." In summary, while Generations X and Y exhibit closer 
alignment in their perspectives, Generation Z is the cohort that most strongly agrees that their 
organization possesses the right tools. Still, they need to be utilized more effectively and to their 
full extent. Tables 4.16 and 4.17 partially confirm our fourth hypothesis: Generations X, Y, and 
Z differ in their adoption of the digital workplace based on enterprise integration and 
technological tools usage, with Generation Z exhibiting more distinct views compared to 
Generations X and Y, who share a relatively closer perspective.

Table 4.18 highlights distinctions among generations regarding assessing six items identified 
as predecessors of a successful digital workplace. Pearson's Chi -squared test application 
underscores leadership as a facet that delineates varying perceptions among genera tions. 
Approximately three-quarters of Generation Y, paradoxically, both highly value and devalue 
leadership, while 68.9% of Generation Z and 62.9% of Generation X regard it positively or very 
positively. Autonomy and collaboration, when assessed  using the bivariate correlation test, 
statistically differentiate the three generations. These predecessors and two others (Trust 
between co -workers and Communication) exhibit a positive relationship in evaluation that 
increases with younger generations. In other words, the correlation reveals that the younger the 
generation, the more positively they evaluate these predecessors. It confirms our fifth

A consistent trend in this set of questions is Generation Z's greater detachment and lower 
commitment toward their current organization. Variables such as "Working in my organization 
has great personal significance for me" (76.3%, 69.4%, and 55.7%); "I str ive to work beyond 
what is expected of me" (84.6%, 82.1%, and 62.3%); "I have a strong sense of belonging in my 
organization" (71.0%, 62.3%, and 37.7%); "I believe in the mission and purpose of the 
organization I work for" (83.6%, 70.7%, and 60.7%); "I oft en go beyond what is expected of 
me to help my team succeed" (87.6%, 85.9%, and 59.0%); "I care about the future of the 
organization I work for" (97.0%, 83.1%, and 77.1%); and "I work harder than expected to help 
my organization succeed" (80.4%, 70.7%, and  49.2%) all follow a consistent pattern with 
Generation X indicating the highest agreement and Generation Z the lowest. This trend is 
further reinforced by the reversal of the pattern (Generation Z agreeing the most and Generation 
X the least) in response to the variable "In the last six months, someone at work has spoken to 
me about my progress" (70.1%, 76.8%, and 85.2%). Although not statistically significant, 
several other factors also align with this pattern, such as "I'm willing to make an extra effort  
without being asked" (88.6%, 84.9%, and 78.7%); "I pay a lot of attention to the responsibility 
I have in my work" (89.7%, 89.2%, and 80.3%); and "I am very focused when I am working on 
my activities" (85.6%, 83.0%, and 77.15), all displaying higher agreement levels in Generation 
X and lower agreement levels in Generation Z. Furthermore, certain variables, while not strictly 
conforming to the previous criteria, still underscore the closer alignment between Generations 
X and Y and their more significant divergence from Generation Z. Examples include "In the 
last seven days, I have received recognition or praise for doing a good job" (50.5%, 54.2%, and 
39.4%) and "My supervisor, or someone at work, seems to care about me as a person" (74.2%, 
75.0%, and 65.5%). It partially confirms our sixth hypothesis: Generations X, Y, and Z differ 
in their level of engagement in the organization, with Generation Z exhibiting more distinct 
views than Generations X and Y, who share a relatively closer perspective. 
4.1.2.4  Conclusions from the Confirmatory Study of Our Concept Model 
The influence of generational factors on various behaviors has garnered significant attention 
within the realm of research. This interest is relevant in studies related to perceptions and 
attitudes toward job performance. Disparities in work behavior, perc eptions, and attitudes 
across generations, especially within digital work environments, warrant further investigation. 
Our confirmatory study revealed that there were only sometimes markedly significant 

DSR Design and Development 
82 
 
differences among the three generations regarding engagement, level of commitment, or 
organizational expectations. It is essential to subject the prevailing fascination surrounding 
Millennials and their group ethos to further scrutiny. 
The challenges of designing a digital workplace are amplified by a multigenerational 
workforce's diverse needs and values. This complexity is further compounded by many factors 
influencing employees' perceptions of their work environment. This confirmatory  study has 
explored 73 factors that impact employee engagement in a digital workplace. After data 
analysis, we updated our conceptual model to add two factors related to Enterprise Integration 
and Technology Tools, namely, ‘My organization and IT clearly communicate the policies and 
rules for working in a virtual environment’ and ‘Unique use of AI, Analytics, or Augmented 
reality tools that can fundamentally change a generally accepted business model.’ 
One notable trend observed is Generation Z employees' greater detachment and reduced 
commitment toward their organizations. This paradoxical finding suggests that despite being 
the generation most associated with technology and virtual work environments, G eneration Z 
appears to have less faith in the efficiency of an entirely virtual approach to work. Instead, they 
seem to value face-to-face interactions more. Interestingly, while Generations X and Y exhibit 
more remarkable similarities in their perceptions , Generation Z believes that the organization 
possesses the right tools but may need to use them more optimally. This presents an opportunity 
for leadership to play a crucial role in enhancing the commitment and attachment of Generation 
Z employees. Clear alignment of their roles and responsibilities with organizational goals and 
leveraging their knowledge to improve the digital workplace environment, for instance, 
soliciting their input on better tool utilization, can be instrumental in addressing this 
generation's concerns. In summary, understanding the unique perspectives of different 
generations in the digital workplace is essential, and proactive strategies can help bridge the 
gap and enhance overall employee engagement. 
4.2  Artifact Development 
As technology continues to reshape our work, it becomes imperative to develop frameworks 
and methodologies that adapt to these changes and empower organizations to optimize 
employee engagement within digital environments. Drawing upon the insights gleaned from 
two SLRs, one longitudinal phenomenological study, and one confirmatory investigation, we 
developed the initial proposal of a comprehensive framework and method tailored to meet the 
employee's needs in digital workplaces. Our proposed framework and method incorporate eight 
instruments with practical guidelines and best practices to cultivate and sustain employee 
engagement within the digital workplace.  
The following sub -sections present the initial proposal of our framework and method and 
elucidate the rationale behind each component used in the design of our artifact. 
4.2.1  Framework and Method 
After confirming the impact of various factors outlined in the literature and assessing their 
similarities and differences across different generations, we developed a framework to aid 
organizations in creating engaging digital workplaces for a diverse workforce spanning multiple 
generations. We adapted the model proposed by M. Attaran et al. (2019), which comprises three 
sequential phases - Assess and Design, Build Platform, Adopt, Innovate, Enhance, and 
Transform - to suit our needs. Specifically, we intr oduced a fourth phase to facilitate the 
evaluation of enhancements made in the preceding stages. Moreover, we advocate for a cyclical 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
83 
 
rather than linear approach to this process, aligning with Deming's PDCA cycle (Dudin et al., 
2015) and allowing continuous improvement. We provide a visual representation of our 
proposal in Figure 4.5. 
 
Figure 4.5 - Framework to support the implementation of an engaging digital workplace. 
These four phases encapsulate the digital workplace's implementation, ongoing maintenance, 
and evolution. We propose utilizing some instruments during the execution of each framework 
phase.  
Phase 1: Awareness 
The Awareness phase gathers information to comprehend the current organizational landscape 
and pinpoint the necessary inputs for subsequent phases. Key activities include conducting 
surveys, interviews, and assessments to understand the current state of th e digital workplace, 
identifying pain points, and gathering feedback from employees across different generations. 
Instruments: Initial semi -structured interview (Appendix D, Table D.1), Initial Online 
Questionnaire (Section 4.1.2.1), Workplace Arrangement Model (I1), Virtuality and Enterprise 
Integration levels (I2), Barriers and Enablers (I3), EES (I4), Actor -network Theory (I5), Gens 
X, Y, and Z interests, values, and needs (I6), and Best Practices (I7). 
Phase 2: Design 
The Design phase focuses on scanning and delineating strategies and potential solutions to 
enhance the digital workplace. This involves analyzing the data collected in the Awareness 
phase, identifying trends and patterns, and collaborating with stakeholder s to prioritize and 
develop tailored solutions that address the diverse needs of the multigenerational workforce. 
Instruments: Best Practices (I7) and Agile methodology (I8). 
Phase 3: Build and Adopt 
The Build and Adopt phase entails developing and implementing the solutions outlined and 
prioritized in the preceding phase. This includes deploying new technologies, redesigning 
processes, and providing training and support to ensure smooth employee adoption. During this 


DSR Design and Development 
84 
 
phase, continuous communication and feedback loops are essential to address challenges and 
refine the solutions. 
Instruments: Actor-network Theory (I5) and Agile Framework (I8). 
Phase 4: Evaluate 
Lastly, the Evaluate phase is dedicated to gauging and analyzing the outcomes to facilitate 
continuous improvement. Key metrics such as employee engagement, the maturity level of 
technology usage, and enterprise integration are monitored to assess the effe ctiveness of the 
implemented solutions. Lessons learned are documented, and adjustments are made to refine 
the digital workplace further. 
Instruments: Simplified Online Questionnaire (Section 4.1.2.1), Workplace Arrangement 
Model (I1), Virtuality and Enterprise Integration levels (I2), Barriers and Enablers (I3), and 
EES (I4). 
The second to fourth phases are cyclical for continuous improvement. This iterative approach 
allows for ongoing refinement and adaptation to changing organizational and employee needs 
and technological advancements. Each cycle builds upon the insights gained from the previous 
one, fostering a continuous strategy for improving employee engagement. The Evaluate phase 
can be applied after various cycles of the Design and Build and Adopt phases as the stakeholders 
understand that the solutions implemented have the time to start reflecting results.  
Furthermore, it is essential to understand each stage of the organization's development and align 
the strategy with the stakeholders. According to Panteli et al. (2019), the process of cultivating 
engagement entails employing several strategies: 
● Developing Engagement: Fostering clarity regarding the goals, roles, and tasks, 
alongside facilitating face-to-face meetings whenever feasible, helps to establish a sense 
of purpose and ownership among employees. 
● Supporting Engagement: Promoting open communication regarding the activities’ 
progress, regular updates on members' progress, and fostering peer -to-peer support are 
crucial elements in supporting engagement. Providing resources and tools that facilitate 
collaboration and knowledge sharing further enhances engagement. 
● Nourishing Engagement: Reflecting on lessons learned, commemorating achievements, 
and fostering connections are essential for nurturing engagement within the workforce. 
Celebrating milestones, recognizing contributions, and promoting a sense of community 
foster a positive work environment and motivate employees to stay engaged in the 
digital workplace initiative. 
Figure 4.6 depicts the operationalization of the framework's application process, which 
commences with the Awareness phase. The different instruments, represented as sheets with 
folded corners, serve as tools for the various tasks.

number of cycles undertaken can be adjusted based on team availability or achieving the desired

Phase 2: Design 
During the Design phase, the team must define the relevant items to improve the team's 
engagement based on the conflicts or barriers identified during the previous phase. The details on 
how to perform Phase 2 - Design are described below: 
2.1. Define the OPP ( Obligatory Passage Point)  and common procedures to establish teams’ 
stability, as suggested by ANT. 
2.1.1. Examine the network, conflicts, or problems identified during the previous phase, 
considering their immediate causes and underlying systemic factors.  
2.1.2. Understand the network of actors involved and their interactions; targeted 
interventions can be devised to mitigate conflicts and foster a more stable team 
environment.  
2.1.3. Tailor the challenges identified within the organization that may promote stability. 


DSR Design and Development 
90 
 
Recommendations: Understanding the existing conflicts and identifying the OPP will 
help define the most relevant information to analyze and prioritize in the following steps. 
This step is optional and may be skipped depending on the organization's objectives, such 
as a quicker initial diagnostic. 
Support tools or instruments: Actor-Network theory (I5). 
Input: SWOT and employee’s network built in the previous phase. 
Output: OPP definition. 
2.2. Analyze and define the alternative solutions to achieve the common goal.   
2.2.1. Assess the Best Practices (I7) list to identify which items should support the OPP 
achievement.  
2.2.2. Understand the high-level benefits and risks of each one of the alternatives. 
2.2.3. Identify critical areas necessary to allocate resources to achieve the OPP. However, 
it is crucial to recognize that prioritizing an item should only occur if it is feasible to secure 
the necessary resources for building and implementing the proposed changes. 
Support tools or instruments: Actor-Network theory (I5) and Best Practices (I7). 
Input: OPP and identified best practices. 
Output: Best Practices updated. 
2.3. Prioritize and detail the solution to achieve the common goal. 
2.3.1. Evaluate the alternative solutions based on their feasibility, effectiveness, and 
alignment with organizational goals. This ensures the  chosen approach is well -suited to 
address the prioritized item and drive positive outcomes for the team and the organization. 
2.3.2. Detail the prioritized solution to define the scope. 
Recommendations: Consider agile methodologies to promote flexibility, collaboration, 
and iterative problem -solving throughout the design and implementation. By embracing 
agile principles such as continuous improvement and adaptive planning, organizations can 
respond effec tively to changing circumstances and optimize the impact of their 
interventions. 
Support tools or instruments: Actor-Network theory (I5) and Agile methodology (I8). 
Input: Best Practices (I7). 
Output: Alternative solution prioritized and detailed. 
After concluding the Design phase, the team will have the information needed to develop and 
implement the prioritized solution. The details on how to perform Phase 3 - Build and Adopt are 
described below: 
Phase 3: Build and Adopt 
During the Build and Adopt phase, the involved resources will develop and implement the solution 
outlined and prioritized in the preceding phase.  
1.1. Involve the stakeholders to align the prioritized solution and interests. 
1.1.1. Meet with the stakeholders to present the defined solution. 
1.1.2. Align the necessary resources to build and implement the solution. 
1.1.3. Align the plan and next steps.

Building upon the preceding step, stakeholders can determine whether to proceed with the project 
to continuously enhance the implementation of engaging digital workplaces. This decision -
making process is contingent upon various factors, including resource availability, organizational 
priorities, and the effectiveness of previous cycles. By deliberating on the analysis's outcomes and 
evaluating the organization's current state, stakeholders can make informed decisions regarding 
the necessity and feasibility of initiating subsequent cycles. 
The following section provides detailed information on the proposed instruments in the method 
procedures. 
4.2.2   Instruments of the Framework and Method 
The following sub-sections delineate the foundational instruments developed throughout our DSR 
project to bolster the artifacts: a framework and method for fostering engaging digital workplaces 
tailored for a multigenerational workforce. These instruments support the execution of the 
proposed framework and method procedu res. Each component is designed to address key facets 
of the digital work environment, namely, the workplace arrangement model, the degree of 
virtuality and enterprise in integration, barriers and enablers perceived in the digital environment, 
employee eng agement, the networks and diverse actors relevant in a digital environment; the 
different values and needs from a multi -generational workforce, best practices, and Agile 
methodology. Through a systematic exposition of these instruments, this section aims t o provide 
a detailed description of the theoretical underpinnings, methodological considerations, and 
practical implications undergirding the development and implementation of the proposed 
framework and method. 
4.2.2.1  Workplace Arrangement Model (I1) 
As organizations embrace virtuality and adopt digital technologies, the number of systems, 
applications, and platforms they use increases. These disparate systems may include 
communication tools, project management software, and customer relationship manag ement 
systems. Integration is necessary to connect and synchronize these systems to ensure smooth 
operations and effective collaboration in a virtual environment. The workplace arrangements as a 
function of virtuality and enterprise integration (Figure 4.8 ) were developed to bridge a gap 
identified in the literature. As highlighted by Attaran et al. (2019), "[c] ompanies that are not 
adopting an integrated approach [...] are failing to capitalize on significant opportunities digital 
workplace could deliver".  

DSR Design and Development 
94 
 
 
Figure 4.8 - Workplace arrangement model. 
The physical workplace quadrant represents the traditional scenario where employees share a 
typical office space (Mukherjee et al., 2012). It is characterized by a low level of virtuality and a 
high level of enterprise integration. In such organizations, e mployees need access to company -
provided devices like laptops or cell phones to work outside the physical workspace. Conversely, 
a remote work model is implemented when an organization enables some employees to work 
remotely using devices like laptops or c ell phones but with limited access to information. This 
model exhibits low levels of virtuality and enterprise integration, with employees relying solely 
on electronic communication to connect with colleagues and needing more face-to-face interaction 
(Santana & Cobo, 2020). Remote workers often work independently and have limited access to 
information and collaboration with their co -located office team (Deshpande et al., 2016). In 
contrast, the virtual workplace quadrant involves IT -mediated communication t o facilitate 
collaboration between employees in different geographic locations (Park et al., 2015). In a virtual 
workplace, employees heavily rely on technological platforms to share information and complete 
interdependent tasks (Guinea et al., 2012). This  environment is characterized by a high level of 
virtuality but a low level of enterprise integration. The organization provides the necessary 
equipment to all employees, regardless of their physical location. Although geographical distance 
between team members is not a barrier, effective knowledge sharing, trust, and collaboration can 
be hindered by different time zones and communication challenges. Finally, the digital workplace 
aims to provide a comprehensive solution that enables enterprise integration and fosters 
socialization between team members, knowledge sharing, trust, and collaboration. It seamlessly 
connects people, processes, and technology throughout the organization, allowing employees to 
work from anywhere without needing a shared physical sp ace. The digital workplace offers a 
digitally driven experience characterized by high virtuality and enterprise integration. A company 
is considered to adopt a digital workplace when people, technology platforms, and processes from 
different departments ar e seamlessly connected, eliminating compromises in their activities 
compared to the physical workplace. 
The following subsections provide the necessary information to identify the level of enterprise 
integration and virtuality and, consequently, determine an organization's or team's workplace 
arrangement model. 
 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
95 
 
4.2.2.2  The Virtuality and Enterprise Integration Levels (I2) 
All four workplace arrangement models share virtuality level as a defining element. Scholars have 
described virtuality as a feature that sets virtual teams apart from traditional face -to-face teams 
(Ferretti, 2016). Virtuality is a multifaceted concept, wi th authors mentioning two to six 
dimensions, including (1) geographic or spatial separation, (2) differences in time zones or work 
schedules, (3) variations in organizational structures, (4) cultural divergence, (5) the extent of in-
person interactions, and (6) technology utilization (Ferretti, 2016). To focus on the scope of our 
research question, we excluded the temporal, organizational, and cultural dimensions. To 
thoroughly examine the cultural aspect, it is essential to conduct targeted research on digital work 
that delves into the various stages of each country's digital transformation journey.  
We consider three dimensions: the first is the geographic or spatial distance, the physical distance 
that exists between employees who work in different locations (Kirkman et al., 2002; Schweitzer 
& Duxbury, 2010). This information shall be collected during Phase 1: Awareness, with the initial 
semi-structured interview with the organization's stakeholders (Appendix D, Table D.1). The 
second dimension is the proportion of time that team members have in -person interactions vs. 
virtual ones. It highlights the relevance of promoting face -to-face interactions in some moments 
(Hambley et al., 2007; Derven, 2016; Stratone, 2019). Many studies discuss maintaining face-to-
face interactions to build trust and manage conflicts, especially during team formation (Kauppila 
et al., 2011; Stratone, 2019; Adamovic, 2018). This information shall be collected during Phase 
1: Awareness, with the Online Questionnaire (Table 4.8, question T.3). 
The third dimension is the relative degree of the use of technology (Gilson et al., 2015). To assess 
the degree of technology usage, we propose an adaptation of the IT Value Hierarchy (Urwiler & 
Frolick, 2008), represented in Figure 4.9. 
 
Figure 4.9 - IT Value Hierarchy, adapted from Maslow’s hierarchy model. 
We used the five levels and criteria that Urwiler and Frolick (2008) proposed and associated them 
with the tools and technologies identified during our SLR on DW and related to each level. Table 
4.20 details the rubric we propose to measure the technology usage dimension of the virtuality 
construct. This information must be collected during Phase 1. Awareness , and 4. Evaluate with 
the Online Questionnaire (Table 4.9, question O.7). The alternatives presented as responses to this 
question in the survey are described in the "virtuality enablers" column of the table below. Three 
of these alternatives have been modified to ensure consistency in measurement using the 3 -point 
Likert-type scale from 1 to 3, namely 'Little, if any, IT standardization' in the questionnaire is 
presented as 'IT standardization is in place,' 'Few, if any, IT policies' in the questionnaire i s 
presented as 'Comprehensive IT policies are established,' and 'Dedicated IT resources provide 
reactive technical support' in the questionnaire is presented as 'Dedicated technical staff provides 
proactive technical support.' 


DSR Design and Development 
96 
 
Table 4.20 - Rubric proposed for technology usage, adapted from Urwiler & Frolick (2008). 
The combination of technology usage, geographic distance, and the proportion of time that team 
members spend in -person interactions provides the virtuality level to classify the workplace 
arrangement model.  
Enterprise integration involves connecting and integrating various systems, applications, and 
processes to enable seamless communication, collaboration, and data sharing. Drawing from the 
different contributions in the extant literature, we propose a rubri c to measure enterprise 
Level of 
Tech Usage 
Type of Technology or 
Tool 
Virtuality Enablers

integration, as shown in Table 4.21. This information shall be collected during Phase 1: 
Awareness, with the Online Questionnaire (Table 4.9, question O.6). 
Table 4.21 - Rubric proposed to measure Enterprise Integration. 
Enterprise integration is crucial in a digital workplace. It enables seamless communication, 
collaboration, and data sharing across different systems, applications, and departments. By 
fostering interoperability among the various systems and processes present in the enterprise, it 
enables a comprehensive understanding of the most beneficial processes, especially in a virtual 
environment (Ortiz et al., 1999). 
The combination of virtuality level and enterprise integration level determines the model of 
workplace arrangements detailed in the previous section. Both are classified using a 3 -point 
Likert-type scale from 1 to 3. Enterprise integration is calculated using the average of the collected 
responses for this topic. While the virtuality level consists of three dimensions, one is technology 
usage, which is calculated with the average of the collected responses for this topic. For 
calculation, the average frequency the employees meet their co -workers face-to-face, consider 3 
for ‘Rarely,’ 2.5 for ‘Once a month,’ 2 for ‘It is not a regular,’ 1.5 for ‘1 -2 times a week’, and 1 
for ‘More than two times a week.’ Regarding the geographic or spatial distance, it should be 
considered 1 when the entire team is collected in the same office or offices in the same city, 2 
when the team is spread in different offices but the same country, and 3 when the team is spread 
globally. The virtuality level is calculated by SUM(AVG(technology usage) + AVG(FtF) + 
AVG(Distance))/3. 
4.2.2.3  Barriers and Enablers (I3) 
Using the data gathered from the SLR on DW and employee engagement, we could understand 
the positive and negative influence of some factors on trust, leadership, knowledge sharing, 
communication, autonomy, and collaboration. Figure 4.10 consolidates the known barriers and 
enablers of a digital workplace scattered throughout the existing literature. These barriers and 
enablers influence the six digital workplace predecessors, conseque ntly affecting employee 
engagement.

Figure 4.10 - Barriers and Enablers in a digital workplace. 
During the phenomenological study, we identified three additional barriers experienced only 
during extreme situations, such as COVID -19: anxiety, difficulty keeping a work routine, and 
difficulty accommodating job tasks with the child’s care and home -schooling. These barriers 
should be considered in future studies if applied during crises. This information shall be collected 
during Phases 1 and 4, Awareness and Evaluate, with the Online Questionnaire (Table 4.9, 
questions O.4 and O.5), and it is classified using a 3-point Likert-type scale from 1 to 3. 
4.2.2.4  Employee Engagement Scale (I4) 
Employee engagement is a work -related positive psychological state constructed by cognitive, 
emotional, and behavioral energy (Shuck et al., 2017). The EES comprises 12 questions, 
considering four for each dimension (cognitive, emotional, and behavioral) ( Shuck et al., 2017). 
In 2017, Gallup proposed a different scale. It considered the basic needs, individual needs, 
teamwork needs, and personal growth to measure employee engagement (Gallup, 2013). We 
propose using the EES adapted with some of the Gallup Q12 questions to include individual needs 
as the measurement tool, as detailed in Table 4.22. 
During the Awareness phase, the EES should be dispatched with an online questionnaire to 
identify the level of employee engagement before and after the project. At the end of each cycle 
of the framework, the EES should be dispatched again to the employees to analyze the 
effectiveness of the implemented solution on the level of employee engagement. The responses 
are collected using a 5 -point Likert -type scale ranging from 'excellent/strongly agree' to 'very 
poor/strongly disagree.' Suppose there is a high number of respondents. In that case,  we 
recommend performing a factor analysis to characterize and differentiate the respondents into 
three or four groups: actively disengaged, disengaged, engaged, and very engaged. If the number 
of respondents is not statistically relevant, we recommend seg regating them into three or four 
groups using cut-offs for the scale by the maximum and minimum scores.

4.2.2.5  Actor-network Theory (I5) 
We propose the use of ANT during phase 1. Awareness, 2. Design, and 3. Build and Adopt our 
framework and method; its theoretical background is provided in section 2.2. To effectively apply 
ANT during the Awareness phase, the critical focus is mapping actors, networks, and conflicts. 
Utilizing the insights gathered from the online questionnaire, it is possible to understand human 
and digital elements, their interactions, and their impacts. The questions I.6 and O.7 presented in 
Tables 4.7 and 4.9 provide the necessary information. Additional documents or interviews can be 
requested to understand the existing actors, networks, and conflicts clearly. By employing ANT, 
it is possible to visualize employees' networks within the digital workplace, mapping out the 
diverse actors involved. This enables the identification of environmental conflicts or issu es, 
ensuring a comprehensive understanding of the system dynamics. 
During the Design phase, it is recommended that the OPP be defined and procedures established 
for team stability. Building on insights from the previous phase, network conflicts and underlying 
systemic factors can be examined. By understanding the network of actors and their interactions, 
targeted interventions can be devised to mitigate existing conflicts in the team environment. 
Leveraging ANT and input from SWOT (Strengths, Weaknesses, Opportunities, and Threats) 
analysis, it is possible to define the OPP and tailor challenges within the organization to overcome 
the identified conflicts in the network. Understanding existing conflicts and determining the OPP 
supports prioritization in subsequent steps. 
In the Build and Adopt phase, it is necessary to analyze alternative solutions to achieve the 
common goal and mobilize necessary resources. By assessing best practices and understanding 
their benefits and risks, it is possible to identify critical areas fo r resource allocation. ANT and 
Best Practices (I7) guide this process, ensuring that the prioritization of solutions aligns with 
resource availability. Effective communication and negotiation with stakeholders, supported by 
ANT, facilitate the mobilization  of resources for successful solution adoption. Through this 
approach, we support the design of the digital workplace in a manner that optimizes engagement. 
4.2.2.6  Generations X, Y, and Z's interests, values, and needs (I6) 
To address the best practices, mitigate the perceived barriers, and promote enablers in the digital 
workplace, we combined the information gathered in the second SLRs and the confirmatory study 
regarding the interests, values, and of generations X, Y, and Z to indicate the most relevant barriers 
and enablers for the three generations (detailed in Table 4.23). This information shall be used 
during the Awareness phase to confirm if each Generation's barriers and enablers identified in the 
online survey are consistent (step 1.8).
