4.2.2.7  Best Practices (I7) 
To be aligned with the organization's strategy to foster employee engagement, we 
complemented instrument I6, presented in Section 4.2.2.6, with the strategy (Develop 
Engagement, Support Engagement, or Nourish Engagement) that may be more suitable to 
mitigate each barrier or promote each enabler presented in Table 4.24. Differentiating between 
the three, 'developing' engagement entails cultivating it over time. Usually, the initial or 
essential actions are necessary to keep the minimum level of employee enga gement. 
'Supporting' engagement involves providing resources to maintain current levels, while 
'nourishing' engagement fosters a nurturing environment for sustained and improved 
engagement. The recommendations provided in Table 4.24 serve as a guide accord ing to the 
selected strategy. However, practices recommended in previous stages of engagement may be 
considered if they were identified as relevant by the team.  
To effectively apply the Best Practices component (I7) during the Awareness and Design 
phases, it is recommended first to confirm employees' perceived barriers and enablers by 
generation (step 1.8). Additionally, confirming the SWOT analysis with the team involved and 
discussing possible solutions ensures comprehensive input (step 1.9). Finally, updating the best 
practices list with possible improvements/solutions according to team needs and priorities 
ensures alignment with the common goal (step 1.10). In the Design phase, analyzing and 
defining alternative solutions involves assessing the best practices list, filtering according to the 
strategy, understanding benefits and risks, and identifying key resource allocation areas (step 
2.2). According to Panteli et al. (2019), the process of cultivating engagement entails employing 
three strategies: 
● Developing Engagement: Use this approach when engagement is low or when starting a new 
project or initiative. Developing Engagement focuses on building a foundation for engagement 
from the ground up. The practices listed in Table 4.24 associated with this strategy involve 
creating an environment that encourages participation and commitment, often through clear 
communication of goals, expectations, and the value of the project to individuals and the 
organization. 
● Supporting Engagement: Implement this approach when moderate engagement levels  need 
sustaining and strengthening. Supporting Engagement aims to maintain and enhance existing 
levels of engagement. The practices listed in Table 4.24 associated with this strategy involve 
providing ongoing support and resources, recognizing and rewarding contributions, and 
addressing any emerging challenges that may hinder continued engagement. It's about keeping 
the momentum going. 
● Nourishing Engagement: Apply this approach when engagement is high to ensure it remains 
strong and grows even further. Nourishing Engagement focuses on enriching and deepening the 
engagement of already committed individuals. The practices listed in Table 4.24 associated 
with this strategy involve offering advanced opportunities for professional growth, fostering a 
strong sense of community, and providing platforms for individuals to take on leadership roles 
and contribute meaningfully. 
These approaches help tailor strategies to the organization's current engagement level, ensuring 
appropriate actions to foster a highly engaged and motivated workforce.

preferences, and

(Bencsik et al., 2016)

and 
Support 
The proposed best practices should mitigate the negative effects of the barriers and positively 
influence the enablers. Previous studies suggest flexible communication methods, 
understanding the employees, and encouraging positive relationships as alternat ives to soften 
the gap between generations (Westover et al., 2020). A previous study identified that leadership 
has a significant role in employee engagement (Gallup, 2022). The feeling of “unfair treatment 
at work,” unmanageable workload, unclear communication from managers, lack of manager 

DSR Design and Development 
108 
 
support, and unreasonable time pressure are responsible for 70% of the variance in engagement 
(Gallup, 2022). Leadership significantly influences the implementation and adoption of some 
of the practices listed in Table 4.24, such as work predictability, feedback, and communication. 
So, organizations should put effort into hiring and training appropriate managers to support the 
implementation of an engaging digital workplace. 
Training and development, flexible working hours, and cash bonuses are some of the most 
valued benefits (apart from salary) (Pouchová, 2011). Although some practices may be more 
relevant to a particular generation, such as career development being more rel evant to Gen Z 
(Bencsik et al., 2016), the practices presented can be applied to employees regardless of their 
generation. This is because barriers and enablers can be perceived differently because of one's 
generation and the nature of the job, financial situation, spousal and family support, and unique 
personal circumstances (Koekemoer & Crafford, 2019). Additionally, age and gender may 
affect the benefits preferences (Gladwell et al., 2010).  
4.2.2.8  Agile Methodology (I8) 
A substantial body of literature delves into global software engineering for distributed Agile 
teams, examining its advantages and disadvantages. Communication, collaboration, tool 
support, and large team size are cited as the primary challenges of Scrum p ractices. 
Communication hurdles often stem from cultural and linguistic disparities, exacerbated by slow 
or unreliable network connections. Collaboration may need to improve due to inadequate tools 
for managing tasks, bugs, and backlogs, particularly for t eams spanning multiple time zones. 
On the other hand, practices like stand -up meetings, sprints/iterations, and continuous 
integration have successful results (Deshpande et al., 2016). 
Initially crafted for small projects within agile software development, Agile frameworks were 
tailored for single-team execution. However, their demonstrated benefits, including accelerated 
delivery, enhanced product quality, and heightened customer satisfaction, have spurred interest 
in extending this methodology to larger contexts. This interest has given rise to scaling agile 
frameworks aiming to support agile practices at an organizational level and address 
coordination and dependencies across multiple teams. Notable examples include the Scaled 
Agile Framework (SAFe), Large-Scale Scrum (LeSS), and Disciplined Agile Delivery (DAD), 
with SAFe being widely regarded as the most popular, according to the 12th State of Agile 
survey (Hinterberg & Hoffman, 2018) . SAFe, for instance, is engineered for project 
management, planning, and development using Scrum, Extreme Programming (XP) practices, 
and potentially incorporating Kanban or hybrids of these methodologies (Hinterberg & 
Hoffman, 2018).  
Agile frameworks and methodologies play a crucial role in supporting virtual or hybrid teams 
where members operate both remotely and on -site. In today's dynamic work environment, 
characterized by distributed teams and flexible work arrangements, agile approaches provide a 
structured framework for collaboration, communication, and adaptability (Deshpande et al., 
2016). Teams benefit from Agile's emphasis on frequent communication, iterative 
development, and rapid feedback cycles. These principles help bridge the gap between remote 
and co-located team members, fostering a cohesive working environment regardless of physical 
location (Hinterberg et al., 2018). Moreover, agile methodologies offer flexibility to 
accommodate varying time zones and working schedules  within hybrid teams. Agile 
frameworks promote autonomy and accountability among team members, regardless of 
geographic dispersion, by focusing on deliverables and empowering self -organizing teams 
(Deshpande et al., 2016). 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
109 
 
Agile methodology is not mandatory when applying our proposed framework and method. 
However, during the artifact demonstration and evaluation phases, we noticed that the teams 
already familiar with Agile practices and ceremonies had a quicker setup and qui ckly 
understood the proposed procedures. Besides the similarity of the cyclical and interactive 
processes applied in Agile and our framework, other practices and ceremonies are 
recommended. 
During the Design phase of our framework and method, the backlog grooming technique can 
be used to support step 2.3, Prioritizing and detailing the solution to achieve the common goal. 
Sprint Planning and Stand -Up meetings can be used during the Build and Adopt phase, steps 
3.2 and 3.4, supporting the plan and management of the necessary activities to build the 
prioritized solution. Furthermore, the Demo or review and retrospective can be used to present 
the evolution of the developed solution and gather fe edback for continuous improvement and 
lessons learned. The details on the objective of some practices and ceremonies recommended 
to be used when applying our framework and method are described below: 
Stand-Up Meetings: These are brief daily meetings in which team members discuss progress, 
plans, and any obstacles they face. They help keep everyone aligned and informed (Deshpande 
et al., 2016). 
Sprint Planning: This meeting marks the beginning of a sprint in Agile development. The team 
plans the work during the sprint and sets the sprint goal (Deshpande et al., 2016). 
Retrospective: At the end of a sprint, the team holds a retrospective meeting to reflect on what 
went well, what did not, and what can be improved. It is a crucial practice for continuous 
improvement (Deshpande et al., 2016). 
Demo or Review: At the end of a sprint, the team showcases the work completed during the 
sprint to stakeholders and other team members. It is an opportunity for feedback and validation 
(Deshpande et al., 2016). 
Backlog Grooming involves refining and prioritizing items in the product backlog to ensure 
they are ready for inclusion in future sprints (Deshpande et al., 2016). 
These Agile practices and ceremonies help teams stay organized, focused, and adaptable. 
4.3  Conclusion 
This chapter has presented a comprehensive overview of this study's research design and 
development process, focusing mainly on DSR’s Phase 3: Design and Development. By 
utilizing the DSRM model proposed by Peffers et al. (2007) and the conceptual model 
developed based on 75 identified factors, this research has explored the complexities involved 
in a digital workplace to understand how these factors are perceived by a multi -generational, 
considering their different values and needs, and consequently affect ing the employee 
engagement.  
The confirmatory study conducted as part of this research project provided valuable insights 
into the impact of various factors on employee engagement across different generational 
cohorts. Contrary to some prevailing assumptions, the study revealed nuance d differences 
among generations, particularly highlighting the unique perspectives of Generation Z 
employees. Despite their familiarity with technology, Generation Z preferred face -to-face 
interactions and expressed concerns about utilizing digital tools within the workplace. Drawing 
from the longitudinal phenomenological and confirmatory studies and the two SLRs, a 

DSR Design and Development 
110 
 
comprehensive framework and method have been proposed to support organizations in creating 
engaging digital environments for a diverse workforce.  
A digital workplace should offer employees an integrated and all -encompassing work 
experience that is on par with traditional physical workplace setups. Integrating people, 
procedures, and technology should facilitate teams establishing trust, exchanging k nowledge, 
resolving conflicts, and effectively collaborating. To enable this aim, we have expanded upon 
the research conducted by Attaran et al. (2019) and formulated a four-phase iterative approach 
for designing a digital workplace. This framework and met hod encompass four phases - 
Awareness, Design, Build and Adopt, and Evaluate - adopting a cyclical approach inspired by 
Deming's PDCA cycle for continuous improvement. 
The first phase is Awareness, which involves mapping individual, team, and organizational 
characteristics and existing processes, technology usage, and employee relationships. This 
phase aims to gain a thorough understanding of the current state of the workplace. The second 
phase, Design, defines strategies and potential solutions to enhance the current workplace and 
transition towards a digital workplace. This phase involves identifying areas for improvement 
and envisioning the desired future state. The th ird phase is Build, which is dedicated to 
developing and implementing the solutions identified in the Design phase. This includes 
deploying the necessary technologies, configuring systems, and establishing new processes to 
support the digital workplace. The fourth phase, Evaluate, is an addition to the model proposed 
by Attaran et al. (2019). This phase involves assessing and improving the outcomes of the 
previous phases. It includes monitoring key performance indicators, gathering employee 
feedback, and making necessary adjustments to optimize the digital workplace experience. By 
following this iterative approach, organizations can create a digital workplace that aligns with 
their specific needs and maximizes the benefits of technology integration. It ensures a design 
process that enco mpasses the entire employee journey and continuously improves the digital 
workplace. 
Furthermore, the proposed process includes detailed procedures for applying the framework in 
a real organization. It incorporates various instruments, including the Workplace Arrangement 
Model, Virtuality and Enterprise Integration levels, Barriers and Enablers, EES, Best Practices, 
and Agile Framework, to facilitate the implementation and sustainability of digital workplaces 
conducive to fostering employee engagement. The instruments provide guidelines previously 
identified in the literature that support a n iterative approach to designing a digital workplace. 
These guidelines aim to provide practical and evidence -based recommendations for 
practitioners and researchers in the relevant field.

 
111 
 
Chapter 5                                           
DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
After designing and developing the framework and method with detailed processes and 
procedures, this Chapter presents the application and evaluation of the outcomes of our DSR 
project in two real organizations. It offers in-depth insights into our DSR project's Phases 4 and 
5 (Demonstration and Evaluation). We start by outlining the structured approach adopted for 
evaluating the developed artifacts, focusing on their effectiveness, usability, feasibility, and 
scalability. The evaluation design also describe s participants' selection and ethical 
considerations to ensure the integrity and reliability of the process. 
The following section details the demonstration of the artifacts in a real organization through 
iterative cycles. It involves the data collection, analysis, and findings of the two demonstration 
cycles performed with distinct teams from the same company. F urthermore, we present the 
insights and improvements in our framework and method derived from the demonstration 
cycles. 
Next, we present the data gathering, analysis, and results of the method’s evaluation with two 
teams from the second organization involved in this research. We evaluated the developed 
artifacts' usability, feasibility, and scalability, considering their po tential for broader adoption 
and implementation across diverse organizational contexts. 
Finally, we identify the challenges encountered and lessons learned throughout the evaluation 
process, providing valuable insights for future research and practice in digital workplace 
implementation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
112 
 
5.1  Demonstration and Evaluation Design 
Generally, an evaluation design is not characterized by a specific approach but by its objective. 
The empirical research methods used to assess a concept and develop an evaluation strategy 
must be applied systematically (Lau, 2022). Another crucial step to ensure objectivity is 
defining a transparent procedure. We utilized the FEDS framework by Venable et al. (2016) , 
as detailed in Chapter 2 (Section 2.3.4). The process of strategy development encompassed four 
steps: Explanation of the evaluation objectives, Selection of evaluation strategies, Identification 
of properties to be evaluated, and Design of individual eva luation episodes (Venable et al., 
2016). 
The first step involved describing the evaluation's objectives concerning accuracy, risk 
reduction, uncertainty, ethics, and efficiency (Venable et al., 2016). Ensuring accuracy implies 
efficiency and effectiveness, demonstrating that the artifact produces  the intended results. In 
practical artifact usage, there is a risk of misalignment with the intended use or social context, 
potentially leading to ineffectiveness or further issues (Venable et al., 2016). Ethically, it is 
essential to ensure that study pa rticipants face no disadvantages from using the artifact. An 
efficient evaluation balances objectives and available resources, considering the limited time 
frame. In this context, the goal is to assess whether the developed method supports 
organizations implementing engaging digital workplaces, with defined requirements guiding 
the evaluation. During the Demonstrate and Evaluate phases of our DSR project, we focus on 
the method’s validation . Since  it is derived from the framework, this process inherently 
validates it. 
Moving to the second step, selecting the overall evaluation strategy is imperative. The "Human 
Risk & Effectiveness" strategy is recommended for the artifact under consideration. This 
strategy evaluates long -term effectiveness in real -world usage, where re al users heavily 
influence the artifact. Additionally, it allows for a naturalistic evaluation in a real-world context 
(Venable et al., 2016). The temporal focus here is primarily formative, meaning interventions 
or adjustments based on evaluation findings aim to enhance the artifact's alignment with defined 
requirements.  
The third step of the FEDS process involves determining the properties to evaluate, tailored to 
the evaluation's purpose and situation (Venable et al., 2016). In this case, the artifact includes 
several instruments and procedures elaborated in detail in Ch apter 4.3. Finally, the fourth step 
encompasses designing individual evaluation episodes. Initially, method conditions are 
identified, analyzed, and prioritized, leading to the definition of evaluation episodes and a multi-
stage evaluation design. The chos en evaluation strategy, "Human Risk & Effectiveness," 
primarily employs naturalistic methods like case studies in real-world environments.  
The final step involved the design of the specific evaluation episodes. We choose the method 
to be applied in each episode according to Hevner et al. (2004) taxonomy that categorizes 12 
evaluation methods for DSR into five groups: observational (case study, field study), analytical 
(static analysis, architecture analysis, optimization, dynamic testing), experimental (controlled 
experiment, simulation), testing (functional, structural), and descriptive (informed argument, 
scenarios). Table 4.25 summarizes the evaluation categories and methods applied in this study. 
In addition, we classified the evaluation methods to indicate the type of the method used and 
the timeframe when it was applied, as proposed by Venable et al. (2016). The columns ‘Type’ 
and ‘Time’ presented in Table 4.25 differentiate between ex -ante or formative evaluation, 

DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
 
113 
 
which occurs before the construction of the artifact, and ex -post or summative evaluation, 
which happens after the artifact is built. It also informs if the method is naturalistic, like field 
settings, or artificial, like laboratory settings.

We considered the following steps for designing each cycle of validation: 
1. Identify and Analyze the Constraints in the Environment: Assess the available resources 
such as time, people, budget, and research site. Determine which resources are in short 
supply and must be used sparingly. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
114 
 
2. Prioritize the Contextual Factors: Prioritize the identified factors to determine which 
aspects are essential, more important, less important, nice to have, and irrelevant. 
3. Decide a Plan: Determine the number of evaluation episodes and decide when and how 
each episode shall be conducted. The outcome should answer the following questions: 
Who is doing what, and when? 
The evaluation is performed in one round of DSR and adopts a combination of iterative cycles. 
Each cycle used different methods for data collection and analysis and was conducted at 
different times and with other participants in the data collection. Each cycle provides valuable 
feedback for redesigning the following evaluation cycle, as presented in Figure 5.1.  
 
Figure 5.1 - Evaluation cycles, adapted from Venable et al. (2016). 
After conducting the phenomenological and confirmatory studies in Section 4.1, we 
demonstrated the artifact by performing two cycles of demonstration case studies with different 
teams within a financial sector company. This allowed us to refine the process, procedures, and 
guidelines while validating its utility before the final evaluation in another organization from 
the business sector. After each evaluation cycl e, the new findings were used to improve and 
verify the artifact. The individual results of the data collection and analysis, obtained through a 
mixed-methods approach, were also incorporated into a summary evaluation of the artifact. 
Combining quantitative and qualitative data collection and analysis serves as triangulation to 
expand the potential gain of knowledge and mutually validate the results (Flick, 2011).  
In summary, evaluating the designed framework and method to implement an engaging DW 
tailored for a multigenerational workforce employed a Human Risk and effectiveness 
evaluation strategy. This strategy combines formative and summative evaluations to ensure the 
utility and rigor of the artifact. FEDS guided the evaluation process to ensure it was relevant, 
rigorous, and scientific. By following the outlined steps, the evaluation provided valuable 
insights into the effectiveness and utility of the designed a rtifact in real -world organizational 
settings, addressing both design uncertainties and human and social risks.  
 


DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
 
115 
 
5.1.1  Participants and their Recruitment 
The researchers identified three organizations where the artifact's validation could be 
conducted. Drawing upon these established connections, the participant's selection process 
prioritized organizations from the business sector that worked in a virtual or hybrid 
environment, ensuring comprehensive feedback and validation of the artifact's functionality and 
effectiveness. The objective was to validate the primary outcome of this thesis in a real 
organizational environment since its purpose is to solve a real organizational problem, 
supporting companies in bolstering engagement within digital workplaces.  
These criteria were defined following the research question: organizations from the business 
sector with a digital environment and virtual or hybrid settings. After contacting the companies, 
one of them declined to collaborate in evaluating the artifact du e to internal restructuring. The 
other two companies selected participants according to availability, and each selected two 
different teams with similar sizes and activities to participate.  
5.1.2  Ethical Considerations 
Ethical considerations are paramount in any research endeavor, particularly when engaging 
with real organizations. We delineated the ethical guidelines, ensuring informed consent and 
participant privacy, respect for organizational policies and norms, minim ization of harm, data 
collection and analysis integrity, and accountability and transparency of the research process. 
Before engaging with any individuals within the organizations under study, we obtained 
informed consent from all participants. Comprehensive information regarding the purpose and 
procedures of participation was provided. Participants were assured of their anonymity and 
confidentiality throughout the research process. It is important to note that no financial 
incentives were provided to the participants. Moreover, steps were taken to secure any sensitive 
information obtained during interviews or interactions. Additionally, the organizational policies 
and norms were upheld throughout the research process. Efforts were made to collaborate with 
organizational stakeholders transparently, ensuring alignment with the organization's 
objectives. Every effort was made to minimize h arm to individuals and organizations 
participating in the study. Participants were provided avenues for feedback  in case of any 
adverse consequences arising from their involvement in the research. 
Adherence to rigorous methodological standards upheld data collection and analysis integrity. 
Data collection procedures were designed to ensure the findings' accuracy, reliability, and 
validity. Transparency in data analysis was maintained through documen tation of analytical 
processes, coding schemes, and decision trails, allowing for auditability and reproducibility of 
results. 
Accountability and transparency were central tenets guiding the research process. Any conflicts 
of interest or biases were acknowledged and addressed transparently. The roles and 
responsibilities of researchers, participants, and other stakeholders were cl early defined, and 
mechanisms for accountability were established to ensure the ethical conduct of the research. 
This research adhered to ethical principles and guidelines to safeguard participants' rights and 
well-being, uphold the integrity of the research process , and maintain trust and credibility 
within the organizational contexts under study. Through conscientious attention to ethical 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
116 
 
considerations, this research aimed to contribute responsibly to both theory and practice in the 
field of IS. 
5.2  Demonstration of the Method  
This DSR project's demonstration phase commenced with identifying the organization slated to 
evaluate the artifact. The research milieu was situated within a financial sector with two distinct 
IT teams. The selected organization happened to be one where th e researcher currently holds 
the IT manager position. This choice was deliberate, as it is a global company with around 200 
employees and teams working digitally around the globe. The company has several physical 
offices worldwide and embraces a flexible hybrid model, as the company does not have 
mandatory fixed shifts or frequency in the office. Also, it presented a viable option due to the 
researcher’s existing rapport with key IT and Human Resources stakeholders. Furthermore, the 
company is already compromised with employee engagement; an online survey is performed 
yearly to identify the current level of engagement and foster its improvement. In 2023, 83,7% 
of the organization's employees recommended the company as a great workplace. Furthermore, 
84,4% of the employees are satisfied with their work -life balance. Besides, the researcher’s 
familiarity with the organizational landscape facilitated a deeper understanding of the method 
employed. 
Acknowledging the potential for the managerial role of one of the researchers within the 
company, including direct leadership over one of the selected teams, to avoid influencing 
participant responses, a conscious decision was made to categorize this cycle as a demonstration 
rather than the evaluation assessment of the artifact. The research manager position facilitated 
her interaction with the teams . It provided  valuable knowledge regarding the organizational 
context, which helped perform  the demonstration case studies and initial validation and 
improvement of the artifacts. By doing that, we mitigated any potential biases that could arise 
from the researcher's  managerial position, ensuring the integrity and impartiality of the 
evaluation process. 
The alignment meeting with IT and Human Resources directors occurred on May 30, 2023. The 
research objective, context, and findings were summarized, followed by the resources required 
to apply it in the company. In this meeting, we established congruence among the participation 
of two teams, as well as the objectives and expectations of the respective departments regarding 
the forthcoming application of the entire Awareness phase. It consists of the first step (1.1 Align 
project scope and organizational context with the stakeholders). Considering that the researcher 
was already aware of the organization context, it was not applied the initial semi -structured 
interview questionnaire (Appendix D, Table D.1) to gather information about the team's 
structure, dynamics, geographical distribution, and existing organizational policies and 
initiatives related to employee engagement. We refer to Team 1, the team involved during the 
first demonstration cycle, and Team 2, the team involved in the second demonstration cycle.            
5.2.1  First Demonstration Cycle 
The first demonstration cycle was performed with the researcher's IT team. It comprises eight 
employees with a multi -disciplinary background, such as software engineering, mathematics, 
and business analytics. The project started with an alignment meeting w ith all participants on 

DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
 
117 
 
June 13, 2023. This meeting summarized the research objective, context, the main steps, and 
their role in the process. It also reinforced the anonymity of the collected data  and stated that 
the researcher's  role as their manager should not influence their responses. After that, they 
received the link for the online survey (described in Section 4.1.2.1). It consists of the steps ‘1.3 
Communicate the project plan and objectives to the involved team’ and ‘1.4 Co llect the 
information related to individual, team, and organizational characteristics.’ 
Recognizing the importance of maximizing participation and ensuring comprehensive data 
collection, a reminder was dispatched on June 24, 2023, urging individuals to partake in the 
survey process. This proactive measure aimed to bolster response rates and e nhance the 
representativeness of the gathered feedback. The survey concluded on September 14, 2023, 
with all eight designated IT team participants furnishing responses, achieving a 100% response 
rate.  
Following the culmination of data collection, a rigorous analysis of survey responses was 
conducted between September 14 and September 22, 2023. This phase involved systematically 
examining gathered data to discern patterns, trends, and insights pertinent to evaluating the 
artifact. It consist ed of the steps ‘1.5.1 Identify the current Workplace Arrangement Model’, 
‘1.5.2 Identify the level of virtuality and enterprise integration’, ‘1.5.3 Identify the existing 
barriers and enablers’, ‘1.5.4 Identify the current level of employee engagement’, ‘1.6 Identify 
the strengths weaknesses opportunities and threats’, ‘1.7 Map actors, networks, and conflicts ,’ 
and ‘1.8 Confirm employees’ perceived barriers and enablers by generation.’ 
An individual interview session was conducted on August 4, 2023, with a designated intern 
within the team. The participant was chosen due to his recent entry into the company.  The 
interview focused on evaluating the efficacy and user experience of the virtual onboarding 
process, thus providing valuable qualitative insights complementing the quantitative survey 
data. It consists of the step ‘1.9.1 Interview employees to detail the information needed.’ 
On September 29, 2023, a comprehensive SWOT analysis session was convened, with the 
active participation of all eight respondents alongside the principal investigator. This 
collaborative exercise facilitated a holistic assessment of the artifact's internal  capabilities and 
external contextual factors. It consisted of step ‘1.9 Confirm the SWOT analysis with the team 
involved and discuss possible solutions.’ 
Building on the outcomes of the previous steps, the best practices list was updated with possible 
improvements and solutions aligned with the involved team (step 1.10). Since the objective of 
this demonstration cycle was applying the Awareness phase, after  completing that, the formal 
feedback with the findings was provided on October 24, 2023, to critical organizational 
stakeholders, including the IT director, HR director, and Chief Executive Officer (CEO). It 
allowed insight to be spread to other departmen ts of the organization. The CEO and HR 
directors suggested preparing a workshop regarding the challenges and benefits of the digital 
workplace to share the main findings of the research and some of the practical guidelines 
identified by this stage. The workshop occurred online on January 18th, 2024, and all employees 
were invited.

to the conflict of the researcher being their direct manager.

To better understand the context of the employees’ experience, the involved actors, networks, 
and existing conflicts were mapped, as proposed in step 1.7 of the method. The involved actors 
are like the ones identified in our previous study (Figure 4.2). All employees shared the home 
with at least one familiar child(ren), spouse, or parent and used a similar technical toolkit (the 
digital actors). No major conflict was raised during this step. 
Comparing our sample results with existing literature to confirm employees' perceived barriers 
and enablers by generation (step 1.8), we found that five align with previous studies and are 
relevant for all generations. These include lack of resources, over load, communication failure 
or inappropriate information, and involvement and attachment to team members. Additionally, 
the ability to anticipate/predict tasks performed at work is more relevant for Gen Z in our study 
and the literature. 
However, some differences emerged. Our study found workspace conditions more relevant for 
Gen Y, whereas previous literature suggests no significant differences between generations. 
The feeling of isolation is cited as more relevant for Gen Z. Our study highlights the importance 
of appropriate job performance tools for all generations . In contrast, literature suggests it is 
more important for Gen Z. Similarly, identifying organizational values and purpose is relevant 
across all generations in our study. In contrast, literature considers it more important for Gen X 
and Y. Finally, rewards, recognition, and feedback are relevant for all generations in our study, 
contrasting with literature suggesting they are more pertinent to Gen Z and Y. 
With the SWOT tool ready, it was possible to proceed to step ‘1.9 Confirm the SWOT analysis 
with the team involved and discuss potential solutions.’ The team dynamic occurred online on 
29 September 2023 with the researcher and the eight employees. We used the Miro platform to 
support the digital interaction among the participants, as illustrated in Figure 5.12; the data 
presented on the platform is described in Table 5.3.

After this activity, the best practices list was updated with possible actions or solutions (step 
1.10) to enhance the employee's experience in a digital workplace. The first phase of our method 
was concluded. The objective of this first demonstration cycl e was to apply only the steps of 
phase 1. Awareness and the continuity of the design and implementation of the actions proposed 
by the employees followed their existing internal continuous improvement process. As this 
team performs their activities using Agile best practices and ceremonies, the proposed actions 
were further discussed during one of their Retrospective ceremonies, followed by the 
refinement and prioritization during the Grooming, and planned in the following Sprint 
planning according to the other priorities and teams’ capacity. 
5.2.2  Second Demonstration Cycle 
The second demonstration cycle was performed with a different team that worked in a hybrid 
model. It comprises nine participants from the IT department with a multi -disciplinary 
background, such as software engineering and business analytics. Three of the nine participants 
were external consultants allocated full -time work with the team. They all work on the same 
projects, building and maintaining financial IT solutions. This team is managed by two leaders, 
one directly responsible for the software engineering employees and consultants and the other 
for the business analytics. 
The alignment meeting with the two managers occurred on November 6, 2023. This meeting 
presented a summary of the research objective, context, the main steps, and the participant’s 
role in the process. Each leader (from business analysts and software engineering employees) 
shared the information regarding the project and their participation with their team, followed 
by an email from the researcher describing the main steps of the process and reinforcing the 
anonymity of the collection data. It consists of the steps ‘1.1 Align project scope and 
organizational context with the stakeholders’ and ‘1.3 Communicate project plan and objectives 
to the involved team.’ 
On November 8, 2023, they received the link for the online survey (described in Section 
4.1.2.1). It consists of step ‘1.4 Collect the information related to individual, team, and 
organizational characteristics.’ Recognizing the importance of maximizing participation and 
ensuring comprehensive data collection, a reminder was dispatched on January 15, 2024, urging 
individuals to partake in the survey process. Considering the standard vacation period in 
December and January, the survey was extended until January 22, 2024. Seven team members 
completed the responses, achieving a 78% response rate.  

DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
 
129 
 
Following the culmination of data collection, a rigorous analysis of survey responses occurred 
between January 25 and February 16, 2024. This phase involved systematically examining 
gathered data to discern patterns, trends, and insights pertinent to evaluating the artifact. It 
consists of the steps ‘1.5.1 Identify the current Workplace Arrangement Model’, ‘1.5.2 Identify 
the level of virtuality and enterprise integration’, ‘1.5.3 Identify the existing barriers and 
enablers’, ‘1.5.4 Identify the current level of employee engagement’, ‘1.6 Identify the strengths 
weaknesses opportunities and threats’,  ‘1.7 Map actors, networks, and conflicts ,’ and ‘1.8 
Confirm employees’ perceived barriers and enablers by generation.’ 
On February 20, 2024, a comprehensive SWOT analysis session was held, with eight 
participants actively engaging alongside the principal researcher . This collaborative exercise 
facilitated a holistic assessment of the artifact's internal capabilities and external contextual 
factors. It consists of step ‘1.9 Confirm the SWOT analysis with the team involved and discuss 
possible solutions’. 
Building on the outcomes of the previous steps, the best practices list was updated with possible 
improvements and solutions aligned with the involved team (step 1.10). Since the objective of 
this demonstration cycle was to apply the Awareness phase, formal feedback with the findings 
was provided to the IT managers responsible for the team on March 18, 2024, after its 
completion. This allowed the managers to monitor and support improvements, insights, and 
actions. 
Data Collection and Analysis

from November 8th, 2023, to January 22nd, 2024. The nine team members received the online 
questionnaire that  covered the questions described in Section 4.1.2.1. To avoid the 
identification of participants, the questions related to birth year (ID I.1), gender (ID 1.2), 
education level (ID 1.3), organization tenure (ID 1.4), employment status (ID 1.5), and country 
of residence (ID 1.7) were removed from the questionnaire. This information was collected 
from their managers to provide the individual characteristics of the team. Table 5.5 consolidates 
the team's main characteristics and organizational context.

After this dynamic, the best practices list was updated with possible actions or solutions (step 
1.10) to enhance the employee's experience in a digital workplace. The first phase of our method 
was concluded. The objective of this second demonstration cycle was to apply only the steps of 
phase 1. Awareness and the continuity of the design and implementation of the actions proposed 

DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
 
 
139 
 
by the employees followed their existing internal continuous improvement process. The 
proposed actions were presented and further discussed with the teams’ leaders. Three were 
prioritized according to their resources and capacity: ' Support among team members through 
communication channels. Creation of Slack channels by system topic ’, ‘Analysis of a testing tool to 
record screen -executed procedures ,’ and ‘Improvement in implementation processes with 
standardized scripts.’ 
5.2.3  Findings from the Demonstration 
The analysis of the results from the demonstration cycles reveals both similarities and 
differences between the two teams. Both teams involved in the two demonstration cycles 
operate in a hybrid work environment and value in-person activities such as celebrations, team 
building, and training. Both teams also showed a strong preference for the hybrid work model. 
Participants indicated they would only consider giving up this model for a 20 -30% financial 
increase. Additionally, both teams found that working fr om home enhances productivity and 
focus, with participants staying connected longer than working in the office. However, the 
office environment posed challenges for concentration, except for one Team 1 participant who 
could be more focused in the office. T hey prefer work -life balance, competitive wages, and 
career progression opportunities as key organizational attractiveness factors. Both teams 
reported high levels of employee engagement, with scores around 4 out of 5. The teams also 
identified the need fo r clear roles, achievable goals, and appropriate tools as critical enablers 
for their work environment. A commonality between the teams is their familiarity with and 
implementation of agile practices, which facilitated the discussion using a retrospective 
approach. Team 1 already used the Miro tool for their retrospectives, aiding the process.  
There were notable differences between the teams as well. Team 1 includes members from 
Brazil with a manager based in Portugal, leading to less frequent in -person interactions, while 
Team 2's members, including their managers, reside in the same country. T he topic of 
management and leadership was not discussed with Team 1 because the researcher is the direct 
manager of this team. Participants from Team 2 noted that the "coffee" space in the office often 
helps prevent conflicts. Team 1 identified a significant barrier in resource availability 
(technical, people, skills, and processes), while Team  2 did not highlight this issue as 
prominently. In contrast, Team 2 emphasized the importance of conflict management and good 
benefits packages more than Team 1. Despite the similarities related to the perceived barriers, 
the actions proposed by each team differed slightly, reflecting their unique challenges and 
priorities.  
The method is well -suited to achieving the intended objectives and has proven viable when 
applied in real-world contexts. Its strengths include a comprehensive approach that considers a 
wide range of factors influencing employee engagement and offers the f lexibility to support 
tailored solutions. Furthermore, we confirmed the varying perceptions of the factors influencing 
engagement in the DW, even when teams operate within the same organizational context. 
However, its limitations lie in the familiarity wit h Agile methodologies, which may require 
additional explanation for some teams. The Demonstration applied only Phase 1 (Awareness) 
of the method. However, the application of subsequent phases - Design, Build and Adopt, and 
Evaluate - may also be facilitated by participants with prior knowledge of Agile principles, as 
these phases involve solution refinement, prioritization, development, and implementation that 
align with Agile practices. Participants responded positively to the method' s dynamics and 
tools, appreciating how it fostered open communication. Based on the feedback from these two 
demonstrations, we enhanced the artifact by incorporating the SWOT analysis tool. The SWOT 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
140 
 
tool was found to help conduct dynamics, suggesting it should be considered a recommended 
component in future assessments. 
5.3  Evaluation of the Method 
Our artifact was evaluated in a leading company in the food retail sector in Portugal, which had 
over 38 years of experience and more than 38.000 workers. It allowed us to assess the artifacts’ 
usability, feasibility, and scalability. The selected organization happened to be one with whom 
one of the supervisors had contact due to previous collaboration with the University of Minho. 
The company embraces a hybrid model, strongly recommending two to three days per week in 
the office, Wednesdays mandatory, and Mondays and Fridays recommended. 
The alignment meeting with the teams’ manager and supervisors occurred on March 15, 2024. 
The research objective, context, and findings were summarized, followed by the resources 
required to apply it in the company. In this meeting, we established the teams involved that 
worked in a hybrid model, as well as the objectives and expectations of the respective 
departments regarding the forthcoming application of the entire awareness phase of our method. 
Considering the organization's time and resource availability, the scope of the Evaluate phase 
of our DSR project will be the implementation of Phase 1: Awareness of our method. Applying 
the proposed solutions resulting from the first phase will be the company's responsibility, as 
they require more time and resource availability, not just from the team involved, for their 
development and implementation. This first meeting corresponds to  the first step (1.1 Align 
project scope and organizational context with the stakeholders). Two distinct teams, 
Merchandising and Supply Chain, were assigned to collaborate with the investigation. The 
Merchandising team comprises nine members, while the Supply Chain team includes ten . We 
also aligned the expected dates for each step of the process and the participation of the external 
consultants who worked on the same projects in partnership with the team and were allocated 
full-time. The initial semi-structured interview was performed during the meeting to understand 
the general organization's context better. This initial interview confirmed that the company has 
no ongoing initiative to measure employee engagement, leading us to adopt the Develop and 
Support strategy for fostering engagement in the organization (step 1.2 Align with the 
stakeholders the strategy to foster engagement). The supervisors of both teams mentioned that 
not all employees used to go to the office two or three times per week, which led to the inclusion 
of a new question in the online survey to identify the distance from the home to the office to 
analyze if this was a possible reason for low adherence to the frequency in office. Besides that, 
the company also recognizes a high level of dependence between the teams to perform their 
tasks and a lack of time for the senior employees and managers to support the less experienced 
employees. 
On April 10, 2024, the information regarding the project was shared with all involved team 
employees; it constituted step ‘1.3: Communicate the project plan and objectives to the involved 
team members.’ Participants were provided with a clear explanation of the study's objectives 
and ethical considerations, including assurances of anonymity and confidentiality. Participation 
was entirely voluntary and without any form of incentives. Following the procedures, on April 
11, 2024, the participants received the link for the online survey (described in Section 4.1.2.1). 
It consists of step ‘1.4 Collect the information related to individual, team, and organizational 
characteristics.’ Recognizing the importance of maximizing participation and ensuring 
comprehensive data collection, a reminder was dispatched on April 18 and May 5, 2024, urging 
individuals to participate in the survey. Considering the Eastern holidays in April, the surve y 

DSR Demonstration and Evaluation 
 
 
141 
 
was extended until May 20, 2024. All participants from the Merchandising team completed the 
survey, while the Supply Chain team achieved an 80% response rate.  
Following the culmination of data collection, a rigorous analysis of survey responses occurred 
between May 20 and May 22, 2024. This phase involved systematically examining gathered 
data to discern patterns, trends, and insights pertinent to evaluating the artifact. It consists of 
the steps ‘1.5.1 Identify the current Workplace Arrangement Model’, ‘ 1.5.2 Identify the level 
of virtuality and enterprise integration’, ‘1.5.3 Identify the existing barriers and enablers’, ‘1.5.4 
Identify the current level of employee engagement’, ‘1.6 Identify the strengths weaknesses 
opportunities and threats’, ‘1.7 Map actors, networks, and conflicts ,’ and ‘1.8 Confirm 
employees’ perceived barriers and enablers by generation.’ 
On May 23, 2024, a comprehensive SWOT analysis session convened with each team, with the 
involved participants actively participating alongside the principal investigator. This 
collaborative exercise facilitated a holistic assessment of the artifact's inte rnal capabilities and 
external contextual factors. It consists of step ‘1.9: Confirm the SWOT analysis with the team 
involved and discuss possible solutions.’ 
Building on the outcomes of the previous steps, the best practices list with possible 
improvements and solutions aligned with the involved team (step 1.10 of our method). As the 
objective of the evaluation of the real organization was to apply only the Awa reness phase of 
the method, after its completion, a second online survey (Appendix E, Table E.1) was 
performed between June 3 and 18, 2024, to hypothetically assess the impact of the proposed 
practices on employee engagement levels. Hypothetically assessing the effects of the proposed 
solutions on employee engagement levels is relevant for making the evaluation robust because 
it allows validating the method's effectiveness, particularly regarding its ability to accurately 
identify barriers and enablers and support the definition of actions to increase engagement 
levels. The full report was presented to the team managers on July 8, 2024.

Competitive salaries or other financial incentives are cited as the most relevant organizational 
attractiveness factor, followed by work -life balance and flexible work arrangements (Figure

offer without a flexible work model if the financial value exceeded 20 -30% of their current 
salary.
 
 
Figure 5.27 - Merchandising - organizational attractiveness factors. 


 
144 
 
After analyzing the general information about the individual, the team, and the organizational 
context, the workplace arrangement model was identified according to the level of virtuality 
and the levels of enterprise integration (steps 1.5.1 and 1.5.2). Enterprise integration was 
calculated using the average responses collected for this topic (rated on a 3 -point Likert scale, 
from 1 to 3). The results sh owed an enterprise integration level of 2.65, as detailed in Figure 
5.28. 
 
Figure 5.28 - Merchandising - enterprise integration. 
The level of virtuality consists of three dimensions. One of them is the average frequency with 
which this team meets in person; the Merchandising team had an average of 1.5 (3 for 'Rarely,' 
2.5 for 'Once a month,' 2 for 'It is not regular,' 1.5 for '1 -2 times a week,' and 1 for 'More than 
twice a week'). Regarding geographical  or spatial distance, it was considered level 2 , as 
proposed in section 4.2.2.2, The Virtuality and Enterprise Integration Levels (I2),  since the 
employees live in the same country. The third is the use of technology, calculated as the average 
of the responses collected for this topic (rated on a 3-point Likert scale, from 1 to 3). The results 
showed a level of 2.52 for the use of technol ogy, as detailed in Figure 5.29. The item ‘Unique 
use of AI, Analytics, or Augmented reality tools that can fundamentally change a generally 
accepted business model’ had a general evaluation of ‘Disagree,’ considering there was no such 
implementation when the study was performed.  This led to a level of virtuality of 2.01, as 
detailed in the formula below. 
SUM(1.5 + 2 + 2.52)/3. 
 
Figure 5.29 - Merchandising - technological implementation.

Plotting the enterprise integration and virtuality levels in the workplace arrangement model 
(Figure 5.30) shows that this team operates in a digital workplace arrangement model with a 
high level of enterprise integration and a moderate level of virtuality. 
 
Figure 5.30 - Merchandising - workplace arrangement model. 
To identify the existing barriers and facilitators in the workplace (step 1.5.3 of our method), 
participants were asked to rate the impact level of each barrier listed below on their current 
experience. The barriers are illustrated in Figure 5.31. The barrier with the mos t significant 
impact on this team is the lack of adequate information (poor quality, excessive, and unavailable 
on time), followed by poor management or leadership and work overload. 
 
 
Figure 5.31 - Merchandising - barriers. 
As illustrated in Figure 5.32, the most relevant enablers for this team are explicit, achievable, 
and measurable goals, appropriate tools to perform the job, adequate access to information, and 
the ability to anticipate tasks. 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
146 
 
 
Figure 5.32 - Merchandising - enablers. 
The initial data analysis also included employees' perceptions of the predecessors that influence 
engagement, consequently impacting their experience in the digital workplace. Figure 5.33 
consolidates the six antecedents included in the scope of our research: the team rated trust 
among colleagues highest, while knowledge sharing received the lowest score. 
 
Figure 5.33 - Merchandising - predecessors of employee engagement. 
After identifying the barriers and facilitators, we proceed ed to step ‘1.5.4 Identify the current 
level of employee engagement.’ Figure 5.34 consolidates the results for each topic used in our 
employee engagement scale. This team's average level of employee engagement is 3.92 (out of 
5). Employees are generally engaged, with individual levels ranging from 3.31 to 4.44. Two 
items with the lowest overall scores are: 'In the last seven days, I have received recognition or 
praise for doing good work' and 'My supervisor, or someone at work, seems to care about me 
as a person,' which may be related to the previously cited management barrier despite the good 
average level of the leadership predecessor indicated in Figure 5.33. Two other items with lower 
scores are related to organizational connection: 'Working at my organization has great personal 
meaning for me' and 'I believe in the mission and purpose of the organization I work for.’ 


DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
147 
 
 
Figure 5.34 - Merchandising - employee engagement scale. 
Some differences and similarities can be highlighted regarding the preferences and perceptions 
of different generations about various aspects of the work environment. Conflict management 
in person is particularly relevant for Generation Z. Regarding feedback, both Generation Y and 
Generation Z prefer it to be given in person. When it comes to client meetings, Generation Y 
values face-to-face interaction more. In -person training is also considered more relevant by 
Generation Y. However, for team meetings, physical presence is more important for Generation 
Z. 
Only one Generation Z participant mentioned that all activities conducted virtually result in a 
loss of effectiveness. Generation Z also showed more critical views regarding autonomy in the 
workplace. On the other hand, all generations consider that "poor communication and bad 
relationships" are not relevant barriers. 
Clarity in roles and responsibilities is a crucial factor for both Generations Z and Y. 
Additionally, good benefits packages are highly valued by both generations. However, financial 
incentives are essential for all generations. In contrast, flexible work arrangements are less 
relevant to Generation Y. 
Generation Z showed a neutral stance when asked about the personal meaning of working in 
their organizations. Generations Y and Z also demonstrated neutrality towards the statement, "I 
work more than expected to help my organization succeed." Additionally,  Generation Y was 
more neutral when evaluating learning and growth opportunities at work in the past year.

the team dynamics and the rationale for including each selected discussion topic.

Comparing the results obtained from the online survey with the existing literature (step 1.8), 
we selected eight possible solutions previously investigated and aligned with the barriers and 
facilitators identified as most relevant for this team. These include flexible communication 
methods, allowing employees to use office space creatively, promoting integration events 
among team members, creatin g open forums and practical assistance sessions, and ensuring 
effective communication through personal interaction with the team to mitigate some of the 
most relevant barriers. Recognition at the end of deliveries, decision-making opportunities, and 
creating a shared knowledge base are alternatives to foster the most relevant facilitators for the 
team. These alternatives were presented during the dynamics that were conducted. 
With the SWOT tool ready, it was possible to proceed to step ‘1.9 Confirm SWOT analysis 
with the involved team and discuss potential solutions.’ The team dynamics occurred online on 
May 23, 2024, with the researcher and eight employees. We used the Miro platform to support 
digital interaction among the participants. Figure 5.35 illustrates the platform environment 
used, and the data presented can be found in Table 5.9. 
 
Figure 5.35 - Merchandising - initial SWOT using the Miro platform. 
The dynamic was initiated by presenting the main results of the online survey, which provided 
the necessary information for the SWOT elaboration. The entire dynamic was conducted in 
Portuguese, the participant’s native language. Next, the participants had 8 minutes to read the 
cards and complement them with new cards or alter existing ones in the quadrant, if necessary. 
The team decided to move five cards between quadrants, with autonomy and daily processes 
moving from weaknesses to strengths, recognition, celebration, and workplace conditions, and 
from the weakness quadrant to the opportunity quadrant. Knowledge sharing was decided to 
remain between strength and weakness. The team included six new cards: four weaknesses, one 
opportunity, and one threat, as detailed below: 
● [Weakness] Lack of knowledge/training on a particular subject (at project times). 
● [Weakness] Lack of availability from superiors to remove barriers. 
● [Weakness] Lack of work organization. 
● [Weakness] Lack of resources. 
● [Opportunity] Team building activities. 


Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
150 
 
● [Threat] Misinformation or misinterpretations. 
 
With the SWOT completed, we discussed the existing cards to align understanding and different 
perceptions before discussing possible actions related to each card. Employees had another 5 
minutes to interact with the SWOT, selecting the three most relevant cards in the opinion of 
each participant. Then, they had an additional 10 minutes to include possible actions or 
solutions for the cards most appropriate to the team. Some alternatives from the backlog of best 
practices identified previously in the literature were presented. Finally, we discussed the actions 
and alternatives proposed by the participants to align the understanding and perception of all 
involved. The final version of the SWOT tool is presented in Figure 5.36. 
 
 
Figure 5.36 - Merchandising - final SWOT using the Miro platform.

The Merchandising team showed synergy among the participants, who felt comfortable 
expressing their opinions. This indicates that a relationship of trust has already been established 
within the team. 
After this dynamic, the list of best practices was updated with possible actions or solutions (step 
1.10 of our method) to improve the employee experience in a digital work environment and 
complete our method's first phase. The goal of this cycle was to ap ply only the steps of phase 
1: Awareness. The continuation of the design and implementation of the proposed actions 
should follow the organization's internal process according to other priorities and the team's 
capacity. Since the team works in an agile mo del, the proposed actions can go through the 
refinement and prioritization process during Grooming and be planned in Sprint Planning 
according to prioritization and resource availability, with the results evaluated during the 
retrospective. However, some a ctions, such as financial rewards and feedback 360º, 
require other departments, like Human Resources, to proceed.  
For the study, we conducted a second online survey to measure the effectiveness of the proposed 
actions, phase 4 of the method, by measuring employee engagement levels based on 
hypothetical scenarios of implementing some practices identified as relevant to the teams. A 
hypothetical scenario was presented where the organization implements a set of eight 
improvements aimed at increasing team autonomy, improving recognition for work performed, 
reducing employee overload, and optimizing knowledge transfer and communication. For the 
improvements listed below, each participant was asked to indicate which actions would 
positively influence their level of engagement. 
● Train backups and decentralizing knowledge. 
● Formal recognition of additional work, including tangible rewards (bonuses, days off). 
● Formal recognition of additional work, including intangible rewards (public praise, 
merit certificates). 
● Increase the frequency of pre -scheduled individual meetings with managers for 
feedback and alignment. 
● Implement quarterly 360° feedback sessions. 
● Update the workspace with more monitors and a more efficient distribution of spaces 
among teams. 
● Establish shared team goals and communicate project priorities and resource allocation. 
● Implement a project management tool that overviews all ongoing projects, highlighting 
resource needs and team dependencies. 
Eight participants from the Merchandising team completed the second survey, which is 
presented in Table 5.11. The priorities are represented by the columns P1, P2, P3, P4, P5, P6, 
P7, and P8.

     After identifying all the factors mentioned earlier, we moved on to step ‘1.5.4 Identify the 
current level of employee engagement.’ Figure 5.46 consolidates the results for each topic used 
in our employee engagement scale. This team's average level of employee engagement is 4.02 
(out of 5). Employees are generally engaged, with individual levels ranging from 2.5 to 4.69. 
The individual level of engagement did not show significant variation according to generation. 
There was a considerable variation of 87.6% between the most engaged and the least engaged 
participants. Two items with the lowest overall scores are: 'I am able to stay focused on my 
tasks in my work environment' and 'I work more than expected to help my organizatio n 
succeed,' which may be related to the previously mentioned barrier of workload overload.

believe that performing their daily activities in person is more effective. Similarly, conducting

Generation Z demonstrates a high level of trust in their colleagues  and perceives less impact

relevant. 
Regarding attractiveness factors, good benefits packages were not cited as the most attractive 
factor of the organization by any generation. Excellent training and development programs are 
less relevant to Generation Y. At the same time, flexible work arrangements are more relevant 
to Generation Z. International opportunities , and corporate values that match their own were 
not cited as relevant factors by any generation. However, a good reputation for ethical practices 
is more appropriate to Generation Y. Generation Z has a neutral perception of the statement, "I 
am able to stay focused on my tasks in my work environment."

Comparing the results obtained from the online survey with the existing literature (step 1.8), 
we selected eight possible solutions previously investigated that align with the barriers and 
facilitators identified as most relevant for this team. These include flexible communication 
methods, promoting integration events among team members, creating open forums and support 
sessions, and disseminating virtual work policies as alternatives to mitigate some of the most 
relevant barriers for this group. Promoting celebration events, recognition at the end of 
deliveries, oppor tunities for decision -making, and creating a shared knowledge base are 
alternatives to foster the most relevant facilitators for the team. These alternatives were 
presented during the following dynamic. 
With the SWOT tool ready, it was possible to proceed to step ‘1.9 Confirm the SWOT analysis 
with the involved team and discuss potential solutions.’ The dynamic was performed online on 
May 23, 2024, with the researcher and six collaborators. We used the Miro platform to support 
digital interaction among the participants. Figure 5.47 illustrates the platform environment 
used, and the data presented can be found in Table 5.13. 
 
Figure 5.47 - Supply Chain - initial SWOT using the Miro platform. 
The dynamic was initiated by presenting the main results of the online survey, which provided 
the necessary information for the SWOT elaborated initially. The entire activity was conducted 
in Portuguese, the participant's native language. Then, the partici pants had 8 minutes to read 
the cards and complement them with new cards or move existing cards to different quadrants, 
if necessary. The team moved three cards between quadrants: autonomy from weakness to 
threat, communication from opportunity to threat, and collaboration (within the team) from 
weakness to strength. The team added four new cards: one weakness and three threats, as 
detailed below: 
● [Weakness] Collaboration between teams. 
● [Threat] Lack of training. 
● [Threat] Knowledge centralization. 
● [Threat] Communication between areas.

perceptions before discussing possible actions related to each card. The employees had another 
5 minutes to interact with the SWOT, selecting the three most relev ant cards in their opinion. 
Then, the participants had ten more minutes to include possible actions or solutions for the cards 
most appropriate to the team. Some alternatives from the literature's previously identified best 
practices backlog were presented. Finally, we discussed the actions and alternatives proposed 
by the participants to align everyone's understanding and perception. The final version of the 
SWOT tool is presented in Figure 5.48.

After this activity, the list of best practices was updated with possible actions or solutions (step 
1.10) to improve the employee experience in a digital work environment and complete the first 
phase of our method. The goal of this cycle was to apply only the steps of Phase 1: Awareness. 
Continuing the design and implementation of the actions proposed by the employees should 
follow the organization's internal process according to other priorities and team capacity. As 
the team works in an agile model, the proposed actions can go through the refinement and 
prioritization process during Grooming and be planned in Sprint Planning according to 
prioritization and resource availability, with the results evaluated during the retrospective.

However, some actions, such as hiring resources, require approval from other departments, like

For the study purposes, we conducted a second online survey to assess the effectiveness of 
proposed actions, phase 4 of the framework, assessing employee engagement levels based on 
hypothetical scenarios of implementing practices identified as relevant for the teams. A 
hypothetical scenario was presented where the organizati on implements a set of eight 
improvements aimed at increasing team autonomy, enhancing recognition for work done, 
reducing employee workload, and optimizing knowledge and communication flow. We adopted 
the same improvements for both teams due to similar wo rkload and resource constraints. This 
approach also allows for analyzing actions that would have greater organizational relevance. 
For the improvements listed below, each participant was asked to indicate which actions would 
have the most positive influence on their level of engagement: 
● Train backups and decentralizing knowledge. 
● Formal recognition of additional work performed, including tangible rewards (bonuses, 
days off). 
● Formal recognition of additional work, including intangible rewards (public praise, 
merit certificates). 
● Increase the frequency of pre -scheduled individual meetings with managers for 
feedback and alignment. 
● Implement quarterly 360° feedback sessions. 
● Update the workspace with more monitors and a more efficient distribution of spaces 
among teams. 
● Establish shared goals among teams and communicate project priorities and resource 
allocation. 
● Implement a project management tool that overviews all ongoing projects, highlighting 
resource needs and team dependencies.

The items related to organizational engagement and individual commitment had a more 
significant negative impact, suggesting that external factors outside the study may have 
occurred between the first and second surveys, thereby affecting the participants' level of 
engagement. During the meeting with the team’s supervisor on 8th July 2024, she revealed that

the overload of the remaining integrands of the team. Considering the overload was already 
raised as a barrier for this team, the situation worsened, justifying the decrease in employee 
engagement.
