There is an inconsistency in the engagement questions ranked by the Supply Chain team, while 
‘At work, I am focused on my job’ has one of the highest values (4.875), ‘I concentrate on my 
job when I am at work’ has the lowest (3.375). The same is true for ‘I am willing to put in extra 
effort without being asked.’, which has a high evaluation (4.5), in contrast with ‘I work harder 
than expected to help my company be successful.’, with the lowest evaluation (3.375). 
Regarding the extra effort, during the activity with the team, it was possible to identify that the 
extra effort is made to stand out and achieve career progression, not to help the organization. 
The Merchandising team acknowledges that although Autonomy scores low, members have 
autonomy to perform individual tasks. The low rating is related to activities depending on other 
teams that do not have the same priorities and planning. The daily ceremony  (Daily) aids in 
alignment among those involved and managing online activities. The team feels more focused 
when working from home but considers it positive to go to the office for celebrations like team 
breakfasts, birthdays, and team-building activities. These activities are seen as opportunities to 
relax and build bonds among team members. Depending on their role and function, going to the 
office may be more productive for some individuals as it allows for personal alignment. 
However, no one appreciates the interruptions in the office. This topic was attributed to 
probable cultural or generational differences. Before the pandemic, attending the office was not 
considered highly relevant for those already accustomed to a full-remote job. 
The study revealed important insights into the factors influencing the engagement of the Supply 
Chain and Merchandising teams in the digital workplace. While both teams have close initial 
engagement averages (3.92 and 4.02 out of 5), their specific needs a nd challenges vary and 
overlap depending on the aspect. 
Both teams recognize the importance of in -person activities like feedback and conflict 
management. However, while the Supply Chain team values in -person training and meetings, 
the Merchandising team emphasizes celebrations and team -building activities more. 
Competitive salaries and work-life balance are common attractive factors. However, the Supply 
Chain team values training programs more, whereas the Merchandising team appreciates 
flexible work arrangements. 
Overload is a common barrier. Specifically, the Supply Chain team faces resource shortages, 
while the Merchandising team deals with inadequate information related to knowledge 
centralization. Both teams benefit from appropriate work tools and clear, measur able 
objectives. Additionally, the Supply Chain team emphasizes the importance of clear roles and 
responsibilities, while the Merchandising team values adequate access to information and the 
ability to anticipate tasks. 
The engagement assessment revealed that while both teams score highly in focus areas and 
extra effort, the Supply Chain team struggles more with workplace concentration. In contrast, 
the Merchandising team lacks recognition and a strong sense of belonging.  Trust among 
colleagues and knowledge sharing are strengths, but autonomy and collaboration need 
improvement, especially in the Supply Chain team. Both autonomy and collaboration are 
related to knowledge centralization, leading to dependency in executing t asks and a lack of 
knowledge holders to collaborate with colleagues. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
166 
 
The knowledge base with project documentation was initiated two years ago. Much of the 
knowledge remains restricted to a few individuals who are often unavailable due to being sought 
after by all teams. The main risk is that most employees lack a comprehensive view of the entire 
process/system and the potential impacts of changes. Current training sessions help because 
they are recorded, allowing employees to revisit topics as needed for project work. 
The Merchandising team agrees that contributions are recognized , finding it beneficial to 
increase the frequency of pre -scheduled individual meetings with managers. Another topic 
mentioned is potential improvement in physical space, such as providing more monitors and 
reviewing space distribution among teams. 
Despite recognition being highly rated as a facilitator, the Supply Chain team lacks recognition 
for the additional work done. This extra work, combined with self -imposed career growth 
pressure, may contribute to a sense of overload, which is identified as a significant barrier. 
Additionally, some perceive that going to the office may be more productive depending on the 
role and function. 
Autonomy is not a common perception among Supply Chain team members. One possible 
reason is a lack of technical and functional knowledge, resulting in greater dependency on 
others to complete tasks. Collaboration within the team is strong, but collaboratio n with other 
teams can be challenging due not to a lack of willingness but to availability and differing 
priorities. Support teams also appear overloaded, making training and education crucial for the 
Supply Chain team. While there is material on the Confluence platform with some information, 
it is segregated by areas, and knowledge is often centralized among a few people. 
Overall, both teams see value in going to the office once a week, with management roles 
requiring more frequent presence. The teams have the necessary technological tools for work 
and maintain good relationships among members. Participants also value a pos itive 
organizational climate. 
A common perception is that the main area needing improvement is the centralization of 
information among a few individuals overwhelmed by requests from various teams. This 
reduces autonomy in task execution and project delivery. This can affect team communication 
due to a lack of shared goals, misalignment of priorities, and resource allocation across teams 
and projects. 
The preferences and perceptions of different generations within the Supply Chain and 
Merchandising teams regarding the workplace environment show some differences and 
similarities. No generation believes that conducting daily activities exclusively in pers on or 
virtually is more effective. In the Supply Chain team, celebrations are more relevant to 
Generation Y. At the same time, in-person meetings with clients are unimportant to Generation 
Y and Z. Generation Z demonstrates a high level of trust in their c olleagues, less impact from 
barriers such as lack of resources and isolation, and values task anticipation. Governance, 
transparent processes, and incentives for training are essential to both generations in both teams. 
Generation Z values flexible work arrangements more, while training programs are less relevant 
to Generation Y. No generation highlighted good benefits packages and corporate values . 
However, a good reputation for ethical practices is more appropriate  to Generation Y. 
Generation Z has a neut ral perception of workplace concentration. Conflict management and 
in-person feedback are more important to Generation Z in the Merchandising team. In contrast, 
in-person training is more relevant to Generation Y. Both team generations value clear roles 
and good governance practices, maintaining a neutral stance on the personal meaning of work 
and extra effort for organizational success. However, it is impossible to generalize results by 
generation due to the limited number of participants. 

DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
167 
 
5.3.4  Recommendations to the Organization  
The recommendations will be presented  in a unified manner due to the synergies between the 
topics both teams address, with knowledge transfer being the primary common objective. Here 
are some strategies and practical actions that can be implemented and adopted to improve 
employee engagement: 
1. Form Backups and Decentralize Knowledge: 
a) Implement a backup training program where employees are trained to substitute for at 
least one team member. 
b) Formalize critical process documentation at the Confluence platform and ensure it is 
accessible and understandable. 
Benefit: Reduce dependence on specific individuals, widely distribute knowledge, and 
increase team resilience. 
2. Rotate Responsibilities and Cross-Training: 
a) Introduce a program for rotating responsibilities and cross -training between teams. 
Conduct workshops as needed for projects or teams where members from different 
teams share knowledge and practices. 
Benefit: Expand functional knowledge among team members, promote greater autonomy, 
and reduce overload on specific individuals. 
3. Improve of Physical Space and Equipment: 
a) Update the workspace with more monitors. 
b) Review team space distribution based on the number of team members. 
Benefit: Increase comfort and productivity, especially for those needing to be in the office 
for specific functions. 
4. Recognize and Rewards for Additional Work: 
a) Implement a formal recognition system for additional work, including tangible rewards 
(bonuses, days off). 
b) Implement a formal recognition system for additional work, including intangible 
rewards (public praise, merit certificates). 
Benefit: Engage employees, reduce feelings of overload, and recognize extra efforts. 
5. Perform Regular Alignment and Feedback Meetings: 
a) Establish a frequency of pre-scheduled individual meetings with managers for feedback 
and alignment. 
b) Implement quarterly 360° feedback sessions. 
Benefit: Improve recognition of contributions, allow for quicker adjustment of priorities 
and expectations, and enhance satisfaction and performance. 
6. Communicate Shared Goals Clearly: 
a) Establish shared goals between teams. 
b) communicate priorities and resource allocation for projects. 
c) Create a visibility dashboard so all teams can see the project's progress and need in real-
time. 
Benefit: Improve team communication and collaboration, ensure goal alignment, and 
optimize resource utilization. 
7. Allocate Resources and Plan Project Dependencies: 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
168 
 
a) Implement a project management tool that provides an overview of all ongoing projects, 
highlighting resource needs and dependencies between teams. 
b) Allocate resources based on specific project needs, prioritizing knowledge and 
availability of employees. 
Benefit: Ensure the right resources are allocated to suitable projects at the right time, 
reducing overload and improving project efficiency and effectiveness. 
Expected Results: 
● Increased Autonomy: Employees will be more autonomous with backup training and 
improved knowledge access. 
● Increased Recognition: The new recognition system will engage employees, reducing 
feelings of overload. 
● Improved Productivity and Satisfaction: Improved physical space and work flexibility 
will increase productivity and employee satisfaction. 
● Better Communication and Collaboration: Clear goals, efficient communication, and 
project management tools will reduce team friction and improve project delivery. 
● Optimized Resource Allocation: Project overview and resource allocation based on 
specific needs will ensure efficient resource use and better project execution. 
The study identified several factors that can positively influence employee engagement and 
help prevent this phenomenon. Recognition and rewards for performance make employees feel 
valued and motivated. Additionally, knowledge sharing promotes autonomy and  mitigates 
employee overload, facilitating a healthy work -life balance. Companies must encourage and 
provide autonomy to teams to reflect and promote relevant actions to reduce existing barriers 
and enhance facilitators in a DW. Companies can increase empl oyee engagement and create a 
more productive and satisfying work environment by implementing these practical actions and 
a continuous and preventive strategy. 
5.4  Assessment of the Method 
Assessing the method used in the DSR project involves evaluating its design, implementation, 
and outcomes across two distinct teams from one organization. It started with the evaluation 
design that was structured to achieve comprehensive insights into the effectiveness and 
usability of the developed artifact. It followed the FEDS framework (Venable et al., 2016), 
focusing on four three steps: 
1. Explanation of Evaluation Objectives: Clearly defined objectives were articulated to 
assess the artifact's impact on improving the engagement of employees from different 
generations in a digital workplace. Applying the method was explained to all teams 
involved during the demonstration and evaluation of our DSR project. The artifact’s 
application should not impact the employees’ work-related activities. To ensure ethics 
and transparency, all online surveys conducted during the investigation presented the 
following text to clarify the purpose and participants’ unanimity. 
“This study aims to identify the barriers, enablers, and other factors influencing 
employee engagement in a digital workplace, considering the different generations. A 
digital workplace comprehends any place, such as home, office, collaborative space, or 
other, where it is possible to connect to the organization through digital technologies, 
such as laptops and the Internet, to perform your job activities. This research is part of 
a doctoral thesis in Information Science and Technology at the University of Coimbra. 

DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
169 
 
We guarantee to preserve your identity and that of people that the respondent may 
mention when completing the questionnaire, ensuring confidentiality about your 
participation. Upon completing the questionnaire, the respondent declares to have read 
and understood this document. Therefore, the respondent accepts to participate in this 
study. Also, the respondent allows using the data provided voluntarily, strictly for 
academic purposes.” 
2. Selection of Evaluation Strategies: Empirical research methods were systematically 
applied to gather data, ensuring objectivity and reliability in assessing the artifact's 
performance. This allowed for a naturalistic evaluation in a real-world context (Venable 
et al., 2016). The "Human Risk & Effectiveness" strategy was adopted to evaluate the 
artifact's effectiveness in a real -world environment. The interventions or adjustments 
based on evaluation findings to enhance the artifacts were detailed in Section 5.5. 
 
3. Identification of Properties to be Evaluated: The artifact's instruments, the Workplace 
Arrangement Model (C1), Virtuality and Enterprise Integration (I2), Barriers and 
Enablers (I3), Employee Engagement Scale (I4), Actor -network Theory (I5), Gens X, 
Y, and Z Interests, Values, and Needs (I6), Best Practices (I7), and Agile Methodology 
(I8), were identified for evaluation. 
The method achieved its objectives through structured phases and systematic evaluation, 
providing valuable insights into employee engagement and organizational challenges. This was 
evidenced by the positive results in perceived  employee engagement levels during the 
hypothetical application of the proposed solutions, as well as the positive feedback from the 
managers of both teams regarding the importance of the content reported in the final report, 
indicating that the actions under their  purview would be ad opted. The insights from the 
evaluation process offered actionable recommendations for enhancing employee engagement 
in digital workplaces. The overall assessment highlighted the method's robustness in addressing 
complex organizational dynamics, offering practical strategies for organizational improvement 
based on empirical data and systematic evaluation. The proposed timeline, number of iterations, 
and estimated time for each activity were appropriate to achieve the objective of this validation 
stage. During the Evaluate phase of the DSR project, a private survey option was chosen, where 
each participant receives a link to respond to the survey. This approach raised doubts among 
some participants about whether the responses would indeed be anonymous, making it essential 
to emphasize the reason for using this setting in future applications of the method. The four 
teams participating in the Demonstrate and Evaluate phases were already familiar with agile 
practices and ceremonies, which facilitated the execution of the SWOT analysis activity, given 
its similarity to the Agile Retrospective ceremony. In the case of applying the method to teams 
without this prior knowledge, more time may be needed to explain the objectives and how the 
activity should proceed. 
5.5  Challenges and Lessons Learned 
The framework and method presented in Figures 4.5, 4.6, and 4.7 represent the final versions 
of our artifacts. However, during the demonstration and evaluation phases, we identified areas 
for improvement to enhance their applicability and usability. This s ection consolidates the 
lessons learned during the DSR project, providing the rationale for each improvement 
implemented in the framework and method. 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
170 
 
In the evaluation phase, we identified that some steps of our method should be optional to 
streamline its application, especially when time or resources are limited. The steps made 
optional include: 
● Step 1.2: "Align with stakeholders on the strategy to foster engagement." 
● Step 1.7: "Map actors, networks, and conflicts." 
● Step 1.8: "Confirm employees’ perceived barriers and enablers by generation." 
● Step 2.1: "Define the OPP and common procedures to establish team stability." 
These steps were made optional because, for example, an organization might not have a clear 
strategy for step 1.2 and might initially seek to assess the current context and engagement levels. 
Furthermore, steps 1.7, 1.8, and 2.1 require a deeper understanding of employees’ experiences 
and can be time-consuming, depending on the complexity of the environment. 
Additionally, according to data collected in step ‘1.4 Collect information related to individual, 
team, and organizational characteristics, ’ when applying the method with small teams, it is 
crucial to identify if any specific individual classified all factors as negative outliers. This could 
indicate that disengagement stems from specific reasons not applicable to the rest of the team. 
In such cases, an additional meeting with managers (step ‘1.9.1 Interview employees to detail 
information needed’) is recommended. Teams with high levels of trust among coworkers and 
good leadership were more open to discussing top ics and suggesting actions or alternative 
solutions. 
During the demonstration phase, we recognized the need to include step ‘1.6 Identify the 
strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, and threats (SWOT) of our method’ to consolidate the 
findings from step ‘1.4 Collect information related to individual, team, and  organizational 
characteristics.’ This addition facilitates sharing relevant topics with participants in a visually 
accessible manner, which aids in gathering more information during the dynamic. Additionally, 
we updated step ‘1.9 Confirm the SWOT analysis with the team and discuss possible solutions,’ 
by proposing using the SWOT tool. This tool proved to be easily understood, even by those 
unfamiliar with it, and its structure fosters discussions about both positive and negative 
employee perceptions. 
The method effectively provides valuable insights to managers and organizations regarding the 
main barriers and enablers experienced by employees, in addition to various other factors such 
as the use of tools, the indication of the best approach (virtual o r in person) for each activity, 
and the most attractive factors within the organization. Our approach is more comprehensive 
than other methods , considering 75 factors influencing employees’ engagement  in a digital 
work environment. Focusing on the employee is crucial to ensuring an individual perspective, 
considering different needs, values, and behaviors . This  is possible due to  the method’s 
flexibility in supporting different solutions to address these aspects. Additionally, the cyclical 
approach of the method enables continuous improvement, as after overcoming one barrier , 
others may arise. The method is scalable to teams and organizations in the service sector that 
adopt a virtual or hybrid model. The demonstration and evaluation were conducted with teams 
from different business sectors, and no differences were noticed in applying the method in this 
regard. All teams involved in the demonstration and evaluation of the method were already 
familiar with the Agile methodology; however, it may require additional explanation for future 
teams if they are not familiar with it. From the participants' perspective, the iterations were 
conducted without impacting daily activities. Some participants mentioned it felt like a 
retrospective ceremony, but without the mana gers present, they felt more comfortable sharing 
specific points. From the management's point of view, we received positive feedback from the 
managers of both teams regarding the importance of the content reported in the final report, 
indicating that the actions under their purview would be adopted. 

DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
171 
 
5.6  Conclusion 
In this chapter, we presented the demonstration and evaluation phases of our DSR project, 
which aims to develop a framework and method to support organizations in implementing 
engaging digital workplaces tailored for a multigenerational workforce. Utilizin g the FEDS 
framework by Venable et al. (2016), we systematically applied empirical research methods to 
ensure objective and transparent evaluation processes. 
The first demonstration cycle was conducted within an IT team managed by the researcher. The 
cycle revealed a high preference for hybrid work models, emphasizing activities like celebration 
and team building. Critical barriers identified included a lack of  resources and workspace 
conditions, while enablers were clear roles, achievable goals, and appropriate tools. Employee 
engagement was high, though it varied slightly among individuals. The second demonstration 
cycle was conducted with a different IT team managed by other managers in the same company; 
this cycle highlighted the importance of conflict management and competitive wages. Like the 
first team, clear goals and appropriate tools were significant enablers, while the barriers focused 
more on poor tea m relationships and workspace conditions. Engagement levels were 
comparably high, with a noted preference for hybrid work and in -person activities for team 
cohesion. 
The evaluation phase occurred with two teams from a different organization. The 
Merchandising revealed that some members preferred reducing the in -office frequency, 
highlighting work-life balance and flexible arrangements as crucial factors. Critical barri ers 
included inadequate information and management issues, while clear objectives and proper 
tools were vital enablers. Engagement levels were generally high, though certain areas like 
recognition and organizational connection needed improvement. The Suppl y Chain team 
echoed sentiments regarding in -office frequency and emphasized training and workshops as 
crucial. Overload and lack of resources were significant barriers, while clear roles and 
measurable goals were vital enablers. The engagement level between the two teams was close. 
When comparing the two teams of each company, it is possible to identify that, despite working 
in the same organizational context, the teams within the same company have different 
perceptions of barriers in the work environment. This highlights the importa nce of 
understanding group and individual contexts and investing in diverse measures to address other 
needs. It was also possible to identify both positive and negative aspects of having the person 
who applies the framework and method familiar with the team. By knowing the company and 
team, the facilitator can direct the discussion towards known aspects and, by better 
understanding the participants' profiles, have greater freedom to encourage more introverted 
individuals to participate. However, topics such  as management are more challenging to 
address, considering there may be hesitancy in speaking transparently. Participants feel more 
comfortable talking when the facilitator is not an employee of the company. The 
recommendation would be to have an HR person not directly linked to the team or an external 
consultancy apply the framework and method to the teams. Another relevant point when 
comparing the two companies is the difference in the average level of team engagement. The 
company where the framework and  method were applied during the demonstration phase 
already conducts annual actions and measurements of employee engagement levels and showed 
a higher average compared to the second company, which participated in the Evaluate phase 
and does not have measures to assess or improve employee engagement. This demonstrates the 
importance of establishing a continuous engagement improvement process. 
Our findings underscore the importance of tailoring digital workplace strategies to 
accommodate the needs and preferences of an organization's diverse workforce. The hybrid 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
172 
 
work model emerged as a preferred arrangement, necessitating clear communication, robust 
support systems, and effective management practices to maintain high engagement levels. 
Organizations must address barriers like resource limitations and workspace con ditions while 
leveraging enablers like clear roles and adequate tools to foster a productive and engaging 
digital workplace. 
Figure 5.49 represents the steps performed during the phases to Demonstrate and Evaluate our 
DSR project and its primary outcomes. Through these phases, we were able to validate the 
effectiveness of the proposed method by confirming the usability of the proposed instruments, 
confirming the differing perceptions of employees from different generations among the factors 
influencing engagement in the DW, and confirming the positive influence on employee 
engagement with the proposed solutions derived from the method’s application. 
 
Figure 5.49 - DSRM Process Model, emphasizing the Demonstrate and Evaluate phases. 


DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
173 
 
Chapter 6  
DSR Communication and Conclusions 
In DSR, effective communication and dissemination of findings are critical, ensuring the 
research impacts both academic and practical spheres. This chapter delves into the 
communication phase of our DSR project. The findings from this research have been 
disseminated through prestigious conferences and journals, contributing to a broader 
understanding and implementation of effective digital workplaces. 
Section 6.1 outlines the dissemination efforts and details the six scientific papers published as 
part of this research. Each paper addresses different facets of digital workplaces, offering 
insights, models, and guidelines for organizations to enhance emp loyee engagement in a 
multigenerational workforce. These publications not only enrich academic literature but also 
provide practical solutions for businesses navigating the complexities of implementing an 
engaging digital workplace tailored to the needs and values of different generations. 
Section 6.2 summarizes this thesis's contributions to the IS field. It highlights the foundational 
definitions, framework, method, and instruments developed through this research, emphasizing 
their practical applicability and relevance. 
Section 6.3 provides recommendations for practitioners and organizations, drawing on the 
research findings to offer actionable strategies for enhancing employee engagement in digital 
workplaces. These recommendations are grounded in the insights gained fro m the systematic 
literature reviews, empirical studies, and theoretical framework and method developed during 
the research. 
Section 6.4 acknowledges the limitations of the studies conducted and suggests directions for 
future research. Recognizing the dynamic and evolving nature of digital workplaces, this 
section calls for further exploration into demographic, social, and cultu ral aspects and the 
impact of emerging technologies on employee engagement and performance. 
This chapter aims to bridge the gap between theory and practice by comprehensively 
communicating research findings and reflecting on contributions and limitations. It provides a 
roadmap for future research and practical implementation in digital workplaces, emp loyee 
engagement, and generational traits. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
174 
 
6.1  DSR Communication  
The communication phase of this DSR project has disseminated the findings and insights gained 
throughout the study on digital workplaces and employee engagement. This phase involved 
publishing six scientific papers and one under development across various prestigious 
conferences and journals, each contributing to the topic's broader academic and practical 
understanding. 
[HICSS 2022] Designing digital workplaces for employee engagement: Practical 
guidelines from a systematic literature review 
This paper, presented at the 55th Hawaii International Conference on System Sciences (HICSS 
2022), on January 2022 at Maui, Hawaii, USA, pages 6341 -6350, DOI: 
10.24251/HICSS.2022.769. It focuses on the foundational aspects of designing digital 
workplaces to enhance employee engagement. The study consolidate s the findings of an SLR 
on digital workplace and employee engagement. Building on that, we propose a working 
definition for the digital workplace, a model to identify the workplace arrangement as a function 
of virtuality and enterprise integration, and a s et of guidelines across four phases to support 
organizations in implementing an engaging digital workplace. This paper is cited in six papers 
published in AMCIS 2023 and ISD 2023 as the background for the following studies. 
HICSS is a rank-A conference in the IS field. 
[AMCIS 2023] Understanding barriers, enablers, and best practices for creating effective 
multi-generational digital workspaces 
At the Americas Conference on Information Systems (AMCIS 2023), in August 2023, in 
Panama City, Panama. W e presented our findings on the barriers and enablers in digital 
workplaces. Built on one SLR on DW and employee engagement and a second SLR on 
generational traits, this paper contributes to understanding multi -generational digital 
workspaces by consolidat ing a list of known barriers and enablers in digital workplaces and 
identifying best practices to mitigate and promote the latter. Further, we analyze how the 
different generations, X, Y, and Z, perceive these factors. This paper was cited in a book 
published last year: Effective Human Resources Management in the Multigenerational 
Workplace, chapter Diversity, Equity, and Inclusion: Impact on Multi-Generational Workforce 
Engagement and Productivity in the Post -Pandemic Era. Also, it was cited in an article 
published in the Proceedings of the 9th International Conference on Socio -Technical 
Perspectives in IS, Portsmouth, UK. 
AMICS is a rank A conference in the IS field. 
[ISD 2023] Barriers in Digital Workplaces During Covid -19 Lockdowns: A 
Phenomenological Longitudinal Study Using Actor-Network Theory  
The paper was presented at the 31st International Conference on Information Systems 
Development (ISD, 2023), on October 2023 in Lisbon, Portugal, DOI: 10.62036/ISD.2023.8. 
It delved into the barriers encountered in digital workplaces during the COVID -19 pandemic. 
We present a one -year longitudinal phenomenological study that explores how individuals 
perceived and overcame barriers to remote work during COVID -19 lockdowns. Our study 
builds upon a previous systematic literature review. It utilizes ANT to anal yze data collected 
from a survey and two rounds of interviews spaced one year apart. Through this approach, we 
gain insights into the networks of human and non-human actors that emerged and the challenges 
remote employees face. We compiled a comprehensive list of barriers to anticipate in these 

DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
175 
 
circumstances and identified those unique to extreme situations. This paper was cited recently 
in the International Journal of Business & Management Studies.  
ISD is a rank-A conference in the IS field. 
[PAJAIS 2024] Designing Digital Workplaces: A Four -Phase Iterative Approach with 
Guidelines Concerning Virtuality and Enterprise Integration 
Published in the March 2024 Issue of the Pacific Asia Journal of the Association for Information 
Systems (PAJAIS), Vol. 16, Iss. 1, Art. 4, DOI: 10.17705/1pais.16104. This paper introduces a 
four-phase iterative approach for crafting a digital workplace. T his includes a comprehensive 
set of actionable guidelines for each phase previously dispersed within existing literature. 
Additionally, we introduce a rubric for assessing Enterprise Integration, employed in 
conjunction with the Virtuality Level, to charac terize various workplace configurations. 
Finally, we have also listed a set of additional research gaps and promising avenues for 
researchers interested in this field. 
PAJAIS is a Q2 journal with an impact factor of 2.4. 
[AMCIS 2024] When Age Matters. The Attractiveness of Digital Workplaces Across 
Generations 
This paper, another significant contribution to AMCIS 2024, presents the findings from a 
confirmatory study validating the conceptual model developed in our research. Using 
quantitative data analysis from an online survey, we confirmed the relevance and im pact of 
various factors on employee engagement across different generations. 
AMICS is a rank A conference in the IS field. 
[Springer 2024] Exploring Barriers and Engagement in Digital Workplaces: An Analysis 
Spanning the Periods Before, During, and After COVID-19 
This accepted, upcoming publication in Springer Nature Switzerland, DOI: 10.1007/978-3-031-
57189-3_7, compares the barriers and engagement levels in digital workplaces before, during, 
and after the COVID -19 pandemic. This paper extends the one presented at the ISD 2023 
conference. It provides a longitudinal perspective on how the pandemic has reshaped digital 
work environments and employee engagement. 
Springer Innovations in Communication and Computing is a Q4 journal with an impact score 
of 0.78. 
[Under Development] A Framework and Method to Implement Engaging Digital 
Workplaces Tailored for a Multigenerational Workforce 
The final paper, currently under refinement for publication, details the comprehensive 
framework and method developed through this research project. This framework is designed to 
support organizations in assessing, designing, and optimizing their digital workplace strategies 
to enhance employee engagement effectively, considering the diverse needs of a 
multigenerational workforce. The method comprises detailed procedures and tasks supported 
by eight instruments. 
These publications have been crucial in communicating the research findings to the academic 
community and practitioners. By sharing our insights through these diverse channels, we aim 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
176 
 
to contribute significantly to the knowledge of digital workplaces and provide practical 
guidelines for organizations seeking to improve employee engagement in a diverse workforce. 
6.2  Summary of the Contributions 
This thesis offers the following contributions to the IS field of designing engaging digital 
workplaces tailored for a multi-generational workforce: 
● A working definition of a digital workplace, setting a foundational understanding for 
subsequent research and practice.  
● A Framework designed to support organizations in implementing engaging digital 
workplaces. This framework is specifically crafted to address the diverse needs of a 
multi-generational workforce. 
● A Method to support the application of the proposed framework, with detailed 
procedures that organizations can follow. This method includes specific instruments to 
support the execution of tasks within the method, making it practical and actionable for 
specific real-world implementation. Specifically: 
o Workplace Arrangement Model (I1)  defines workplace arrangements as a 
function of virtuality and enterprise integration. It addresses a gap identified in 
the literature, emphasizing the importance of an integrated approach in 
leveraging the full potential of digital workplaces. As Attaran et al. (2019) noted, 
companies that fail to adopt an integrated approach miss out on significant 
opportunities that digital workplaces can offer. 
o Virtuality and Enterprise Integration Levels (I2):  outlines virtuality across 
three dimensions: geographic or spatial distance, which pertains to the physical 
separation of employees; operational distance, which considers the differences 
in processes and workflows; and social distance, which looks at the relational 
and cultural gaps between team members. Enterprise integration involves the 
seamless connection and integration of various systems, applications, and 
processes to facilitate smooth communication, collaboration, and data sharing. 
o Barriers and Enablers (I3): identifies factors that can positively or negatively 
influence six key predecessors of a successful digital workplace: trust, 
leadership, knowledge sharing, communication, autonomy, and collaboration. 
These factors are crucial as they directly impact employee engagement. 
o Employee Engagement Scale (I4): measures the employee engagement levels 
within the digital workplace, providing insights into the effectiveness of 
implemented strategies. We adapted EES with four additional Gallup Q12 
questions to cover the individual needs.  
o Actor-Network Theory (I5): enables visualization of employees' networks and 
conflicts within the digital workplace. ANT helps map out diverse actors 
involved and identify potential environmental conflicts or issues, ensuring a 
comprehensive understanding of system dynamics. 
o Values and Workplace Characteristics by Generation (I6): consolidates the 
interests and values of Generations X, Y, and Z, highlighting the most relevant 
barriers and enablers for each. This consolidation helps tailor digital workplace 
strategies to better suit the distinct preferences of each generation. 

DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
177 
 
o Best Practices (I7):  proposed best practices that aim to mitigate the adverse 
effects of identified barriers while positively influencing the enablers. These 
practices enhance overall employee engagement and productivity in a digital 
environment. 
o Agile Methodology (I8):  proposes applying the existing Agile practices and 
ceremonies to support collaboration, communication, and adaptability in digital 
workplaces. 
Together, these contributions answer our research question by supporting organizations from 
the business services sectors in implementing engaging digital workplaces tailored for a 
multigenerational workforce. Organizations can use our contributions to understand the most 
relevant factors influencing employee engagement in digital workplaces. Then, they can 
identify and implement actions to mitigate barriers  and promote enablers, thus creating more 
engaging and effective digital workplaces tailored to the needs of a multigenerational 
workforce. 
6.3  Recommendations for Practitioners and Organizations 
Technological advancements have enabled organizations worldwide to invest in digital 
transformation, a trend accelerated by the COVID -19 pandemic. Concurrently, the increasing 
presence of Generation Y (Millennials) and Z in the global workforce demands a d eeper 
understanding of their perceptions in digital work environments. However, there is a high 
percentage of disengaged employees, with only 23% of the global workforce feeling engaged 
in 2022, while 59% were not actively engaged, also known as "quiet qui tting." This 
phenomenon, where employees do just enough without engaging or exerting extra effort, can 
lead to lower productivity, low morale, and eventually increased employee turnover. By 
proactively addressing employee engagement, companies can minimize  or prevent quiet 
quitting. 
This doctoral thesis encompassed six studies, namely one SLR on Digital Workplace and 
Employee Engagement, another SLR on Generational Traits, one Longitudinal 
Phenomenological Study, one Confirmatory Study, two demonstration Case Studies , and one 
Case Study to evaluate the artifacts in depth in a real business environment. Each contributed 
to our investigation's outcomes, as presented in Table 6.1.

The contributions presented in the Table above are valuable to digital workplace research. Next, 
we provide practical recommendations for organizations aiming to create compelling digital 
work environments that foster employee engagement and cater to the n eeds of a diverse 
workforce.  
1. Continuous Engagement Improvement Process 
1.1. Establish Continuous Measurement: Regularly assess employee engagement levels and 
barriers to maintain continuous improvement. 
1.2. Adapt to Organizational Contexts: Flexible implementation, tailoring the framework and 
method to the specific organizational context, making optional steps to streamline application. 
2. Address Common Barriers in the Digital Workplace 
2.1. Mitigate Isolation and Anxiety: Implement organizational incentives for physical activities 
and promote social events between co-workers to reduce isolation and anxiety. 

DSR Communication and Conclusions 
 
 
179 
 
2.2. Improve Work Routine and Family Reconciliation: When possible, allow flexible work 
hours to help employees balance work tasks with family responsibilities, especially for those 
caring for children. 
2.3. Supportive Tools and Policies: Offer tools and policies that facilitate a better work -life 
balance, such as access to childcare support and home-schooling resources. 
 
3. Enhance Communication and Connectivity 
3.1. Provide Flexible Communication Methods: Utilize synchronous and asynchronous 
communication methods to improve information flow between teams and departments. 
3.2. Adequate Tools: Ensure employees have effective communication and knowledge-sharing 
tools, including robust internet connectivity and virtual collaboration platforms. 
3.3. Face-to-face interactions: Incorporate in -person interactions, when possible, to promote 
trust among co-workers. 
 
4. Recognize Diverse Needs 
4.1. Engage Generation Z: Align Generation Z employees' roles and responsibilities with 
organizational goals and solicit their input on better tool utilization to improve engagement. 
4.2. Custom Training Programs: Implement flexible training options, including interactive 
sessions and informal learning opportunities, to cater to different learning preferences across 
generations. 
4.3. Tailored Engagement Strategies: Develop engagement strategies that consider  each 
generation's unique preferences and needs, from job flexibility to career progression 
opportunities. 
4.4. Flexible Recognition System: Implement tangible (bonuses, days off) and intangible 
(public praise, merit certificates) rewards for additional work to engage employees and reduce 
feelings of overload. 
Organizations can have a more engaging digital workplace by following these 
recommendations when applying our proposed framework and method. 
6.4  Limitations and Directions for Future Research  
We acknowledge the limitations associated with the studies conducted in this thesis. Firstly, we 
did not analyze how demographic, social, and cultural aspects influence different generations' 
values and needs in a digital workplace. Also, we did not consid er other individual 
characteristics, such as gender, marital status, and education level. This was a conscious 
decision when defining the research scope , but it limited our understanding of these factors. 
The research primarily focused on broad generational differences without delving into specific 
cohort characteristics, particularly Generation Z, which was underrepresented in the literature. 
The samples in all studies conducted in this research included primarily Brazilian and 
Portuguese employees, without considering other geographical and governmental contexts, 
which might affect the relevance and applicability of the findings to other regions. Additionally, 
the respondents from the online survey of the confirmatory study were predominantly from the 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
180 
 
financial or technical sectors, with 70% male and 91% holding bachelor's or master's degrees. 
This distribution may have influenced the results and reduced the generalizability of the 
findings. 
Furthermore, due to time limitations and organizational availability, we did not implement all 
phases of the proposed framework and method in a real organizational environment. 
Implementing changes within the organization requires additional time and resources, and it 
may take longer for these improvements to yield results in employee engagement. The same 
applies to the best practices presented in Instrument 7; we did not validate all items proposed 
in a real organizational environment and their influence on employee engagement. In addition, 
the findings from the demonstration and evaluation phases are limited in terms of the 
generalizability of results since they are contextual and, hence, cannot be directly extrapolated 
to another population. However, they  were valuable in enhancing the framework and method 
applicability and effectiveness, providing rich insights into the digital work environment. 
Future research should explore how demographic, social, and cultural aspects influence 
different generations’ values and needs in a digital workplace. This includes conducting specific 
studies on Generation Z and examining how gender and seniority levels i mpact engagement 
dynamics. There is ample space for further research on the technical aspects of IT in multi -
generational digital workspaces. Investigating how new and emerging technologies, such as 
Immersive Virtual Environments ( IVE), AI tools, and gamif ication platforms, can influence 
engagement and performance is particularly important. 
From a methodological perspective, future studies could validate and improve the proposed 
instruments for identifying and measuring workplace arrangements as a function of virtuality 
and enterprise integration. Longitudinal studies are recommended to capture the intricate nature 
of team dynamics over time. Additionally, multidisciplinary research should be expanded to 
investigate digital workplaces in various contexts, such as energy, healthcare, and creative 
sectors, to provide a broader understanding of t he digital workplace's impact across different 
industries. 
Further studies should focus on cultural and social interactions in digital environments, 
including the relationship between communication satisfaction and national cultures and the 
social interactions between employees. Research should also consider the character istics of a 
diverse workforce and how these influence employees’ perceptions in a digital workplace. This 
includes exploring cross-generational mentorship, development opportunities, and fostering an 
inclusive culture. Finally, validating the proposed fram ework and method in various 
organizational environments is essential to improve their practical applicability and 
effectiveness, thus bridging the gap between theory and practice. This should improve the 
framework and method with the necessary details to facilitate its application by consulting firms 
and companies.

Appendices 
  

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
 
195 
 
Appendix A - Papers Selected in the 
SLR on DW and Employee Engagement 
Table A.1 comprises 61 papers selected for the SLR on digital workplace and employee 
engagement. We classified the papers based on whether it is also a literature review (Yes or 
No), whether workplace arrangement was discussed (e.g., physical workplace, remote work,  
virtual workplace, or digital workplace), whether generational differences were addressed (Yes 
or No), and primary focus (e.g., performance, effectiveness, engagement, satisfaction, 
motivation, or well-being). Column 2 of the table provides a concise synopsis of each article.

We identified studies from seven disciplines. They cover different organizational sectors, such 
as industry and services, and represent multiple countries. About 65% of the studies considered 
two or three generations (X, Y, or Z). Still, only four (around 20%) discussed Generation Z. 
This smaller number was expected since Generation Z only recently entered the workforce. 
 
 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace 
 
 
201 
 
Appendix C - Questionnaires Used in 
the Phenomenological Study 
Table C.1 presents the questions that formed the online survey used to collect data for the 
phenomenological study during the COVID-19 pandemic. As suggested by ANT, we aimed to 
identify the human and digital actors in the digital workplaces and the main b arriers perceived 
by the respondents. The survey was shared with researchers’ social media contacts in Portugal 
and Brazil and conducted in Portuguese, the participant's native language. 
Table C.1 - Questions used in the phenomenological study. 
ID English Question Portuguese Question 
1 What is your level of satisfaction with 
your current work-from-home 
conditions?  
Qual é o seu nível de satisfação com suas 
condições atuais de trabalho em casa?  
2 Do you feel more optimistic or 
pessimistic about working from home 
than last month?  
Em comparação com o mês passado, você se 
sente mais otimista ou pessimista sobre 
trabalhar de casa?  
3 What are your THREE biggest 
challenges with working from home?  
Quais são os seus TRÊS maiores desafios com 
relação a trabalhar de casa?  
4 Do you need any measures to be taken 
to perform your work smoothly while 
working from home?  
Você precisa que alguma providência seja 
tomada para que você possa realizar seu 
trabalho sem dificuldade enquanto trabalha de 
casa?  
5 Which of the following options best 
describes the central sector in which 
you work?  
Qual das seguintes opções melhor descreve o 
setor principal em que trabalha?  
6 Which of the following options best 
describes your employment contract 
model?  
Qual das seguintes opções melhor descreve o 
seu modelo de contrato de trabalho?  
7 Which of the following options best 
describes the schedule of your work 
hours?  
Qual das seguintes opções melhor descreve o 
horário de sua jornada de trabalho?  
8 What technologies, devices, or tools do 
you use during the remote work?  
Durante o período de trabalho remoto, quais 
tecnologias, dispositivos ou ferramentas utiliza?  
9 How does the company track and 
monitor the activities performed?  
Como a empresa acompanha e monitora as 
atividades realizadas? 
10 What is your age group? Qual a sua faixa etária? 
 
Based on the results, we developed the questions for the first round of interviews (presented in 
Table C.2) to identify the use of technological tools, barriers, and feelings experienced at the 
beginning of the pandemic. Below is a translation of the questions used.

Appendix D - Semi-Structured 
Interview Proposed for Initial 
Alignment 
Table D.1 presents the proposed semi -structured interview to be applied in Phase 1. 
Awareness steps 1.1, ‘Align project scope and organizational context with the stakeholders’ 
and 1.2, ‘Align with the stakeholders the strategy to foster engagement’ of our method. The 
objective is to understand better the organizational context  and the information needed to 
identify if any adjustment to the online survey is necessary before applying step 1.4, ‘Collect 
information related to individual, team, and organization al characteristics.’ The questionnaire 
should be modified or complemented according to the organizational context and project needs.  
Table D.1 - Semi-structured questionnaire for the initial interviews (Phase 1: Awareness). 
ID English Question Portuguese Question 
1 What is the size of the involved team(s)? Qual o tamanho das equipas envolvidas? 
2 What is the composition of the involved 
team(s) (employees, consultants, interns)? 
Qual a composição da equipa (funcionários, 
consultores, estagiários)? 
3 What is the geographical location of the 
employees (is it globally distributed, or do 
all team members live in the same 
country)? 
Qual a localização geográfica da equipa (está 
distribuída globalmente ou todos moram no 
mesmo país)? 
4 What is the employees' dependency level 
on carrying out their activities? 
Em geral, qual o nível de dependência dos 
colaboradores para realizar suas atividades? 
5 Is there any conflict already identified or 
any topic of greater relevance to the 
company during the project? 
Existe algum conflito já mapeado ou algum 
tópico de maior relevância para a empresa 
durante o projeto? 
6 Has any recent event that could affect team 
engagement, such as bonus payments, 
structural changes, or layoffs? 
Ocorreu recentemente algum evento que possa 
afetar o nível de envolvimento da equipa, como 
pagamento de bônus, mudança de estrutura ou 
demissões? 
7 Does the company already have any 
initiatives to measure employee 
engagement? 
A empresa já possui alguma iniciativa para 
mensurar o nível de envolvimento dos 
colaboradores? 
8 What is the company's strategy for 
employee engagement: to develop, 
support, or nurture? 
Qual a estratégia da empresa para o 
envolvimento dos colaboradores: desenvolver, 
suportar ou nutrir? 
9 Is there any policy or regulation for digital 
work, such as the requirement to be in the 
office on certain days? 
Existe alguma norma ou política para o 
trabalho digital, como a obrigatoriedade de 
estar no escritório algum dia? 
10 Does the company provide any support for 
employees to work remotely? 
A empresa oferece algum suporte para os 
colaboradores trabalharem remotamente? 

Design Engaging Digital Workplace

Appendix E - Online Survey Used in the 
Evaluation Phase 
The Evaluate phase of our DSR project applied only to Phase 1. Awareness of our framework 
and method. To evaluate the effectiveness of our artifacts, we elaborated an online survey 
describing a hypothetical scenario where the organization implements the pr oposed action to 
measure the impact on the level of employee engagement. Table E.1 presents the scenario and 
questions presented to the participants. The EES presented in question ID 2 had the wording 
adapted to show the alternative, considering the hypothetical scenario.  
Table E.1 - Questions used for hypothetical evaluation of the artifacts in organizations. 
ID English Question Portuguese Question 
1 In a hypothetical scenario where the 
organization implements a set of 
improvements aimed at increasing team 
autonomy, improving recognition for work 
done, reducing employee overload, and 
optimizing knowledge transfer and 
communication, consider the actions listed 
in the left box and drag each action to the 
correct box in order of priority, considering 
which actions you believe would have the 
most positive influence on your level of 
engagement. 
● Formation of backups and 
decentralization of knowledge 
● Formal recognition for additional work 
done, including tangible rewards 
(bonuses, days off) 
● Formal recognition for additional work 
done, including intangible rewards 
(public praise, merit certificates) 
● Increase the frequency of pre-scheduled 
one-on-one meetings with managers for 
feedback and alignment 
● Implement quarterly 360° feedback 
sessions 
● Update the workspace with more 
monitors and more efficient distribution 
of space among teams 
● Establish shared goals among teams 
and communicate priorities and 
resource allocation for projects 
● Implement a project management tool 
that provides an overview of all 
ongoing projects, highlighting resource 
needs and dependencies between teams 
Num cenário hipotético, onde a organização 
implementa um conjunto de melhorias 
visando aumentar a autonomia das equipas, 
melhorar o reconhecimento pelo trabalho 
realizado, reduzir a sobrecarga dos 
colaboradores e otimizar a passagem de 
conhecimento e comunicação, considere as 
ações listadas no quadro esquerdo e carregue 
cada uma das ações listadas e arraste-as para 
a caixa da direita por ordem de prioridade, 
considerando quais ações você acredita que 
teriam maior influência positiva no seu nível 
de envolvimento. 
● Formação de Backups e 
Descentralização do Conhecimento. 
Reconhecimento formal para o trabalho 
adicional realizado, incluindo 
recompensas tangíveis (bônus, dias de 
folga). 
● Reconhecimento formal para o trabalho 
adicional realizado, incluindo 
recompensas intangíveis (elogios 
públicos, certificados de mérito). 
● Aumentar a frequência de reuniões 
individuais pré-agendadas com gestores 
para feedback e alinhamento. 
● Implementar sessões de feedback 360° 
trimestrais. 
● Atualizar o espaço de trabalho com mais 
monitores e uma distribuição mais 
eficiente dos espaços entre as equipas. 
● Estabelecer metas compartilhadas entre 
as equipas e comunicar claramente as 
prioridades e alocação de recursos para 
projetos.
